Conventions. Rings are commutative with \(1\). Results of the text are cited by their numbers in Liu (“Prop. 3.9”, “Cor. 3.32”, …), and results of Chapters 1 and 2 as “Prop. 2.3.12” etc. Exercise labels are those of the book, numbered within each section of the chapter: for instance Exercise 2.11 is the eleventh exercise at the end of Section 3.2. Each exercise is restated before its solution; sub-items that the book displays as nested lists are written as running prose.

Honest notes. Several printed statements need a correction or an interpretation, and each solution says what is proved: Ex. 1.9(b) (the intersection condition is read as nonempty), Ex. 1.10(e) (false for finite \(k\); proved with the needed hypothesis), Ex. 2.21 (needs finite presentation), Ex. 3.5 (needs a nonempty scheme), Ex. 3.13(b) (false for a non-separated base, with the doubled-origin counterexample; the corrected version is proved), and Ex. 3.25 (false as printed, with a counterexample over \(\mathbf Z\)). Some solutions cite standard commutative-algebra facts (for instance the dimension formula for polynomial rings) which are named where used.

3.1 The technique of base change

Throughout, results of the text are cited by their numbers: Prop. 1.2, Prop. 1.4, Prop. 1.16, Prop. 1.23 are those of Section 3.1; references to earlier chapters carry the chapter, for instance Lemma 2.1.7 (spectra and ring homomorphisms), Prop. 2.1.9 (Noether normalization), Cor. 2.1.12 (residue fields of finitely generated algebras), Prop. 2.3.25 (morphisms to an affine scheme), Lemma 2.3.40 (Proj and graded homomorphisms), Prop. 2.4.12 (generic points). Several exercises use Exercise 1.7; its proof is given in full below.

Exercise 1.1

Problem

Let \(S,X,Y\) be sets, \(\varphi:X\to S\), \(\psi:Y\to S\) maps, \(X\times_S Y=\{(x,y)\in X\times Y\mid \varphi(x)=\psi(y)\}\), and let \(p:X\times_S Y\to X\), \(q:X\times_S Y\to Y\) be the restrictions of the projections. Show that for any set \(Z\) and any pair of maps \(f:Z\to X\), \(g:Z\to Y\) with \(\varphi\circ f=\psi\circ g\) there is a unique map \((f,g):Z\to X\times_S Y\) with \(p\circ(f,g)=f\) and \(q\circ(f,g)=g\).

Solution

Existence. Put \((f,g)(z)=(f(z),g(z))\). Since \(\varphi(f(z))=\psi(g(z))\), this pair lies in \(X\times_S Y\), and clearly \(p\circ(f,g)=f\), \(q\circ(f,g)=g\).

Uniqueness. Let \(h:Z\to X\times_S Y\) satisfy \(p\circ h=f\) and \(q\circ h=g\). For \(z\in Z\) write \(h(z)=(x,y)\). Then \(x=p(h(z))=f(z)\) and \(y=q(h(z))=g(z)\), so \(h(z)=(f(z),g(z))\). Hence \(h=(f,g)\). ∎

Exercise 1.2

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of schemes. For a scheme \(T\) let \(f(T):X(T)\to Y(T)\) be the map \(g\mapsto f\circ g\) (here \(X(T)=\operatorname{Mor}(T,X)\)). Show that \(f(T)\) is bijective for every \(T\) if and only if \(f\) is an isomorphism.

Solution

If \(f\) is an isomorphism with inverse \(h\), then \(g\mapsto h\circ g\) is a two-sided inverse of \(f(T)\), because \(h\circ f=\mathrm{Id}_X\) and \(f\circ h=\mathrm{Id}_Y\) (so \(h\circ(f\circ g)=g\) and \(f\circ(h\circ g')=g'\)).

Conversely, assume \(f(T)\) is bijective for all \(T\). Take \(T=Y\). Surjectivity of \(f(Y)\) applied to \(\mathrm{Id}_Y\in Y(Y)\) gives \(h:Y\to X\) with \(f\circ h=\mathrm{Id}_Y\). Now take \(T=X\). Both \(h\circ f\) and \(\mathrm{Id}_X\) belong to \(X(X)\) and

\[ f(X)(h\circ f)=f\circ h\circ f=f=f\circ\mathrm{Id}_X=f(X)(\mathrm{Id}_X). \]

By injectivity of \(f(X)\), \(h\circ f=\mathrm{Id}_X\). So \(h\) is a two-sided inverse of \(f\). ∎

Exercise 1.3

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a dominant morphism of irreducible schemes. Show that the generic fiber of \(f\) is dense in \(X\).

Solution

Let \(\xi\) be the generic point of \(Y\) and \(\eta\) that of \(X\) (Prop. 2.4.12(a): an irreducible scheme has a unique generic point). By Prop. 1.16 the first projection \(X_\xi\to X\) is a homeomorphism onto \(f^{-1}(\xi)\), so the statement means that \(f^{-1}(\xi)\) is dense in \(X\).

By continuity, \(f(X)=f(\overline{\{\eta\}})\subseteq\overline{\{f(\eta)\}}\). Since \(f\) is dominant, \(\overline{f(X)}=Y\), hence \(\overline{\{f(\eta)\}}=Y\). So \(f(\eta)\) is a generic point of \(Y\), and by uniqueness \(f(\eta)=\xi\). Thus \(\eta\in f^{-1}(\xi)\). As \(\eta\) is dense in \(X\), so is \(f^{-1}(\xi)\). ∎

Exercise 1.4

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of affine schemes. Show that for any affine open subscheme \(V\) of \(Y\), \(f^{-1}(V)\) is affine.

Solution

Write \(Y=\Spec A\), \(X=\Spec B\). The inclusion \(j:V\to Y\) is an open immersion and \(V\) is an affine scheme, say \(V=\Spec C\). Consider \(X\times_Y V\), where \(V\) is a \(Y\)-scheme via \(j\). By Prop. 1.2, \(X\times_Y V=\Spec(B\otimes_A C)\) is affine.

On the other hand, Prop. 1.4(d), applied to the \(Y\)-schemes \(X\to Y\) and \(\mathrm{Id}_Y:Y\to Y\) and to the open subschemes \(X\subseteq X\) and \(V\subseteq Y\), shows that \(X\times_Y V\) is isomorphic to \(p^{-1}(X)\cap q^{-1}(V)\subseteq X\times_Y Y\), where \(p,q\) are the projections. Under the canonical isomorphism \(X\times_Y Y\simeq X\) of Prop. 1.4(a), \(p\) becomes \(\mathrm{Id}_X\) and \(q\) becomes \(f\). Hence \(X\times_Y V\simeq f^{-1}(V)\) as schemes (this is the argument of Prop. 1.23 for open immersions). Therefore \(f^{-1}(V)\simeq\Spec(B\otimes_A C)\) is affine. ∎

Exercise 1.5

Problem

Let \(X,Y\) be algebraic varieties over a field \(k\). Show that \(\dim(X\times_k Y)=\dim X+\dim Y\) (use Prop. 2.1.9).

Solution

We only use that \(X,Y\) are nonempty schemes of finite type over \(k\) (separatedness is not needed). The proof has three steps. For a ring \(R\) we use the standard facts: if \(R_0\to R\) is injective and finite then \(\dim R=\dim R_0\) (lying over, going-up, incomparability), and \(\dim k[T_1,\dots,T_m]=m\).

Step 1: dimension of an open cover. Let \(X\) be a topological space covered by opens \(U_i\). Then \(\dim X=\sup_i\dim U_i\). Indeed \(\ge\) is clear (a chain of irreducible closed subsets of \(U_i\) gives one in \(X\) by taking closures, which stay distinct since \(Z=\overline{Z}\cap U_i\) for \(Z\) closed in \(U_i\) and the closure of an irreducible set is irreducible). For \(\le\), take a chain \(Z_0\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq Z_n\) of irreducible closed subsets of \(X\), and choose \(i\) with \(U_i\cap Z_0\neq\emptyset\). Each \(Z_j\cap U_i\) is a nonempty open subset of the irreducible space \(Z_j\), hence irreducible and dense in \(Z_j\); so \(Z_j=\overline{Z_j\cap U_i}\) and the \(Z_j\cap U_i\) form a strictly increasing chain of irreducible closed subsets of \(U_i\).

Step 2: the affine case. Let \(A,B\) be nonzero finitely generated \(k\)-algebras. We claim \(\dim(A\otimes_kB)=\dim A+\dim B\).

First note: if \(A',B'\) are nonzero finitely generated \(k\)-algebras, \(a=\dim A'\), \(b=\dim B'\), then \(\dim(A'\otimes_kB')=a+b\). Indeed, by Prop. 2.1.9 there are finite injective maps \(A_0=k[x_1,\dots,x_{a'}]\to A'\) and \(B_0=k[y_1,\dots,y_{b'}]\to B'\), and by invariance of dimension under finite injective maps \(a'=a\), \(b'=b\). The induced map \(A_0\otimes_kB_0\to A'\otimes_kB'\) is injective (tensor products over a field of injective linear maps are injective: factor it as \(A_0\otimes B_0\to A'\otimes B_0\to A'\otimes B'\) and use that vector spaces are flat) and finite (if \(e_i\) generate \(A'\) over \(A_0\) and \(f_j\) generate \(B'\) over \(B_0\), then the \(e_i\otimes f_j\) generate \(A'\otimes B'\) over \(A_0\otimes B_0\)). Since \(A_0\otimes_kB_0=k[x_1,\dots,x_a,y_1,\dots,y_b]\), we get \(\dim(A'\otimes B')=a+b\).

Lower bound. Choose a minimal prime \(\fp\) of \(A\) with \(\dim A/\fp=\dim A\) (the first member of a chain of maximal length), and likewise \(\fq\subset B\). The surjection \(A\otimes B\to A/\fp\otimes B/\fq\) shows \(\dim(A\otimes B)\ge\dim(A/\fp\otimes B/\fq)=\dim A+\dim B\) (the quotient is nonzero, being a tensor product of two nonzero vector spaces).

Upper bound. Let \(P_0\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq P_n\) be a chain of primes of \(A\otimes B\). Let \(\fp\subset A\) and \(\fq\subset B\) be the preimages of \(P_0\) under \(a\mapsto a\otimes1\), \(b\mapsto1\otimes b\). The map \(A\otimes B\to(A\otimes B)/P_0\) kills \(a\otimes b=(a\otimes1)(1\otimes b)\) whenever \(a\in\fp\) or \(b\in\fq\), so it factors through a surjection \(A/\fp\otimes B/\fq\to(A\otimes B)/P_0\). The chain \(P_i/P_0\) pulls back to a chain of length \(n\) of primes of \(A/\fp\otimes B/\fq\), so \(n\le\dim(A/\fp\otimes B/\fq)=\dim A/\fp+\dim B/\fq\le\dim A+\dim B\).

Step 3: general case. Cover \(X\) and \(Y\) by affine opens \(U_i=\Spec A_i\), \(V_j=\Spec B_j\); the \(A_i,B_j\) are finitely generated \(k\)-algebras. By Prop. 1.4(d) and Prop. 1.2, the open subsets \(p^{-1}(U_i)\cap q^{-1}(V_j)\) of \(X\times_kY\) are isomorphic to \(U_i\times_kV_j=\Spec(A_i\otimes_kB_j)\) and cover \(X\times_kY\). By Step 1 (used for \(X\times_k Y\), \(X\) and \(Y\)) and Step 2,

\[ \dim(X\times_kY)=\sup_{i,j}\dim(A_i\otimes B_j)=\sup_{i,j}(\dim A_i+\dim B_j)=\sup_i\dim A_i+\sup_j\dim B_j=\dim X+\dim Y. \]

(All these numbers are finite by Prop. 2.1.9.) ∎

Exercise 1.6

Problem

Let \(\pi:T\to S\) be a morphism of schemes. Suppose that \(\pi\) is an open or closed immersion, or that \(S=\Spec A\) and \(\pi\) is induced by a localization \(A\to F^{-1}A\) for a multiplicative set \(F\subset A\).

  1. (a)

    Let \(f,g:Z\to T\) be two morphisms of schemes such that \(\pi\circ f=\pi\circ g\). Show that \(f=g\).

  2. (b)

    Let \(X,Y\) be \(T\)-schemes. Show that the canonical morphism \(X\times_TY\to X\times_SY\) is an isomorphism.

  3. (c)

    Show that (b) can be false if the hypothesis on \(T\to S\) is not satisfied.

Solution

(a) We use the following fact. Two morphisms of schemes \(f,g:Z\to T\) coincide as soon as they have the same underlying continuous map and the same stalk maps \(f^\#_z=g^\#_z:\mathcal O_{T,f(z)}\to\mathcal O_{Z,z}\) for all \(z\in Z\). Indeed, for an open \(W\subseteq T\) and \(s\in\mathcal O_T(W)\), the sections \(f^\#(s)\) and \(g^\#(s)\) of \(\mathcal O_Z(f^{-1}W)\) have germs \(f^\#_z(s_{f(z)})=g^\#_z(s_{f(z)})\) at every point \(z\), hence are equal.

Next, in each of the three cases the map \(\pi\) is injective on points, and every stalk map \(\pi^\#_t:\mathcal O_{S,\pi(t)}\to\mathcal O_{T,t}\) is surjective. For an open immersion, \(\pi\) is a homeomorphism onto an open set and \(\pi^\#_t\) is an isomorphism. For a closed immersion, \(\pi\) is a homeomorphism onto a closed set, \(\mathcal O_S\to\pi_*\mathcal O_T\) is a surjective map of sheaves, and \((\pi_*\mathcal O_T)_{\pi(t)}=\mathcal O_{T,t}\), so \(\pi^\#_t\) is surjective. For a localization, \(\pi\) is injective by Lemma 2.1.7(c), and for a prime \(\fp\) of \(A\) with \(\fp\cap F=\emptyset\) the stalk map is \(A_\fp\to(F^{-1}A)_{F^{-1}\fp}=A_\fp\), an isomorphism.

Now let \(\pi\circ f=\pi\circ g\). As maps of sets, \(\pi(f(z))=\pi(g(z))\) gives \(f(z)=g(z)\) by injectivity. For the stalk maps, \((\pi\circ f)^\#_z=f^\#_z\circ\pi^\#_{f(z)}\) and likewise for \(g\), so \(f^\#_z\circ\pi^\#_{f(z)}=g^\#_z\circ\pi^\#_{f(z)}\), and surjectivity of \(\pi^\#_{f(z)}\) gives \(f^\#_z=g^\#_z\). By the fact above, \(f=g\).

(b) Let \(a:X\to T\), \(b:Y\to T\) be the structure morphisms, so \(X,Y\) are \(S\)-schemes via \(\pi\circ a\), \(\pi\circ b\). Let \(W=X\times_TY\) with projections \(p_W,q_W\), and \(Z=X\times_SY\) with projections \(p,q\). The canonical morphism \(\varphi:W\to Z\) is \((p_W,q_W)\) (it exists since \(\pi a p_W=\pi b q_W\)). Since \(\pi\circ(a\circ p)=\pi\circ(b\circ q)\) (definition of \(Z\)), (a) gives \(a\circ p=b\circ q\). Hence \(p,q\) are morphisms of \(T\)-schemes if \(Z\) is given the structure \(c=a\circ p:Z\to T\), and the universal property of \(W\) gives \(\psi=(p,q):Z\to W\) with \(p_W\psi=p\), \(q_W\psi=q\).

Then \(p\circ(\varphi\psi)=p_W\psi=p\) and \(q\circ(\varphi\psi)=q\); the uniqueness in the universal property of \(Z\) gives \(\varphi\psi=\mathrm{Id}_Z\). Also \(p_W\circ(\psi\varphi)=p\varphi=p_W\) and \(q_W\circ(\psi\varphi)=q_W\). Moreover \(\psi\varphi\) is a \(T\)-morphism: the structure morphism of \(W\) is \(a\circ p_W\) and \(a\circ p_W\circ\psi\varphi=a\circ p\circ\varphi=a\circ p_W\). By the uniqueness in the universal property of \(W\) over \(T\), \(\psi\varphi=\mathrm{Id}_W\). So \(\varphi\) is an isomorphism.

(c) Take \(S=\Spec\R\), \(T=X=Y=\Spec\C\), with the canonical morphism \(T\to S\) (it is neither an open immersion, nor a closed immersion, nor a localization). Then \(X\times_TY=\Spec\C\) is a single point, whereas \(X\times_SY=\Spec(\C\otimes_\R\C)\) has two points: see Exercise 1.10(c) below, where \(\C\otimes_\R\C\simeq\C\times\C\) is proved. So \(X\times_TY\to X\times_SY\) is not an isomorphism (not even surjective). ∎

Exercise 1.7

Problem

Let \(X,Y\) be \(S\)-schemes, \(p,q\) the projections from \(X\times_SY\) to \(X,Y\). Fix \(s\in S\). Show that for any \(x\in X_s\) and any \(y\in Y_s\) there is a natural homeomorphism

\[ \Spec(k(x)\otimes_{k(s)}k(y))\to\{z\in X\times_SY\mid p(z)=x,\ q(z)=y\}. \]
Solution

Here \(x\in X_s\) means that \(x\in X\) lies over \(s\) (Prop. 1.16), so \(k(s)\to k(x)\) and \(k(s)\to k(y)\) are defined. Put \(K=k(x)\otimes_{k(s)}k(y)\) and \(F=\{z\mid p(z)=x,\ q(z)=y\}\).

The map. Let \(i_x:\Spec k(x)\to X\) and \(i_y:\Spec k(y)\to Y\) be the canonical morphisms. The structure morphism of \(X\) composed with \(i_x\) equals \(\Spec k(x)\to\Spec k(s)\to S\), and similarly for \(y\). Hence the compositions \(\Spec K\to\Spec k(x)\to X\) and \(\Spec K\to\Spec k(y)\to Y\) are \(S\)-morphisms (both \(S\)-structures on \(\Spec K\) factor through \(\Spec k(s)\)), and give \(u:\Spec K\to X\times_SY\). The images of \(p\circ u\) and \(q\circ u\) are \(\{x\}\) and \(\{y\}\), so \(u(\Spec K)\subseteq F\). We show that \(u\) is a homeomorphism onto \(F\).

Reduction to the affine case. Let \(V=\Spec A\) be an affine open neighborhood of \(s\) in \(S\), and \(X'=\Spec B\), \(Y'=\Spec C\) affine open neighborhoods of \(x\), \(y\) in the preimages of \(V\) in \(X\), \(Y\). By Prop. 1.4(d), \(X'\times_SY'\simeq p^{-1}(X')\cap q^{-1}(Y')\) is an open subscheme \(W\) of \(X\times_SY\), it contains \(F\), and \(F\) is the set of points of \(W\) whose images under the projections of \(W\) are \(x\) and \(y\). By Exercise 1.6(b) (applied to the open immersion \(V\to S\); the only property used there is 1.6(a)), \(X'\times_SY'\simeq X'\times_VY'=\Spec(B\otimes_AC)\) (Prop. 1.2). The morphism \(u\) factors through the open subscheme \(W\) since its image lies in \(F\subseteq W\), and the resulting morphism \(\Spec K\to W=\Spec(B\otimes_AC)\) is, by the universal property, the one induced by the ring homomorphism \(B\otimes_AC\to k(x)\otimes_Ak(y)\), \(b\otimes c\mapsto\bar b\otimes\bar c\). Note \(k(x)\otimes_Ak(y)=K\): the ring map \(A\to k(s)\) is a surjection followed by a localization, hence an epimorphism of rings, and so for \(k(s)\)-vector spaces \(M,N\) every \(A\)-bilinear map \(M\times N\to L\) (\(L\) a \(k(s)\)-vector space) is \(k(s)\)-bilinear. (If \(\lambda=\bar a_1/\bar a_2\) with \(a_i\in A\), \(\bar a_2\neq0\), then \(a_2\beta(\lambda m,n)=\beta(a_1m,n)=a_1\beta(m,n)\), and \(a_2\) acts invertibly on \(L\).) So \(M\otimes_AN=M\otimes_{k(s)}N\).

Algebra. Let \(P\subset B\), \(Q\subset C\) be the prime ideals of \(x\), \(y\), and \(R=B\otimes_AC\), \(\bar B=B/P\), \(\bar C=C/Q\), with fraction fields \(k(x)\), \(k(y)\). The points of \(F\) are the primes \(\fr\) of \(R\) whose preimages in \(B\) and \(C\) (under \(b\mapsto b\otimes1\), \(c\mapsto1\otimes c\)) are \(P\) and \(Q\). Such a prime contains the ideal \(J\) generated by the images of \(P\) and \(Q\), and \(R/J\simeq\bar B\otimes_A\bar C=:\bar R\) (right exactness of \(\otimes\)). By Lemma 2.1.7(b), \(\Spec\bar R\to\Spec R\) is a homeomorphism onto \(V(J)\), and under it \(F\) corresponds to the set of primes \(\bar\fr\) of \(\bar R\) whose preimages in \(\bar B\) and \(\bar C\) are \(0\), i.e. which contain no \(\beta\otimes1\) (\(\beta\ne0\)) and no \(1\otimes\gamma\) (\(\gamma\neq0\)). Since \(\bar\fr\) is prime and \(\beta\otimes\gamma=(\beta\otimes1)(1\otimes\gamma)\), this says exactly that \(\bar\fr\) is disjoint from the multiplicative set \(\Sigma=\{\beta\otimes\gamma\mid\beta\ne0,\gamma\neq0\}\). By Lemma 2.1.7(c), \(\Spec(\Sigma^{-1}\bar R)\) is homeomorphic to the set of such primes. Finally, localization commutes with tensor products, so

\[ \Sigma^{-1}\bar R=\Sigma^{-1}(\bar B\otimes_A\bar C)\simeq(\bar B\setminus0)^{-1}\bar B\otimes_A(\bar C\setminus0)^{-1}\bar C=k(x)\otimes_Ak(y)=K. \]

(The map from left to right is defined by the universal property of localization, since \(\beta\otimes\gamma\mapsto\beta\otimes\gamma\) is a unit on the right; the map in the other direction sends \((\beta/\beta')\otimes(\gamma/\gamma')\) to \((\beta\otimes\gamma)/(\beta'\otimes\gamma')\); they are inverse on generators.) The composite \(\Spec K\to\Spec\bar R\to\Spec R\) is \(\Spec\) of \(R\to K\), i.e. \(u\). So \(u\) is a homeomorphism onto \(F\). ∎

Exercise 1.8

Problem

Let \(X\to S\) be a surjective morphism. Show that \(X\times_SY\to Y\) is surjective for any \(S\)-scheme \(Y\) (use Prop. 1.16).

Solution

Let \(y\in Y\) and let \(s\in S\) be its image. Since \(X\to S\) is surjective there is \(x\in X\) over \(s\), i.e. \(x\in X_s\) in the notation of Prop. 1.16. By Exercise 1.7 the set \(\{z\in X\times_SY\mid p(z)=x,\ q(z)=y\}\) is homeomorphic to \(\Spec(k(x)\otimes_{k(s)}k(y))\). This tensor product of two nonzero vector spaces over the field \(k(s)\) is nonzero (a basis of \(k(x)\) tensored with a basis of \(k(y)\) gives a basis), so its spectrum is nonempty. Hence there is \(z\) with \(q(z)=y\), and \(X\times_SY\to Y\) is surjective. Thus surjectivity is stable under base change. ∎

Exercise 1.9

Problem

Let \(k\) be a field, \(A=k(u)\otimes_kk(v)\) the tensor product of two purely transcendental extensions of \(k\) of transcendence degree \(1\).

  1. (a)

    We have \(k(u)=T^{-1}k[u]\), \(T\) the set of nonzero elements of \(k[u]\). Deduce that \(A\) is the localization of \(k[u,v]\) with respect to the multiplicative set \(T'\) of the nonzero elements of the form \(P(u)Q(v)\in k[u,v]\).

  2. (b)

    Let \(\fm\) be a maximal ideal of \(k[u,v]\). Show that there is a \(P(u)\in\fm\setminus\{0\}\). Deduce that \(T'\cap\fm\neq\emptyset\).

  3. (c)

    Show that the maximal ideals of \(A\) are of the form \(gA\) with \(g\in k[u,v]\setminus(k[u]\cup k[v])\) irreducible in \(k[u,v]\).

  4. (d)

    Show that \(\Spec A\) is an infinite set and \(\dim A=1\).

Solution

(The printed “\(T'\cap\fm\ne\emptyset\)” in (b) is read as “\(\neq\)”; this is what is true, and what is needed in (c).)

(a) \(T'\) is multiplicative (a domain) and consists of products \(P(u)Q(v)\) with \(P,Q\neq0\). Each such element is a unit in \(A\), with inverse \(P(u)^{-1}\otimes Q(v)^{-1}\), so the canonical map \(k[u,v]=k[u]\otimes k[v]\to A\) induces \(\alpha:T'^{-1}k[u,v]\to A\). Conversely let \(R=T'^{-1}k[u,v]\). Every nonzero \(P(u)\) is a unit in \(R\) (it lies in \(T'\)), so by the universal property of localization \(k[u]\to R\) extends to a \(k\)-algebra map \(k(u)\to R\); likewise \(k(v)\to R\). By the universal property of the tensor product of \(k\)-algebras these give \(\beta:A\to R\). The composites \(\beta\alpha\) and \(\alpha\beta\) are the identity on the generators \(u,v\) (resp. on \(a\otimes1\), \(1\otimes b\)) of the respective algebras, hence are identities (for \(\alpha\beta\): \(A\) is generated by the elements \(a\otimes1,1\otimes b\) as a ring, since \(a\otimes b=(a\otimes1)(1\otimes b)\)). So \(A=T'^{-1}k[u,v]\); in particular \(A\) is a domain.

(b) The field \(k[u,v]/\fm\) is a finitely generated \(k\)-algebra, hence a finite extension of \(k\) (Cor. 2.1.12). So the image of \(u\) is algebraic over \(k\): there is a nonzero \(P\in k[u]\) with \(P(u)\in\fm\). Then \(P(u)=P(u)\cdot1\in T'\cap\fm\).

(c) Primes of \(A=T'^{-1}k[u,v]\) correspond bijectively to primes \(\fp\) of \(k[u,v]\) with \(\fp\cap T'=\emptyset\) (Lemma 2.1.7(c)), by \(\fp\mapsto\fp A\), and inclusions are preserved both ways. Since \(\fp\) is prime, \(\fp\cap T'=\emptyset\) is equivalent to \(\fp\cap k[u]=0\) and \(\fp\cap k[v]=0\).

Let \(\fp\ne0\) be such a prime. As \(k[u,v]\) is a UFD, \(\fp\) contains an irreducible element \(g\), and \((g)\) is prime. If \(\fp\ne(g)\), then \(0\subsetneq(g)\subsetneq\fp\) and, since \(\dim k[u,v]=2\), \(\fp\) is a maximal ideal; but maximal ideals meet \(T'\) by (b). So \(\fp=(g)\) with \(g\) irreducible, and \(g\notin k[u]\cup k[v]\) because \(g\in\fp\) and \(\fp\cap k[u]=\fp\cap k[v]=0\).

Conversely let \(g\) be irreducible with \(g\notin k[u]\cup k[v]\). Then \((g)\cap T'=\emptyset\): if \(g\mid P(u)Q(v)\) with \(P,Q\ne0\), then \(g\mid P\) or \(g\mid Q\) (\(g\) is prime), and a divisor of a nonzero polynomial in \(u\) alone has degree \(0\) in \(v\), so \(g\in k[u]\) or \(g\in k[v]\), a contradiction. Thus \(gA\) is a proper prime ideal of \(A\). It is maximal: a prime strictly containing it would be \(0\) or \(g'A\), and \(gA\subseteq g'A\) means \((g)\subseteq(g')\) in \(k[u,v]\), hence \(g'=gh\) with \(h\) a unit (\(g'\) irreducible), so \(g'A=gA\).

Finally, \(A\) is not a field since \(g=u-v\notin k[u]\cup k[v]\) is irreducible (degree \(1\)) and gives the proper nonzero ideal \(gA\). So \(0\) is not maximal, and every maximal ideal of \(A\) is a nonzero prime, hence of the form \(gA\) as described. Together with the previous paragraph, the maximal ideals of \(A\) are exactly the \(gA\) with \(g\) irreducible and \(g\notin k[u]\cup k[v]\).

(d) For \(n\ge1\) the polynomials \(g_n=v-u^n\) are irreducible (of degree \(1\) in \(v\), monic in \(v\)) and do not lie in \(k[u]\cup k[v]\). They are pairwise non-associate (the coefficient of \(v\) is \(1\), and the units of \(k[u,v]\) are the elements of \(k^*\)), so the ideals \((g_n)\) are pairwise distinct, and so are the \(g_nA\). Hence \(\Spec A\) is infinite. By (c), every nonzero prime of \(A\) is maximal, so there is no chain \(\fp_0\subsetneq\fp_1\subsetneq\fp_2\) of primes, and \(0\subsetneq gA\) is a chain of length \(1\). Therefore \(\dim A=1\). ∎

Exercise 1.10

Problem

Let \(\pi:X\to S\), \(\rho:Y\to S\) be \(S\)-schemes. Let \(\operatorname{sp}(X)\times_{\operatorname{sp}(S)}\operatorname{sp}(Y)\) be the fibered product of sets defined by \(\pi,\rho\), endowed with the topology induced by the product topology on \(\operatorname{sp}(X)\times\operatorname{sp}(Y)\).

  1. (a)

    Show that there is a canonical continuous map \(f:\operatorname{sp}(X\times_SY)\to\operatorname{sp}(X)\times_{\operatorname{sp}(S)}\operatorname{sp}(Y)\).

  2. (b)

    Show that \(f\) is surjective.

  3. (c)

    Let \(X=Y=\Spec\C\), \(S=\Spec\R\). Show that \(X\times_SY\simeq\Spec(\C\oplus\C)\) and that \(f\) is not injective.

  4. (d)

    In the situation of Exercise 1.9, with \(X=\Spec k(u)\), \(Y=\Spec k(v)\), \(S=\Spec k\), show that \(f\) has infinite fibers.

  5. (e)

    Let \(S=\Spec k\) with \(k\) an arbitrary field. By studying \(X=Y=\mathbb A^1_k\), show that the image of an open subset under \(f\) is not necessarily open.

Solution

(a) Let \(p,q\) be the projections. They are continuous, and \(\pi\circ p=\rho\circ q\) (the projections are \(S\)-morphisms), so \(f=(p,q)\) maps \(X\times_SY\) into the subset \(\{(x,y)\mid\pi(x)=\rho(y)\}\). A map into a subspace of a product, whose components are continuous, is continuous for the induced topology.

(b) Let \((x,y)\) be a point of the target, \(s=\pi(x)=\rho(y)\). By Exercise 1.7 the fiber \(\{z\mid p(z)=x,q(z)=y\}\) is homeomorphic to \(\Spec(k(x)\otimes_{k(s)}k(y))\), which is nonempty, since a tensor product of two nonzero vector spaces over a field is nonzero. So \((x,y)\in f(X\times_SY)\).

(c) We have \(\C=\R[x]/(x^2+1)\), so \(\C\otimes_\R\C\simeq\C[x]/(x^2+1)=\C[x]/((x-i)(x+i))\). Concretely, define \(\theta:\C\otimes_\R\C\to\C\times\C\), \(a\otimes b\mapsto(ab,a\bar b)\). This is well defined (\(\R\)-bilinear in \((a,b)\)), multiplicative on pure tensors, so a ring homomorphism. The images of \(1\otimes1,\ 1\otimes i,\ i\otimes1,\ i\otimes i\) are \((1,1),(i,-i),(i,i),(-1,1)\). Their \(\R\)-span contains \((1,1)\pm(-1,1)\), hence \((0,1)\) and \((1,0)\), and \((i,i)\pm(i,-i)\), hence \((i,0)\) and \((0,i)\), so it is all of \(\C\times\C\). Both sides have dimension \(4\) over \(\R\), so \(\theta\) is an isomorphism. By Prop. 1.2, \(X\times_SY=\Spec(\C\otimes_\R\C)\simeq\Spec(\C\times\C)=\Spec\C\sqcup\Spec\C\), which has exactly two points. The target \(\operatorname{sp}(X)\times_{\operatorname{sp}(S)}\operatorname{sp}(Y)\) is a single point. So \(f\) is surjective but not injective.

(d) Here \(\operatorname{sp}(X)\), \(\operatorname{sp}(Y)\), \(\operatorname{sp}(S)\) are single points, so the target of \(f\) is one point and the only fiber is all of \(\operatorname{sp}(X\times_SY)=\Spec A\) (Prop. 1.2), which is infinite by Exercise 1.9(d).

(e) We assume \(k\) is infinite; for finite \(k\) the assertion is actually false, see the remark at the end. By Example 1.3 (or Prop. 1.2), \(X\times_SY=\Spec k[u]\otimes_kk[v]=\Spec k[u,v]\), and \(f(\fp)=(\fp\cap k[u],\fp\cap k[v])\). Since \(\operatorname{sp}(S)\) is a point, the target is \(\mathbb A^1_k\times\mathbb A^1_k\) with the product of the Zariski topologies. The open sets of \(\mathbb A^1_k\) are \(\emptyset\) and complements of finite sets of closed points (Example 2.1.4).

Let \(U=D(u-v)\subset\Spec k[u,v]\), which contains the generic point \((0)\), and \(f((0))=(\xi,\xi)\) where \(\xi\) is the generic point of \(\mathbb A^1_k\). So \((\xi,\xi)\in f(U)\). For \(c\in k\) let \(a_c=(u-c)\in\mathbb A^1_k\) (a closed point, \(a_c\ne a_{c'}\) for \(c\neq c'\)) and \(b_c=(v-c)\). If \(\fp\) is a prime with \(f(\fp)=(a_c,b_c)\), then \(u-c,v-c\in\fp\), hence \(u-v\in\fp\) and \(\fp\notin U\). Thus \((a_c,b_c)\notin f(U)\) for all \(c\in k\).

Suppose \(f(U)\) were open. It would contain a basic neighborhood \(V_1\times V_2\) of \((\xi,\xi)\) with \(V_i=\mathbb A^1_k\setminus F_i\) for finite sets \(F_i\) of closed points. As \(k\) is infinite there is \(c\in k\) with \(a_c\notin F_1\) and \(b_c\notin F_2\) (only finitely many \(c\) are excluded). Then \((a_c,b_c)\in V_1\times V_2\subseteq f(U)\), contradicting the above. So \(f(U)\) is not open, although \(U\) is.

Remark (finite \(k\)): the printed statement is false. Let \(k\) be finite; we show that then \(f(U)\) is open for every open \(U\subseteq\Spec k[u,v]\), so no example exists. Since \(f(\bigcup U_i)=\bigcup f(U_i)\) it suffices to treat \(U=D(h)\), \(h\neq0\). Pull out of \(h\) all irreducible factors lying in \(k[u]\cup k[v]\): \(h=P(u)Q(v)h'\), where \(h'\) has no irreducible factor in \(k[u]\cup k[v]\). Let \(A_P\) (resp. \(B_Q\)) be the finite set of closed points of \(\mathbb A^1_k\) given by the irreducible factors of \(P\) (resp. \(Q\)).

By Exercise 1.7 (with \(S=\Spec k\)), the fiber of \(f\) over \(t=(t_1,t_2)\) is \(\Spec K_t\) with \(K_t=k(t_1)\otimes_kk(t_2)\), so \(t\notin f(U)\) iff the image \(h_t\) of \(h\) lies in every prime of \(K_t\). Each \(K_t\) is reduced: \(k\) is perfect, so \(k(t_1)\otimes_k k(t_2)\) is reduced when one factor is finite over \(k\), and it is a domain (a localization of \(k[u,v]\)) when both are \(k(u),k(v)\). Hence \(t\notin f(U)\) iff \(h_t=0\). We determine these \(t\).

\(t=(\xi,\xi)\): \(K_t\) is a localization of \(k[u,v]\) and \(h\ne0\), so \(t\in f(U)\).

\(t=(\xi,b)\) with \(b=(g(v))\), \(g\) irreducible: \(K_t=k(u)[v]/(g)\) has \(k(u)\)-basis \(1,\dots,v^{\deg g-1}\). Divide \(h=gq+r\) in \(k[u][v]\) (\(g\) is monic in \(v\)) with \(\deg_vr<\deg g\); then \(h_t=0\) iff \(r=0\) iff \(g\mid h\) in \(k[u,v]\). As \(g\) is prime and \(g\nmid h'\), \(g\nmid P(u)\) (it has positive degree in \(v\)), this holds iff \(g\mid Q\), i.e. iff \(b\in B_Q\). Symmetrically \((a,\xi)\notin f(U)\) iff \(a\in A_P\).

\(t=(a,b)\), \(a=(f_a(u))\), \(b=(f_b(v))\) closed points: if \(a\in A_P\) or \(b\in B_Q\) then \(P_t=0\) or \(Q_t=0\), so \(t\notin f(U)\). Suppose \(a\notin A_P\), \(b\notin B_Q\). Then \(P_t,Q_t\) are nonzero elements of the fields \(k(a)\), \(k(b)\), hence units of \(K_t\), and \(h_t=0\) iff \(h'_t=0\). Let \(\alpha\) be the class of \(u\) in \(k(a)\); then \(K_t=k(a)[v]/(f_b)\) and \(h'_t=0\) iff \(f_b\mid h'(\alpha,v)\) in \(k(a)[v]\). Now \(h'(\alpha,v)\neq0\), for otherwise every coefficient (in \(k[u]\)) of \(h'\) as a polynomial in \(v\) is divisible by \(f_a\), so \(f_a\mid h'\), contradicting the choice of \(h'\). Hence \(\deg f_b\le\deg_vh'\), and symmetrically \(\deg f_a\le\deg_uh'\). As \(k\) is finite, there are only finitely many irreducible polynomials of bounded degree, so only a finite set \(Z\) of pairs \((a,b)\) of this last kind can lie outside \(f(U)\).

Altogether the complement of \(f(U)\) is

\[ (A_P\times\mathbb A^1_k)\ \cup\ (\mathbb A^1_k\times B_Q)\ \cup\ Z, \]

a union of two products of a finite set of closed points with \(\mathbb A^1_k\) and of a finite set of points \(\{a\}\times\{b\}\), all closed in the product topology (finite sets of closed points are closed in \(\mathbb A^1_k\)). So \(f(U)\) is open. Hence for finite \(k\) the image of an open set is always open, and the assertion of (e) holds only for infinite \(k\). ∎

Exercise 1.11

Problem

Let \(k\) be a field and \(z\in\mathbb P^n_k(k)\). Choose homogeneous coordinates such that \(z=(1,0,\dots,0)\).

  1. (a)

    Show that there is a morphism \(p:\mathbb P^n_k\setminus\{z\}\to\mathbb P^{n-1}_k\) such that over \(\overline k\), the algebraic closure of \(k\), we have \(p_{\overline k}(a_0,a_1,\dots,a_n)=(a_1,\dots,a_n)\) for every point of \(\mathbb P^n_{\overline k}(\overline k)\). Such a morphism is called a projection with center \(z\).

  2. (b)

    Let \(X\) be a closed subset of \(\mathbb P^n_k\) not containing \(z\). Show that \(X\) cannot contain \(p^{-1}(y)\) for any \(y\in\mathbb P^{n-1}_k\).

Solution

Write \(\mathbb P^n_k=\operatorname{Proj}k[T_0,\dots,T_n]\) and \(\mathbb P^{n-1}_k=\operatorname{Proj}k[S_1,\dots,S_n]\). The point \(z=(1:0:\dots:0)\) is the point of the homogeneous prime \((T_1,\dots,T_n)\), i.e. \(\{z\}=V_+(T_1,\dots,T_n)\).

(a) Let \(\phi:C=k[S_1,\dots,S_n]\to B=k[T_0,\dots,T_n]\) be the homomorphism of graded rings with \(S_j\mapsto T_j\). Then \(M=\phi(C_+)B=(T_1,\dots,T_n)\) and \(V_+(M)=\{z\}\). By Lemma 2.3.40 we get a morphism of \(k\)-schemes \(p:\mathbb P^n_k\setminus\{z\}\to\mathbb P^{n-1}_k\) with \(p^{-1}(D_+(S_i))=D_+(T_i)\) (\(1\le i\le n\)), whose restriction to \(D_+(T_i)=\Spec k[T_j/T_i]_{0\le j\le n}\) is the morphism of affine schemes given by the inclusion \(C_{(S_i)}=k[S_j/S_i]_{1\le j\le n}\subseteq B_{(T_i)}\).

Over \(\overline k\): \(p_{\overline k}=p\times\mathrm{Id}_{\overline k}\) is the restriction of \(p\) to the open subscheme \((\mathbb P^n_k\setminus\{z\})_{\overline k}=\mathbb P^n_{\overline k}\setminus\{z\}\) (Prop. 1.4(d), Prop. 1.9) and on \(D_+(T_i)_{\overline k}=\Spec\overline k[T_j/T_i]\) it is given by the inclusion \(\overline k[S_j/S_i]_{1\le j\le n}\subseteq\overline k[T_j/T_i]_{0\le j\le n}\) (base change of a morphism of affine schemes, Example 1.8). Let \(a=(a_0,\dots,a_n)\in\mathbb P^n(\overline k)\), \(a\ne z\), i.e. \((a_1,\dots,a_n)\neq0\); choose \(i\ge1\) with \(a_i\ne0\). The point \(a\) is the \(\overline k\)-point of \(D_+(T_i)_{\overline k}\) given by the \(\overline k\)-algebra homomorphism \(T_j/T_i\mapsto a_j/a_i\). Composing with \(p_{\overline k}\) we get the \(\overline k\)-point of \(D_+(S_i)\subseteq\mathbb P^{n-1}_{\overline k}\) given by \(S_j/S_i\mapsto a_j/a_i\) (\(1\le j\le n\)), i.e. the point \((a_1:\dots:a_n)\). This proves (a).

(b) Let \(y\in\mathbb P^{n-1}_k\) be any point and suppose \(p^{-1}(y)\subseteq X\). We derive \(z\in X\). Choose \(i\in\{1,\dots,n\}\) with \(y\in D_+(S_i)\). Let \(K=k(y)\) and let \(b_j\in K\) be the image of \(S_j/S_i\) in \(K\) (\(1\le j\le n\); \(b_i=1\)). Then the canonical morphism \(\Spec K\to\Spec k[S_j/S_i]\subseteq\mathbb P^{n-1}_k\) has image \(\{y\}\) and corresponds to \(S_j/S_i\mapsto b_j\). The vector \((b_1,\dots,b_n)\in K^n\) is nonzero.

Let \(\phi_K:K[T_0,\dots,T_n]\to K[t,s]\) be the graded homomorphism with \(T_0\mapsto t\), \(T_j\mapsto b_js\) (\(j\ge1\)). The ideal generated by the images is \((t,b_1s,\dots,b_ns)=(t,s)\) because \(b_i=1\), so by Lemma 2.3.40 (with \(V_+((t,s))=\emptyset\)) we get a morphism \(G_0:\mathbb P^1_K\to\mathbb P^n_K\) with \(G_0^{-1}(D_+(T_j))=D_+(\phi_K(T_j))\). Let \(G:\mathbb P^1_K\to\mathbb P^n_K\to\mathbb P^n_k\) be its composite with the projection of the base change (Prop. 1.9).

Claim 1: \(G(D_+(s))\subseteq p^{-1}(y)\). We have \(G_0^{-1}(D_+(T_i))=D_+(b_is)=D_+(s)\) and \(D_+(T_i)\subseteq\mathbb P^n_k\setminus\{z\}\). On \(D_+(s)=\Spec K[\tau]\) (\(\tau=t/s\)), the morphism \(G_0\) into \(D_+(T_i)_K=\Spec K[T_j/T_i]\) is given by \(T_j/T_i\mapsto b_j\) (\(j\ge1\)) and \(T_0/T_i\mapsto\tau\). Hence \(p\circ G|_{D_+(s)}\) corresponds to the ring homomorphism \(k[S_j/S_i]\to K[\tau]\), \(S_j/S_i\mapsto b_j\in K\subset K[\tau]\). This is the composite of \(k[S_j/S_i]\to K\) (\(S_j/S_i\mapsto b_j\)) and \(K\subseteq K[\tau]\), so \(p\circ G|_{D_+(s)}\) factors as \(D_+(s)\to\Spec K\to\mathbb P^{n-1}_k\) and its image is \(\{y\}\). So \(G(D_+(s))\subseteq p^{-1}(y)\subseteq X\).

Claim 2: \(G(\infty)=z\), where \(\infty=V_+(s)\) is the point \((1:0)\) of \(\mathbb P^1_K\). The point \(\infty\) lies in \(D_+(t)=\Spec K[\sigma]\) (\(\sigma=s/t\)) as the prime \((\sigma)\), and \(G_0^{-1}(D_+(T_0))=D_+(t)\), where \(G_0\) is given by \(T_j/T_0\mapsto b_j\sigma\) (\(j\ge1\)). The preimage of \((\sigma)\) in \(K[T_j/T_0]\) is \((T_1/T_0,\dots,T_n/T_0)\), and its preimage in \(k[T_j/T_0]\) is the maximal ideal \((T_j/T_0)_{1\le j\le n}\), which is the point \(z\).

Now \(G^{-1}(X)\) is closed in \(\mathbb P^1_K\) and contains \(D_+(s)\) by Claim 1. The generic point \(\eta\) of \(\mathbb P^1_K\) (the prime \((0)\) of \(K[\tau,\tau^{-1}]=K[\sigma,\sigma^{-1}]\)) lies in \(D_+(s)\), and its closure in \(D_+(t)=\Spec K[\sigma]\) is all of \(\Spec K[\sigma]\), which contains \(\infty\). So \(\infty\in\overline{\{\eta\}}\subseteq G^{-1}(X)\), and \(z=G(\infty)\in X\) by Claim 2. This contradicts \(z\notin X\). Hence \(X\) does not contain \(p^{-1}(y)\). (The proof works for every point \(y\) of the scheme \(\mathbb P^{n-1}_k\), closed or not, and a fortiori for the \(\overline k\)-points of \(\mathbb P^{n-1}\) through their images.) ∎

3.2 Applications to algebraic varieties

Conventions for this section. An algebraic variety over a field \(k\) is a scheme of finite type over \(k\) (Example 2.3). A scheme is Noetherian if it is a finite union of affine opens \(\Spec A_i\) with \(A_i\) Noetherian; then every affine open has a Noetherian ring. Results of the text are cited by their numbers (“Prop. 2.2” is in Section 3.2, “Prop. 1.16” in Section 3.1, “Prop. 2.5.10” and “Ex. 2.3.17” in Chapter 2). Honest notes: in Ex. 2.6 the map \(K(Y)\to K(X)\) only makes sense when \(f\) is dominant, and this is part of the meaning of “birational”. In Ex. 2.7 the varieties \(\overline X,\overline Y\) are over \(\overline k\) and \(X,Y\) over \(K\) (the bars are lost in some printings). In Ex. 2.9 the maps are \(X(\overline k)\to Y(\overline k)\). In Ex. 2.10(b) the deduction about \(X_{\overline k}\) is proved by a direct argument (Galois transitivity is proved for finite Galois \(K\), and \(\overline k/k\) need not be separable). Several solutions use common lemmas that are proved where first needed (Lemma L in 2.4, Lemmas M and U in 2.5, Lemma A in 2.7, Lemma C in 2.9, Lemma D in 2.10).

Exercise 2.1

Problem

Let \(Y\) be a Noetherian scheme. Show that any \(Y\)-scheme \(X\) of finite type is Noetherian. Moreover, if \(Y\) is of finite dimension, then so is \(X\).

Solution

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be the structure morphism and write \(Y=V_1\cup\dots\cup V_m\) with \(V_i=\Spec A_i\), \(A_i\) Noetherian. Since \(f\) is quasi-compact, \(f^{-1}(V_i)\) is quasi-compact, hence a finite union of affine opens \(U_{ij}=\Spec B_{ij}\). By Def. 2.1, \(B_{ij}\) is a finitely generated \(A_i\)-algebra, i.e. a quotient of a polynomial ring \(A_i[T_1,\dots,T_{n_{ij}}]\), which is Noetherian by Hilbert's basis theorem. So \(B_{ij}\) is Noetherian, and \(X=\bigcup_{i,j}U_{ij}\) is a finite union of affine opens with Noetherian rings: \(X\) is Noetherian.

Dimension. Fact: if a topological space \(T\) is a union of open subsets \(T_\lambda\), then \(\dim T=\sup_\lambda\dim T_\lambda\). Indeed, \(\ge\) is clear (if \(Z_0\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq Z_n\) are irreducible closed subsets of \(T_\lambda\), their closures in \(T\) are irreducible, closed and strictly increasing, since \(\overline{Z_j}\cap T_\lambda=Z_j\)). Conversely, given a chain \(Z_0\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq Z_n\) of irreducible closed subsets of \(T\), choose \(\lambda\) with \(T_\lambda\cap Z_0\neq\emptyset\). Each \(Z_j\cap T_\lambda\) is a nonempty open subset of the irreducible \(Z_j\), hence irreducible and dense in \(Z_j\), and closed in \(T_\lambda\). The inclusions \(Z_j\cap T_\lambda\subsetneq Z_{j+1}\cap T_\lambda\) are strict because both are dense in \(Z_j\), resp. \(Z_{j+1}\), and \(Z_j\ne Z_{j+1}\) are closed. So \(\dim T_\lambda\ge n\).

Now let \(d=\dim Y<\infty\). By the fact, \(\dim A_i=\dim V_i\le d\) (note \(\dim V_i\le \dim Y\) by the easy half). For a Noetherian ring \(A\) one has \(\dim A[T_1,\dots,T_n]=\dim A+n\) (Krull; e.g. Matsumura, Commutative Ring Theory, Thm. 15.4), and a quotient of a ring has dimension at most that of the ring. So \(\dim B_{ij}\le d+n_{ij}\), and by the fact, \(\dim X=\max_{i,j}\dim U_{ij}\le d+\max_{i,j}n_{ij}<\infty\). ∎

Exercise 2.2

Problem

Show that any open immersion into a locally Noetherian scheme is a morphism of finite type.

Solution

Let \(j:U\to Y\) be an open immersion, \(Y\) locally Noetherian. Identify \(U\) with the open subscheme \(j(U)\) of \(Y\). Cover \(Y\) by affine opens \(V_i=\Spec A_i\) with \(A_i\) Noetherian. We check the hypothesis of Prop. 2.2 for this cover.

The space \(V_i=\Spec A_i\) is a Noetherian topological space (the ascending chain condition on radical ideals gives the descending chain condition on closed subsets), so every open subset of it is quasi-compact. In particular \(j^{-1}(V_i)=U\cap V_i\) is quasi-compact. Since the principal opens \(D(a)\subseteq U\cap V_i\) (\(a\in A_i\)) form a base of the topology of \(U\cap V_i\), there are \(a_{i1},\dots,a_{ir_i}\in A_i\) with \(U\cap V_i=D(a_{i1})\cup\dots\cup D(a_{ir_i})\). Each \(D(a_{il})\simeq\Spec (A_i)_{a_{il}}\) (Lemma 2.3.7) is affine, and \(\mathcal O(D(a_{il}))=(A_i)_{a_{il}}=A_i[1/a_{il}]\) is a finitely generated \(A_i\)-algebra. By Prop. 2.2, \(j\) is of finite type. ∎

Exercise 2.3

Problem

An immersion of schemes is a morphism which is an open immersion followed by a closed immersion.

  1. (a)

    Let \(f:X\to Y\) be an immersion. Show that it can be decomposed into a closed immersion followed by an open immersion.

  2. (b)

    Show that the converse of (a) is true if \(f\) is moreover quasi-compact (e.g., if \(Y\) is locally Noetherian). Use the scheme-theoretic closure of \(f\) (Exercise 2.3.17).

  3. (c)

    Let \(f:X\to Y\), \(g:Y\to Z\) be two immersions with \(g\) quasi-compact. Show that \(g\circ f\) is an immersion.

Solution

We use freely that compositions of open immersions, and of closed immersions, are again open, resp. closed immersions, and that a closed immersion restricted over an open subset of the target is a closed immersion (both defining conditions are local on the target).

(a) Write \(f=i\circ j\) with \(j:X\to Z\) an open immersion and \(i:Z\to Y\) a closed immersion. Identify \(X\) with the open subscheme \(j(X)\) of \(Z\). The set \(T=i(Z\setminus j(X))\) is closed in \(Y\) (\(i\) is a homeomorphism onto a closed subset), so \(W=Y\setminus T\) is open. As \(i\) is injective, \(i^{-1}(W)=Z\setminus (Z\setminus j(X))=j(X)=X\). Hence \(i\) restricts to a closed immersion \(i':X=i^{-1}(W)\to W\), and \(f\) is \(i'\) followed by the open immersion \(W\hookrightarrow Y\).

(b) Let \(f=\iota\circ g\) with \(g:X\to W\) a closed immersion and \(\iota:W\to Y\) an open immersion, and assume \(f\) quasi-compact. (If \(Y\) is locally Noetherian, \(\iota\) is quasi-compact by Ex. 2.3.17(b), \(g\) is quasi-compact by Ex. 2.3.17(a), and a composition of quasi-compact morphisms is quasi-compact: if \(V\subseteq Y\) is affine open then \(\iota^{-1}(V)\) is a finite union of affines \(V_l\), and \(g^{-1}(V_l)\) is quasi-compact.) By Ex. 2.3.17(c),(d), \(\mathcal J=\Ker(f^\#:\mathcal O_Y\to f_*\mathcal O_X)\) defines a closed subscheme \(Z=V(\mathcal J)\) of \(Y\) (the scheme-theoretic closure of \(f(X)\)). Since \(f^{-1}(W)=X\), we have \((f_*\mathcal O_X)|_W=g_*\mathcal O_X\), and so \(\mathcal J|_W=\Ker(g^\#:\mathcal O_W\to g_*\mathcal O_X)\). As \(g\) is a closed immersion, \(g\) identifies \(X\) with the closed subscheme of \(W\) defined by this ideal sheaf, i.e. with \(Z\cap W\) (an open subscheme of \(Z\)). Therefore \(X\simeq Z\cap W\hookrightarrow Z\) is an open immersion, and \(f\) is this open immersion followed by the closed immersion \(Z\to Y\) (indeed \(X\simeq Z\cap W\to Z\to Y\) is \(X\to W\to Y\), which is \(f\)). So \(f\) is an immersion.

(c) Write \(f=c_f\circ o_f\) with \(o_f:X\to Y'\) open and \(c_f:Y'\to Y\) closed. By (a), \(g=o_g\circ c_g\) with \(c_g:Y\to Z'\) closed and \(o_g:Z'\to Z\) open. Then \(h:=g\circ c_f=o_g\circ(c_g\circ c_f)\) is a closed immersion followed by an open immersion. It is quasi-compact, being the composition of the quasi-compact \(g\) with the closed immersion \(c_f\) (quasi-compact by Ex. 2.3.17(a)). By (b), \(h=c''\circ o''\) with \(o'':Y'\to Z''\) open and \(c'':Z''\to Z\) closed. Hence \(g\circ f=h\circ o_f=c''\circ(o''\circ o_f)\), where \(o''\circ o_f\) is an open immersion and \(c''\) a closed immersion. So \(g\circ f\) is an immersion. ∎

Exercise 2.4

Problem

Let \(X,Y\) be schemes over a locally Noetherian scheme \(S\), with \(Y\) of finite type over \(S\). Let \(x\in X\). Show that for any morphism of \(S\)-schemes \(f_x:\Spec\mathcal O_{X,x}\to Y\), there exist an open subset \(U\ni x\) of \(X\) and a morphism of \(S\)-schemes \(f:U\to Y\) such that \(f_x=f\circ i_x\), where \(i_x:\Spec\mathcal O_{X,x}\to U\) is the canonical morphism (in other words, the morphism \(f_x\) extends to an open neighborhood of \(x\)).

Solution

Lemma L. Let \(A\) be a Noetherian ring, \(C\) a finitely generated \(A\)-algebra, \(R\) an \(A\)-algebra, \(\Sigma\subseteq R\) a multiplicative subset, and \(\bar\psi:C\to\Sigma^{-1}R\) a homomorphism of \(A\)-algebras. Then there are \(\sigma\in\Sigma\) and an \(A\)-algebra homomorphism \(\psi:C\to R_\sigma\) such that \(\bar\psi\) is \(\psi\) followed by \(R_\sigma\to\Sigma^{-1}R\).

Proof. By Hilbert's basis theorem \(C\simeq A[t_1,\dots,t_n]/(P_1,\dots,P_m)\) with \(P_l\in A[t_1,\dots,t_n]\). Write \(\bar\psi(t_i)=r_i/s\) with \(r_i\in R\) and a common denominator \(s\in\Sigma\), and put \(\rho_i=r_i/s\in R_s\). The element \(P_l(\rho_1,\dots,\rho_n)\in R_s\) has image \(\bar\psi(P_l(t))=0\) in \(\Sigma^{-1}R\). By the definition of localization there is \(w_l\in\Sigma\) killing it in \(R_s\). Put \(\sigma=s\,w_1\cdots w_m\). In \(R_\sigma\) the \(w_l\) are units, so \(P_l(\rho)=0\) there. Hence \(t_i\mapsto\rho_i\) defines an \(A\)-algebra homomorphism \(\psi:C\to R_\sigma\), and its composite with \(R_\sigma\to\Sigma^{-1}R\) agrees with \(\bar\psi\) on the generators \(t_i\). ∎

Now the exercise. Let \(s\in S\) be the image of \(x\), and choose an affine open \(S_0=\Spec A\ni s\) of \(S\); \(A\) is Noetherian. The image \(y\) of the closed point of \(\Spec\mathcal O_{X,x}\) under \(f_x\) lies over \(s\) (as \(f_x\) is an \(S\)-morphism). Choose an affine open \(V=\Spec B\ni y\) of \(Y\) inside the preimage of \(S_0\); \(B\) is a finitely generated \(A\)-algebra because \(Y\) is of finite type over \(S\). The preimage \(f_x^{-1}(V)\) is open and contains the closed point of the local scheme \(\Spec\mathcal O_{X,x}\). The closed point lies in the closure of every point, so an open set containing it contains every point. Hence \(f_x^{-1}(V)=\Spec\mathcal O_{X,x}\), and \(f_x\) factors through \(V=\Spec B\), i.e. it is given by a ring homomorphism \(\bar\psi:B\to\mathcal O_{X,x}\). Since \(f_x\) is an \(S\)-morphism and \(S_0\) is affine, \(\bar\psi\) is an \(A\)-algebra homomorphism.

Choose an affine open \(U_0=\Spec C\ni x\) of \(X\) lying over \(S_0\), and let \(\mathfrak P\subset C\) be the prime ideal of \(x\), so that \(\mathcal O_{X,x}=C_{\mathfrak P}\). Apply Lemma L with \(R=C\), \(\Sigma=C\setminus\mathfrak P\): there are \(\sigma\in C\setminus\mathfrak P\) and an \(A\)-homomorphism \(\psi:B\to C_\sigma\) with \(\bar\psi=(C_\sigma\to C_{\mathfrak P})\circ\psi\). Put \(U=D(\sigma)=\Spec C_\sigma\ni x\) and let \(f:U\to V\subseteq Y\) be the morphism \(\Spec\psi\). It is an \(S\)-morphism, since over the affine \(S_0\) both structure maps correspond to \(A\to C_\sigma\) (\(\psi\) is \(A\)-linear). The morphism \(i_x\) corresponds to \(C_\sigma\to C_{\mathfrak P}\), so \(f\circ i_x\) corresponds to \(B\to C_\sigma\to C_{\mathfrak P}\), which is \(\bar\psi\). Thus \(f\circ i_x=f_x\). ∎

Exercise 2.5

Problem

Let \(S\) be a locally Noetherian scheme. Let \(X,Y\) be \(S\)-schemes of finite type. Let us fix \(s\in S\). Let \(\varphi:X\times_S\Spec\mathcal O_{S,s}\to Y\times_S\Spec\mathcal O_{S,s}\) be a morphism of \(S\)-schemes. Show that there exist an open set \(U\ni s\) and a morphism of \(S\)-schemes \(f:X\times_SU\to Y\times_SU\) such that \(\varphi\) is obtained from \(f\) by the base change \(\Spec\mathcal O_{S,s}\to U\). If \(\varphi\) is an isomorphism, show that there exists such an \(f\) which is moreover an isomorphism.

Solution

Setup. Choose an affine open \(S_0=\Spec A\ni s\) with \(A\) Noetherian, and let \(\mathfrak p\subset A\) be the prime of \(s\), \(\Sigma=A\setminus\mathfrak p\), \(T=\Spec A_{\mathfrak p}=\Spec\mathcal O_{S,s}\). The morphism \(T\to S\) factors through \(S_0\). If \(X_0,Y_0\) are the preimages of \(S_0\) in \(X,Y\), then \(X\times_ST=X_0\times_{S_0}T\) (a pair of morphisms \(Z\to X\), \(Z\to T\) with the same image in \(S\) lands in \(X_0\) since \(Z\to T\to S\) factors through \(S_0\)) and similarly for \(Y\); and \(X_0\times_S U=X_0\times_{S_0}U\) for \(U\subseteq S_0\) open. So we may replace \(S\) by \(S_0\), \(X\) by \(X_0\) and \(Y\) by \(Y_0\), and assume \(S=\Spec A\). Then \(X,Y\) are quasi-compact and \(X_a:=X\times_SD(a)\) is the preimage of \(D(a)\), for \(a\in A\). Write \(X_T=X\times_ST\), \(p:X_T\to X\) the projection, and similarly for \(Y\). For \(a\in\Sigma\) the open \(D(a)\) contains \(s\), and the sets \(D(a)\), \(a\in\Sigma\), form a fundamental system of neighbourhoods of \(s\) among principal opens.

A morphism \(Z\to T=\Spec A_{\mathfrak p}\) is a ring homomorphism \(A_{\mathfrak p}\to\mathcal O(Z)\), hence is determined by its restriction to \(A\). So two morphisms into \(T\) with the same composite to \(S\) coincide. Applied to \(q_Y\circ\varphi\) and \(q_X\) (\(q\) the maps to \(T\)), this shows that \(\varphi\) is a \(T\)-morphism. Hence \(\varphi\) corresponds, via the universal property of \(Y_T=Y\times_ST\), to the \(A\)-morphism \(\Phi=\operatorname{pr}_Y\circ\varphi:X_T\to Y\), and a \(T\)-morphism \(X_T\to Y_T\) is determined by its composite with \(\operatorname{pr}_Y\). Moreover, for an affine open \(X_i=\Spec B_i\) of \(X\) we have \((X_i)_T=\Spec(B_i\otimes_AA_{\mathfrak p})=\Spec\Sigma^{-1}B_i\), and for \(b\in B_i\) the principal open \(D(b)\subseteq(X_i)_T\) is the base change of the affine open \(D(b)\subseteq X_i\) (localization commutes with base change).

Lemma M. Let \(G=\Spec F\) with \(F\) an \(A\)-algebra, and \(\Omega\subseteq G\) an open set containing \(G_T:=p(G\times_ST)=\{P\in\Spec F:\ P\cap\Sigma=\emptyset\}\). Then \(G_a=G\times_SD(a)\subseteq\Omega\) for some \(a\in\Sigma\).

Proof. Write \(G\setminus\Omega=V(J)\). If \(J\Sigma^{-1}F\) were a proper ideal, it would lie in a prime of \(\Sigma^{-1}F\), i.e. there would be a prime \(P\supseteq J\) of \(F\) with \(P\cap\Sigma=\emptyset\), i.e. a point of \(V(J)\cap G_T=\emptyset\). So \(1=j/s'\) in \(\Sigma^{-1}F\) with \(j\in J\), \(s'\in\Sigma\). There is \(w\in\Sigma\) with \(w(s'-j)=0\) in \(F\), so \(a:=ws'\in J\cap\Sigma\) (as an element of \(F\)). Then \(V(J)\subseteq V(a)\), i.e. \(D(a)=G_a\subseteq\Omega\). ∎

Lemma U. Let \(X,Y\) be \(A\)-schemes of finite type and \(u,v:X\to Y\) two \(A\)-morphisms with \(u\circ p=v\circ p\) (equivalently \(u_T=v_T\)). Then \(u=v\) on \(X_a\) for some \(a\in\Sigma\).

Proof. Cover \(X\) by finitely many affine opens; if the assertion holds on each with elements \(a_k\), it holds on \(X\) for \(a=\prod a_k\). So let \(X=\Spec F\). Let \(Y=Y_1\cup\dots\cup Y_t\) with \(Y_j=\Spec C_j\) affine open. For \(x\in X_T:=p(X\times_ST)\) we have \(u(x)=v(x)\). Hence \(\Omega:=\bigcup_j\bigl(u^{-1}(Y_j)\cap v^{-1}(Y_j)\bigr)\) is an open set containing \(X_T\). By Lemma M, \(X_{a_1}\subseteq\Omega\) for some \(a_1\in\Sigma\). Cover the affine \(X_{a_1}\) by finitely many principal opens \(D(f_m)=\Spec R_m\) with \(D(f_m)\subseteq u^{-1}(Y_{j(m)})\cap v^{-1}(Y_{j(m)})\). On \(D(f_m)\), \(u\) and \(v\) correspond to \(A\)-algebra homomorphisms \(u^*,v^*:C_{j(m)}\to R_m\) which agree after composing with \(R_m\to\Sigma^{-1}R_m\) (because \(u\circ p=v\circ p\)). Let \(c_1,\dots,c_n\) generate the \(A\)-algebra \(C_{j(m)}\). Each \(u^*(c_l)-v^*(c_l)\) is killed by some \(w_{ml}\in\Sigma\). With \(a=a_1\prod_{m,l}w_{ml}\) we get \(u^*=v^*\) on \(D(f_m)\cap X_a\) for all \(m\), so \(u=v\) on \(X_a\). ∎

Existence of \(f\). As \(X_T\) is quasi-compact, we can cover it by finitely many sets \(D(b_m)\subseteq(X_{i(m)})_T\), \(b_m\in B_{i(m)}\) (so \(D(b_m)\) is the base change of the affine open \(X_m':=D(b_m)\subseteq X_{i(m)}\), with \(\mathcal O(X_m')=:B_m'\) finitely generated over \(A\)), such that \(\Phi(D(b_m))\subseteq Y_{j(m)}\) with \(Y_{j(m)}=\Spec C_{m}\) a fixed finite affine cover of \(Y\). (Take all principal \(D(b)\) contained in some \(\Phi^{-1}(Y_j)\); these cover \(X_T\) since principal opens form a base; then pass to a finite subcover.) Let \(X'=\bigcup_mX_m'\), an open subscheme of \(X\) containing \(p(X_T)\). For each of the finitely many affine opens \(X_i\) of the chosen cover of \(X\), Lemma M (with \(\Omega=X'\cap X_i\)) gives \(a_i\in\Sigma\) with \((X_i)_{a_i}\subseteq X'\). With \(a_0=\prod_ia_i\) we get \(X_{a_0}\subseteq X'\). Replace \(X\) by \(X_{a_0}\) and \(X_m'\) by \(X_m'\cap X_{a_0}=\Spec(B_m')_{a_0}\) (again affine, with ring finitely generated over \(A\)). This changes nothing after base change to \(T\), since \(a_0\) is a unit on \(T\) (so \((X_{a_0})_T=X_T\) and \(\varphi\), \(\Phi\) are unchanged). So we may assume \(X=\bigcup_mX_m'\) with \(X_m'=\Spec B_m'\) affine, \(B_m'\) finitely generated over \(A\), and \(\Phi((X_m')_T)\subseteq Y_{j(m)}\).

On \((X_m')_T=\Spec\Sigma^{-1}B_m'\) the morphism \(\Phi\) is given by an \(A\)-algebra homomorphism \(\bar\psi_m:C_m\to\Sigma^{-1}B'_m\). By Lemma L (in 2.4; \(C_m\) is finitely generated over the Noetherian \(A\)) there are \(a_m\in\Sigma\) and an \(A\)-homomorphism \(\psi_m:C_m\to(B'_m)_{a_m}\) inducing \(\bar\psi_m\). Let \(f_m:(X'_m)_{a_m}\to Y_{j(m)}\subseteq Y\) be \(\Spec\psi_m\); it is an \(A\)-morphism with \(f_m\circ p=\Phi\) on \((X'_m)_T\). Put \(a'=\prod a_m\) and restrict all \(f_m\) to \((X_m')_{a'}\). The overlaps \((X_m')_{a'}\cap(X_{m'}')_{a'}\) are open subschemes of the Noetherian affine \(X_m'\) (its ring is finitely generated over the Noetherian \(A\)), hence finite unions of affine opens \(E_\nu\). On each \(E_\nu\) the two morphisms \(f_m,f_{m'}\) agree after composing with \(p\) (both equal \(\Phi\) there), so by Lemma U they agree on \((E_\nu)_{a_\nu}\) for some \(a_\nu\in\Sigma\). Let \(a_1=a'\prod_\nu a_\nu\) (finite product over all overlaps). Then the \(f_m|_{(X_m')_{a_1}}\) agree on overlaps and glue to an \(A\)-morphism \(f:X_{a_1}\to Y\) with \(f\circ p=\Phi\).

Put \(U=D(a_1)\ni s\). Since \(f\) is an \(A\)-morphism, it maps \(X_{a_1}=X\times_SU\) into the preimage \(Y_{a_1}=Y\times_SU\) of \(U\), giving a morphism of \(S\)-schemes (even of \(U\)-schemes) \(\tilde f:X\times_SU\to Y\times_SU\). Its base change \(\tilde f_T:X_T\to Y_T\) satisfies \(\operatorname{pr}_Y\circ\tilde f_T=f\circ p=\Phi=\operatorname{pr}_Y\circ\varphi\), and both are \(T\)-morphisms, so \(\tilde f_T=\varphi\).

The isomorphism case. Let \(\varphi\) be an isomorphism. Apply the above to \(\varphi\) and to \(\varphi^{-1}\): we get \(\tilde f:X_{a_1}\to Y_{a_1}\) and \(\tilde g:Y_{a_2}\to X_{a_2}\) with \(\tilde f_T=\varphi\), \(\tilde g_T=\varphi^{-1}\). Put \(a_3=a_1a_2\) and restrict both to \(D(a_3)\). Then \(\tilde g\circ\tilde f\) and the identity are \(A\)-morphisms \(X_{a_3}\to X\) whose base changes to \(T\) agree (\(\varphi^{-1}\varphi=\operatorname{id}\)). By Lemma U (applied to the finite type \(A\)-scheme \(X_{a_3}\)) they agree on \(X_{a_3a_4}\) for some \(a_4\in\Sigma\). Similarly \(\tilde f\circ\tilde g=\operatorname{id}\) on \(Y_{a_3a_5}\). On \(U'=D(a_3a_4a_5)\) the restrictions of \(\tilde f,\tilde g\) are mutually inverse isomorphisms \(X\times_SU'\simeq Y\times_SU'\), and their base change to \(T\) is still \(\varphi\). ∎

Exercise 2.6

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of integral schemes. Let \(\xi\) be the generic point of \(X\). We say that \(f\) is birational if \(f^\#_\xi:K(Y)\to K(X)\) is an isomorphism. Let us suppose that \(X\) and \(Y\) are of finite type over a scheme \(S\), and that \(f\) is a morphism of \(S\)-schemes. Show that \(f\) is birational if and only if there exist a non-empty open subset \(U\) of \(X\) and an open subset \(V\) of \(Y\) such that \(f\) induces an isomorphism from \(U\) onto \(V\).

Solution

Convention. The homomorphism \(f^\#_\xi:\mathcal O_{Y,f(\xi)}\to\mathcal O_{X,\xi}=K(X)\) is a map \(K(Y)\to K(X)\) only if \(f(\xi)=\eta\), the generic point of \(Y\), i.e. if \(f\) is dominant. So “birational” means: \(f(\xi)=\eta\) and \(f^\#_\xi:K(Y)=\mathcal O_{Y,\eta}\to K(X)\) is an isomorphism.

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Let \(U\ne\emptyset\) be open in \(X\), \(V\) open in \(Y\) and \(f|_U:U\to V\) an isomorphism. Then \(\xi\in U\) is the generic point of \(U\), and \(V\) is a nonempty open set of \(Y\), so \(\eta\in V\) and \(\eta\) is the generic point of \(V\). The homeomorphism \(f|_U\) sends the generic point \(\xi\) of \(U\) to the generic point \(\eta\) of \(V\). The stalk of the isomorphism \(f|_U\) at \(\xi\) is \(f^\#_\xi:\mathcal O_{V,\eta}=K(Y)\to\mathcal O_{U,\xi}=K(X)\), an isomorphism. (Finite type is not used here.)

(\(\Rightarrow\)) Let \(f(\xi)=\eta\) and \(f^\#_\xi:K(Y)\to K(X)\) be an isomorphism. Choose an affine open \(\Spec A\subseteq S\) and a nonempty affine open \(V_0=\Spec B\subseteq Y\) over it. Since \(\eta\in V_0\) and \(f(\xi)=\eta\), the preimage \(f^{-1}(V_0)\) contains \(\xi\); choose a nonempty affine open \(U_0=\Spec C\subseteq f^{-1}(V_0)\). As \(f\) is an \(S\)-morphism, \(U_0\) lies over \(\Spec A\) and \(C\) is a finitely generated \(A\)-algebra (\(X\) is of finite type over \(S\)). The map \(f^\#:B\to C\) is \(A\)-linear, so \(C\) is generated by finitely many elements \(x_1,\dots,x_m\) as a \(B\)-algebra. Both \(B\) and \(C\) are integral domains with \(K(Y)=\operatorname{Frac}B\), \(K(X)=\operatorname{Frac}C\), and \(f^\#_\xi\) is the extension of \(f^\#:B\to C\). In particular \(B\to C\) is injective, and we identify \(B\subseteq C\subseteq K(X)=K(Y)=\operatorname{Frac}B\) (the last equality is the hypothesis that \(f^\#_\xi\) is bijective). Write \(x_i=b_i/d_i\) with \(b_i,d_i\in B\), \(d_i\neq0\) and put \(d=d_1\cdots d_m\ne0\). Then \(C=B[x_1,\dots,x_m]\subseteq B_d\), and \(B\subseteq C\) gives \(B_d\subseteq C_d\). So \(C_d=B_d\).

Let \(V=D(d)\subseteq V_0\) and \(U=D(f^\#(d))\subseteq U_0\). Both are nonempty (\(d\ne0\) in the domains \(B\subseteq C\)). We have \(f(U)\subseteq V\), and \(f|_U:U=\Spec C_d\to V=\Spec B_d\) is \(\Spec\) of the inclusion \(B_d\to C_d\), which is the identity of \(B_d=C_d\). So \(f|_U:U\to V\) is an isomorphism. ∎

Exercise 2.7

Problem

Let \(k\) be a field and \(\overline k\) the algebraic closure of \(k\). Let \(\overline X,\overline Y\) be algebraic varieties over \(\overline k\), \(\overline f\) a morphism from \(\overline X\) to \(\overline Y\). Show that there exist a finite extension \(K\) of \(k\), algebraic varieties \(X,Y\) over \(K\), and a morphism \(f:X\to Y\), such that \(X_{\overline k}=\overline X\), \(Y_{\overline k}=\overline Y\), and \(\overline f=f\times_K\overline k\).

Solution

Here \(K\) is a finite subextension of \(\overline k/k\) (so \(\overline k\) is also an algebraic closure of \(K\)), \(X_{\overline k}=X\times_K\overline k\), and “\(=\)” means a fixed isomorphism of \(\overline k\)-schemes, compatible with the morphisms. Write \(L=\overline k\) throughout. If a datum is defined over \(K\), its base change to any finite \(K'\supseteq K\) (inside \(L\)) is defined over \(K'\); so we may always enlarge \(K\) finitely many times.

Lemma A. Let \(K\subseteq L\) be fields and \(X\) a \(K\)-scheme, \(p:X_L\to X\) the projection. (i) \(p\) is surjective. (ii) For \(z\in X_L\) over \(x\in X\), \(\mathcal O_{X,x}\to\mathcal O_{X_L,z}\) is injective. (iii) If \(f,g:X\to Y\) are \(K\)-morphisms with \(f_L=g_L\) (\(Y\) a \(K\)-scheme), then \(f=g\).

Proof. (i) The fibre of \(p\) over \(x\) is \(\Spec(k(x)\otimes_KL)\) (Prop. 1.16), and \(k(x)\otimes_KL\ne0\) since \(L\) is a nonzero free \(K\)-module. (ii) Take an affine open \(\Spec A\ni x\) with \(x\leftrightarrow\mathfrak p\) and \(z\leftrightarrow\mathfrak q\subset A\otimes_KL\). The ring \(A\otimes_KL\) is free over \(A\), so \((A\otimes_KL)_{\mathfrak q}\) is a localization of a flat \(A\)-module, hence flat over \(A\), and as an \(A_{\mathfrak p}\)-module it is flat over \(A_{\mathfrak p}\) (the elements of \(A\setminus\mathfrak p\) are units). So \(A_{\mathfrak p}\to(A\otimes L)_{\mathfrak q}\) is a flat local homomorphism, hence faithfully flat, hence injective. (iii) We have \(p_Y\circ f_L=f\circ p_X\), so \(f\circ p_X=g\circ p_X\). By (i), \(f(x)=g(x)\) for all \(x\). For \(t\in\mathcal O_Y(W)\) and \(x\in f^{-1}(W)\), the germs of \(f^\#(t)\) and \(g^\#(t)\) at \(x\) are \(f^\#_x(t_{f(x)})\) and \(g^\#_x(t_{f(x)})\), and they have the same image in \(\mathcal O_{X_L,z}\) (\(z\) over \(x\)) because \(f\circ p_X=g\circ p_X\). By (ii) they coincide. So \(f^\#(t)=g^\#(t)\), and \(f=g\). ∎

Claim 2 (morphisms descend). Let \(X,Y\) be varieties over a finite subextension \(K\) and \(h:X_L\to Y_L\) an \(L\)-morphism. Then there are a finite \(K_2\supseteq K\) in \(L\) and a \(K_2\)-morphism \(f:X_{K_2}\to Y_{K_2}\) with \(f_L=h\).

Proof. Cover \(Y\) by finitely many affine opens \(Y_j=\Spec C_j\), and \(X\) by finitely many affine opens \(X_i=\Spec B_i\). Then \((X_i)_L=\Spec(B_i\otimes_KL)\) is covered by the principal opens \(D(\bar b)\), \(\bar b\in B_i\otimes_KL\), with \(D(\bar b)\subseteq h^{-1}((Y_j)_L)\) for some \(j\). By quasi-compactness finitely many suffice, say \(D(\bar b_m)\) (\(m=1,\dots,M\)) in \((X_{i(m)})_L\) with \(h(D(\bar b_m))\subseteq(Y_{j(m)})_L\). Choose a finite \(K_1\supseteq K\) such that all \(\bar b_m\) lie in \(B_{i(m)}\otimes_KK_1\) (finitely many elements, each a finite sum of tensors); call them \(b_m\). Put \(W_m=D(b_m)\subseteq(X_{i(m)})_{K_1}\), an affine open with \((W_m)_L=D(\bar b_m)\). Since the \((W_m)_L\) cover \(X_L\) and \(p:X_L\to X_{K_1}\) is surjective (Lemma A), the \(W_m\) cover \(X_{K_1}\).

On \((W_m)_L=\Spec(R_m\otimes_{K_1}L)\), where \(R_m=\mathcal O(W_m)\), the morphism \(h\) is given by a \(K\)-algebra homomorphism \(\bar\psi_m:C_{j(m)}\to R_m\otimes_{K_1}L\). Write \(C_{j(m)}=K[T_1,\dots,T_n]/(Q_1,\dots,Q_r)\) and let \(c_l\) be the classes of \(T_l\). Choose a finite \(K_2\supseteq K_1\) such that all \(\bar\psi_m(c_l)\) lie in the subring \(R_m\otimes_{K_1}K_2\) (the inclusion \(R_m\otimes K_2\subseteq R_m\otimes L\) is injective because \(L\) is free over \(K_2\)). Since the \(Q_s\) vanish on the \(\bar\psi_m(c_l)\) in the big ring, they vanish in the subring, so \(\bar\psi_m\) restricts to a \(K\)-algebra homomorphism \(\psi_m:C_{j(m)}\to R_m\otimes_{K_1}K_2\), i.e. to a \(K_2\)-morphism \(f_m:(W_m)_{K_2}\to(Y_{j(m)})_{K_2}\) with \((f_m)_L=h|_{(W_m)_L}\) (two \(L\)-algebra homomorphisms from \(C_{j(m)}\otimes_KL\) agreeing on the \(c_l\otimes1\) agree).

On \((W_m\cap W_{m'})_{K_2}\) the morphisms \(f_m,f_{m'}\) have the same base change to \(L\), namely \(h\) restricted, so they agree by Lemma A(iii). Hence they glue to \(f:X_{K_2}\to Y_{K_2}\). Then \(f_L\) and \(h\) agree on the open cover \(\{(W_m)_L\}\) of \(X_L\), so \(f_L=h\). ∎

Claim 1 (objects descend). For every variety \(\overline X\) over \(L\) there are a finite \(K\) and a variety \(X\) over \(K\) with \(X_L\simeq\overline X\).

Proof. Cover \(\overline X\) by finitely many affine opens \(\overline U_i=\Spec\overline B_i\), \(\overline B_i=L[T_1,\dots,T_{n_i}]/\overline I_i\) with \(\overline I_i\) generated by finitely many polynomials (Hilbert). Choose a finite \(K_1\) containing all coefficients of these generators and of the relevant data below, and let \(U_i=\Spec K_1[T]/I_i\) with \(I_i\) generated by the same polynomials; then \((U_i)_L=\overline U_i\). The set \(\overline U_{ij}=\overline U_i\cap\overline U_j\) is open in the Noetherian space \(\overline U_i\), hence a finite union of principal opens \(D(\bar b_{ijl})\), \(\bar b_{ijl}\in\overline B_i\). Enlarging \(K_1\), all \(\bar b_{ijl}\) come from \(b_{ijl}\in K_1[T]/I_i\). Let \(U_{ij}=\bigcup_lD(b_{ijl})\subseteq U_i\); then \((U_{ij})_L=\overline U_{ij}\) (Lemma A(i): \(p\) is surjective, so the open set \(U_{ij}\) is determined by its preimage). The transition isomorphisms \(\varphi_{ij}:\overline U_{ij}\to\overline U_{ji}\) descend by Claim 2 (applied to the varieties \(U_{ij},U_{ji}\)) to \(K\)-morphisms \(\varphi_{ij}':(U_{ij})_{K}\to(U_{ji})_{K}\) over some finite \(K\supseteq K_1\) (a common one for the finitely many pairs). The relations \(\varphi_{ji}'\circ\varphi'_{ij}=\operatorname{id}\), \(\varphi'_{ij}((U_{ij}\cap U_{ik})_K)=(U_{ji}\cap U_{jk})_K\) and \(\varphi_{ik}'=\varphi'_{jk}\circ\varphi_{ij}'\) on \((U_{ij}\cap U_{ik})_K\) hold after base change to \(L\) (they hold for the \(\varphi_{ij}\), being gluing data of \(\overline X\)). The equalities of morphisms then hold already over \(K\) by Lemma A(iii), and the equality of open sets because an open subset of a \(K\)-scheme is determined by its preimage under the surjection \(p\). Thus the \((U_i)_K\) glue along the \(\varphi'_{ij}\) to a \(K\)-scheme \(X\), covered by finitely many affines of finite type, i.e. a variety. Its base change \(X_L\) is glued from the \((U_i)_L=\overline U_i\) along the \(\varphi_{ij}\), so \(X_L\simeq\overline X\). ∎

Conclusion. By Claim 1 there are \(X_1\) over \(K_1\) and \(Y_1\) over \(K_1'\) with isomorphisms \(\alpha:(X_1)_L\simeq\overline X\), \(\beta:(Y_1)_L\simeq\overline Y\). Let \(K\) be the compositum of \(K_1,K_1'\) (finite over \(k\)), and replace \(X_1,Y_1\) by their base changes \(X,Y\) to \(K\). Then \(h=\beta^{-1}\circ\overline f\circ\alpha:X_L\to Y_L\) is an \(L\)-morphism, and Claim 2 gives, over a finite \(K_2\supseteq K\), a morphism \(f:X_{K_2}\to Y_{K_2}\) with \(f_L=h\). Replacing \(K\) by \(K_2\) and \(X,Y\) by \(X_{K_2},Y_{K_2}\), and identifying \(X_L\) with \(\overline X\) via \(\alpha\) and \(Y_L\) with \(\overline Y\) via \(\beta\), we get \(X_L=\overline X\), \(Y_L=\overline Y\) and \(f_L=\overline f\). ∎

Exercise 2.8

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of schemes. We say that \(f\) has finite fibers if \(f^{-1}(y)\) is a finite set for every \(y\in Y\). We say that \(f\) is quasi-finite if, moreover, \(\mathcal O_{X_y,x}\) is finite over \(k(y)\) for every \(x\in X_y\). Show that a morphism of finite type with finite fibers is quasi-finite. Give an example of a morphism with finite fibers which is not quasi-finite.

Solution

Let \(f\) be of finite type with finite fibers. For \(y\in Y\) the fibre \(X_y\) is an algebraic variety over \(K:=k(y)\) (Remark 2.5) whose underlying space is the finite set \(f^{-1}(y)\). Let \(x\in X_y\) and choose an affine open \(\Spec B\ni x\) of \(X_y\); \(B\) is a finitely generated \(K\)-algebra with finitely many prime ideals, and \(\mathcal O_{X_y,x}=B_{\mathfrak m}\), where \(\mathfrak m\) is the prime of \(x\).

Every prime of \(B\) is maximal. Suppose \(\mathfrak p\) is a non-maximal prime. Then \(B/\mathfrak p\) is a finitely generated \(K\)-domain which is not a field, so it has dimension \(d\ge1\) (a domain integral over \(K\) is a field). By Noether normalization, \(K[T_1,\dots,T_d]\subseteq B/\mathfrak p\) is an integral extension, so \(\Spec(B/\mathfrak p)\to\Spec K[T_1,\dots,T_d]\) is surjective (Prop. 2.5.10). But \(K[T_1,\dots,T_d]\) has infinitely many primes: the ideals \((g)\) with \(g\in K[T_1]\) irreducible are distinct primes of \(K[T_1,\dots,T_d]\), and there are infinitely many irreducible polynomials (if \(g_1,\dots,g_n\) were all of them, any irreducible factor of \(g_1\cdots g_n+1\) would be a new one). So \(B\) would have infinitely many primes, a contradiction.

Hence \(B\) is Noetherian of dimension \(0\), i.e. Artinian (Atiyah–Macdonald, Thm. 8.5), so \(B\simeq\prod_iB_{\mathfrak m_i}\) over its finitely many maximal ideals (ibid., Thm. 8.7). \(B\) has finite length as a \(B\)-module with composition factors \(B/\mathfrak m_i\), each of which is finite over \(K\) by Zariski's lemma (Nullstellensatz). So \(\dim_KB<\infty\). The local ring \(B_{\mathfrak m}\) is one of the direct factors of \(B\), hence \(\dim_KB_{\mathfrak m}<\infty\): \(\mathcal O_{X_y,x}\) is finite over \(k(y)\). So \(f\) is quasi-finite.

Example. Let \(k\) be a field, \(R=k[T]_{(T)}\) and \(f:X=\Spec R\to Y=\Spec k\). The only fibre is \(X\) itself, which has exactly two points (the generic point \((0)\) and the closed point \((T)\)), so \(f\) has finite fibers. For the closed point \(x\), \(\mathcal O_{X_y,x}=R\) is not finite over \(k(y)=k\) (it contains \(k[T]\), of infinite dimension). So \(f\) is not quasi-finite (consistently, \(f\) is not of finite type). ∎

Exercise 2.9

Problem

Let \(X,Y\) be algebraic varieties over a field \(k\), with \(X\) geometrically reduced. Let \(f,g:X\to Y\) be two morphisms that canonically induce the same map \(X(\overline k)\to Y(\overline k)\). We want to show that \(f=g\) as morphisms.

  1. (a)

    Show that \(f(x)=g(x)\) for any closed point \(x\in X\), and then show the same equality for any point \(x\).

  2. (b)

    Reduce to the case \(X\) affine, \(Y=\mathbb A^n_k\), and \(k\) algebraically closed.

  3. (c)

    Using Exercise 2.3.7 and Lemma 2.1.18, show that \(f=g\).

Solution

The hypothesis means \(f\circ u=g\circ u\) for every \(k\)-morphism \(u:\Spec\overline k\to X\) (the map \(X(\overline k)\to Y(\overline k)\) is \(u\mapsto f\circ u\)).

Lemma C. Let \(Z\) be a variety over \(k\). (i) A point \(z\in Z\) is closed iff \([k(z):k]<\infty\). (ii) Every nonempty open subset of \(Z\) contains a closed point of \(Z\).

Proof. (i) If \(z\) is closed, it is closed in an affine open \(\Spec B\ni z\), so it is a maximal ideal and \(k(z)=B/\mathfrak m\) is finite over \(k\) by Zariski's lemma. Conversely let \([k(z):k]<\infty\) and let \(z'\in\overline{\{z\}}\). Take an affine open \(\Spec B'\ni z'\); it contains \(z\) (a neighbourhood of \(z'\) meets \(\{z\}\)). Let \(\mathfrak p\subset B'\) be the prime of \(z\). Then \(B'/\mathfrak p\subseteq k(z)\) is a domain integral over \(k\), hence a field, so \(\mathfrak p\) is maximal and \(z\) is closed in \(\Spec B'\). Thus \(z'\in\overline{\{z\}}\cap\Spec B'=\{z\}\), so \(z'=z\). (ii) A nonempty open set contains a nonempty affine open \(\Spec B'\) with \(B'\neq0\) finitely generated over \(k\); a maximal ideal of \(B'\) gives a point with \(k(z)=B'/\mathfrak m\) finite over \(k\), which is closed by (i). ∎

(a) Let \(x\in X\) be closed. Then \([k(x):k]<\infty\) by Lemma C(i). Choose a \(k\)-embedding \(\tau:k(x)\to\overline k\), and let \(u\) be the composition \(\Spec\overline k\to\Spec k(x)\to X\), a \(\overline k\)-point with image \(x\). Then \(f\circ u=g\circ u\) has image \(f(x)\) and also \(g(x)\). So \(f(x)=g(x)\). (This part does not need \(X\) to be geometrically reduced.)

Now let \(x\in X\) be arbitrary and \(Z=\overline{\{x\}}\), with the reduced structure; it is a variety over \(k\) with generic point \(x\). Closed points of \(Z\) are the points of \(Z\) with finite residue extension, hence closed in \(X\) (Lemma C(i) applied to \(Z\) and to \(X\)), so \(f=g\) on them. For \(W\subseteq Y\) open, \(x\in f^{-1}(W)\) iff \(f^{-1}(W)\cap Z\neq\emptyset\) (a nonempty open subset of the irreducible \(Z\) contains its generic point) iff \(f^{-1}(W)\cap Z\) contains a closed point \(z\) of \(Z\) (Lemma C(ii)) iff \(g^{-1}(W)\cap Z\) contains a closed point of \(Z\) (as \(f(z)=g(z)\)) iff \(x\in g^{-1}(W)\). So \(f(x)\) and \(g(x)\) lie in the same open subsets of \(Y\). A scheme is \(T_0\): if \(y\ne y'\), take an affine open \(\Spec C\) containing one of them. If it misses the other, it separates them. Otherwise \(y,y'\) are distinct primes \(\mathfrak p_y,\mathfrak p_{y'}\) of \(C\), one of which is not contained in the other, say \(\mathfrak p_{y'}\not\subseteq\mathfrak p_y\); for \(c\in\mathfrak p_{y'}\setminus\mathfrak p_y\) the set \(D(c)\) contains \(y\) but not \(y'\). So \(f(x)=g(x)\). Thus \(f\) and \(g\) have the same underlying map.

(b) Reduction to \(k=\overline k\). Put \(L=\overline k\), \(\overline X=X_L\), \(\overline Y=Y_L\), \(\overline f=f_L\), \(\overline g=g_L\). By hypothesis \(\overline X\) is reduced. An \(L\)-point \(v:\Spec L\to\overline X\) corresponds to the \(k\)-point \(u=p_X\circ v\in X(L)\) (Prop. 2.18(a)), and \(p_Y\circ\overline f\circ v=f\circ u\). As \(f\circ u=g\circ u\), we get \(p_Y\circ\overline f\circ v=p_Y\circ\overline g\circ v\), so \(\overline f\circ v=\overline g\circ v\) (two \(L\)-points of \(\overline Y\) with the same image in \(Y\) coincide, again by Prop. 2.18(a)). So \(\overline f,\overline g\) satisfy the hypotheses over the algebraically closed field \(L\), with \(\overline X\) reduced. If we know \(\overline f=\overline g\), then \(f=g\) by Lemma A(iii) of Ex. 2.7.

Reduction to \(X\) affine, \(Y=\mathbb A^n\). Now assume \(k=\overline k\) and \(X\) reduced; by (a), \(f(z)=g(z)\) for all \(z\in X\). Let \(z\in X\), \(y=f(z)=g(z)\). Choose an affine open \(V=\Spec C\ni y\) of \(Y\) and an affine open \(U=\Spec B\ni z\) of \(X\) with \(f(U)\subseteq V\) (hence \(g(U)\subseteq V\), same maps on points). We have \(C=k[T_1,\dots,T_n]/I\), so \(i:V\to\mathbb A^n_k\) is a closed immersion, and \(B\) is a reduced finitely generated \(k\)-algebra. A morphism \(U\to V\) is a ring homomorphism \(C\to B\), and composing with the surjection \(k[T]\to C\) is injective on such homomorphisms. So \(f|_U=g|_U\) iff \(i\circ f|_U=i\circ g|_U\) as morphisms \(U\to\mathbb A^n_k\). Since the \(U\)'s cover \(X\), it suffices to show this.

(c) Conclusion. The morphisms \(i\circ f|_U,i\circ g|_U:U\to\mathbb A^n_k\) correspond to the images \(f_l,g_l\in B\) of \(T_l\) (\(l=1,\dots,n\)). We show \(f_l=g_l\). Let \(\mathfrak m\subset B\) be a maximal ideal and \(x\) the corresponding closed point of \(U\), which is closed in \(X\) (Lemma C). As \(k=\overline k\), \(B/\mathfrak m=k\) (Zariski's lemma), so \(u_x:\Spec k\to U\subseteq X\) is a \(k\)-point with \(f\circ u_x=g\circ u_x\). Apply \(i\): the point \(i\circ f\circ u_x\in\mathbb A^n(k)=k^n\) is the homomorphism \(k[T]\to B\to B/\mathfrak m=k\), \(T_l\mapsto f_l\bmod\mathfrak m\), i.e. it is the point \((f_1(x),\dots,f_n(x))\) (this is Ex. 2.3.7, and is immediate from the correspondence between morphisms into an affine scheme and ring homomorphisms). Likewise \(i\circ g\circ u_x=(g_1(x),\dots,g_n(x))\). Hence \(f_l-g_l\in\mathfrak m\) for every maximal ideal \(\mathfrak m\) of \(B\). For a finitely generated algebra \(B\) over a field, the Jacobson radical equals the nilradical (Nullstellensatz; Lemma 2.1.18). Since \(B\) is reduced, \(f_l-g_l\in\bigcap_{\mathfrak m}\mathfrak m=\Nil(B)=0\). So \(i\circ f|_U=i\circ g|_U\), hence \(f|_U=g|_U\), and so \(\overline f=\overline g\) by gluing over the cover. By (b), \(f=g\) in the original setting. ∎

Exercise 2.10

Problem

Let \(k\) be a field and \(K/k\) a finite Galois extension with group \(G\). Let \(X\) be an algebraic variety over \(k\).

  1. (a)

    Let \(L\) be an extension of \(k\). Show that \(G\) acts transitively on \(\Spec(L\otimes_kK)\).

  2. (b)

    Let us suppose \(X\) is irreducible. Show that \(G\) acts transitively on the irreducible components of \(X_K\). Deduce from this that the irreducible components of \(X_{\overline k}\) have the same dimension.

  3. (c)

    Let us suppose \(X\) is connected. Show that \(G\) acts transitively on the connected components of \(X_K\).

Solution

For \(\sigma\in G\), \(G\) acts on \(\Spec K\) by \(\Spec\sigma\), on \(\Spec(L\otimes_kK)\) by \(\Spec(\operatorname{id}\otimes\sigma)\), and on \(X_K=X\times_k\Spec K\) by \(\operatorname{id}\times\Spec\sigma\); these are automorphisms of schemes (not of \(K\)-schemes), hence homeomorphisms, and they commute with the projection \(p:X_K\to X\).

(a) Let \(R=L\otimes_kK\), \(\sigma_R=\operatorname{id}\otimes\sigma\). Since \(G\) is a group, it is enough to show: for primes \(\mathfrak p,\mathfrak q\) of \(R\) there is \(\sigma\in G\) with \(\sigma_R(\mathfrak p)=\mathfrak q\). \(R\) is finite over \(L\) (\(K/k\) is finite), so \(R/\mathfrak p\) and \(R/\mathfrak q\) are domains that are finite-dimensional over \(L\), hence fields \(\kappa(\mathfrak p),\kappa(\mathfrak q)\), finite over \(L\). We have \(k\)-homomorphisms \(j_{\mathfrak p}:K\to\kappa(\mathfrak p)\), \(j_{\mathfrak q}:K\to\kappa(\mathfrak q)\), \(x\mapsto\overline{1\otimes x}\). Let \(\Omega\) be an algebraic closure of \(\kappa(\mathfrak q)\). As \(\kappa(\mathfrak p)\) is algebraic over \(L\subseteq\Omega\), there is an \(L\)-embedding \(\tau:\kappa(\mathfrak p)\to\Omega\). The two \(k\)-embeddings \(\tau\circ j_{\mathfrak p}\) and \(j_{\mathfrak q}\) of \(K\) in \(\Omega\) have the same image, since \(K/k\) is normal (the image of any \(k\)-embedding is the set of roots in \(\Omega\) of the minimal polynomials of the elements of \(K\), all of which split in \(K\)). So \(\sigma:=j_{\mathfrak q}^{-1}\circ\tau\circ j_{\mathfrak p}\) is a well defined \(k\)-automorphism of \(K\), i.e. \(\sigma\in G\), with \(\tau\circ j_{\mathfrak p}=j_{\mathfrak q}\circ\sigma\). Consider the ring homomorphisms \(\alpha,\beta:R\to\Omega\) given by \(\alpha(l\otimes x)=l\cdot\tau j_{\mathfrak p}(x)\) and \(\beta(l\otimes x)=l\cdot j_{\mathfrak q}(x)\). Then \(\ker\alpha=\mathfrak p\) (it is \(R\to\kappa(\mathfrak p)\) followed by the injective \(\tau\)) and \(\ker\beta=\mathfrak q\). Moreover \(\alpha(l\otimes x)=l\,j_{\mathfrak q}(\sigma x)=\beta(l\otimes\sigma x)\), i.e. \(\alpha=\beta\circ\sigma_R\), so \(\mathfrak p=\sigma_R^{-1}(\mathfrak q)\). Hence \(G\) acts transitively.

Lemma D. Let \(X\) be a variety over \(k\), \(L/k\) an algebraic extension and \(p:X_L\to X\) the projection. Let \(c\in X_L\) be the generic point of an irreducible component \(C\) of \(X_L\), and \(x=p(c)\). Then \(x\) is the generic point of an irreducible component \(D\) of \(X\), and \(\dim C=\dim D\).

Proof. The ring \(\mathcal O_{X_L,c}\) has a unique prime ideal (as \(c\) has no proper generization). The homomorphism \(\varphi:\mathcal O_{X,x}\to\mathcal O_{X_L,c}\) is flat and local (as in Lemma A(ii) of Ex. 2.7). Suppose \(x\) had a proper generization, i.e. \(\mathcal O_{X,x}\) had a prime \(\mathcal Q\subsetneq\mathfrak m_x\). Going-down for the flat \(\varphi\) gives a prime \(\mathfrak q\subseteq\mathfrak m_c\) with \(\varphi^{-1}(\mathfrak q)=\mathcal Q\neq\mathfrak m_x=\varphi^{-1}(\mathfrak m_c)\), so \(\mathfrak q\ne\mathfrak m_c\), contradicting uniqueness of the prime. So \(x\) is the generic point of \(D=\overline{\{x\}}\), an irreducible component. For dimensions, let \(\Spec A\ni x\) be an affine open; then \(c\in\Spec(A\otimes_kL)\), corresponding to a prime \(\mathfrak q\) with \(\mathfrak q\cap A=\mathfrak p_x\), and \(C\cap\Spec(A\otimes L)=V(\mathfrak q)\), \(D\cap\Spec A=V(\mathfrak p_x)\). The extension \(A/\mathfrak p_x\to(A\otimes_kL)/\mathfrak q\) is injective and integral (\(L/k\) algebraic), so the two rings have the same dimension (Prop. 2.5.10). The affine opens \(\Spec A\ni x\) cover \(D\) (they are those meeting \(D\)), and the corresponding \(\Spec(A\otimes L)\) cover \(C\) (these are exactly the ones containing \(c\), i.e. with \(x\in\Spec A\)). By the Fact in Ex. 2.1, \(\dim C\) and \(\dim D\) are the suprema of the equal numbers \(\dim V(\mathfrak q)\), \(\dim V(\mathfrak p_x)\). ∎

(b) Let \(X\) be irreducible with generic point \(\eta\). By Lemma D, every irreducible component of \(X_K\) has its generic point in \(F:=p^{-1}(\eta)\) (as \(p(c)\) is the generic point of a component of \(X\), hence \(\eta\)). Conversely, every point \(c'\in F\) is the generic point of an irreducible component of \(X_K\): \(F\) is homeomorphic to \(\Spec(k(\eta)\otimes_kK)\) (Prop. 1.16), and this ring is finite over the field \(k(\eta)\), hence Artinian, so every point of \(F\) is closed in \(F\). A generization \(c''\) of \(c'\) in \(X_K\) satisfies \(p(c'')\rightsquigarrow\eta\), so \(p(c'')=\eta\) and \(c''\in F\); hence \(c''=c'\). So \(c'\) has no proper generization, and the generic point of an irreducible component of \(X_K\) containing \(c'\) equals \(c'\). Therefore the generic points of the irreducible components of \(X_K\) are exactly the points of \(F\), i.e. of \(\Spec(k(\eta)\otimes_kK)\). The action of \(G\) on \(X_K\) preserves \(F\) and restricts to the action \(\Spec(\operatorname{id}\otimes\sigma)\) on \(\Spec(k(\eta)\otimes_kK)\). By (a) with \(L=k(\eta)\), \(G\) acts transitively on \(F\), hence on the irreducible components of \(X_K\) (they are the closures of the points of \(F\), and \(G\) acts by homeomorphisms).

Consequences for dimensions. Since each \(\sigma\in G\) is a homeomorphism of \(X_K\), it preserves dimension of closed subsets; by transitivity all irreducible components of \(X_K\) have the same dimension. For \(\overline k\) we argue directly (as \(\overline k/k\) may be inseparable, it is not a finite Galois extension; the same proof applies to any algebraic extension). Let \(C\) be an irreducible component of \(X_{\overline k}\). By Lemma D (with \(L=\overline k\)), \(\dim C=\dim D\), where \(D\) is an irreducible component of \(X\); since \(X\) is irreducible, \(D=X\). Hence every irreducible component of \(X_{\overline k}\) has dimension \(\dim X\), in particular they all have the same dimension.

(c) Let \(X\) be connected. The space \(X_K\) is Noetherian (Ex. 2.1, \(K/k\) finite, \(X_K\) of finite type over \(K\)), so it has finitely many connected components, each open and closed. Let \(C\) be one of them and \(C'=\bigcup_{\sigma\in G}\sigma(C)\), \(C''=X_K\setminus C'\). Both are open and closed in \(X_K\) (unions of connected components), and \(G\)-stable.
