The map \(p\) is closed: over an affine open \(\Spec A\subseteq X\) it is \(\Spec(A\otimes_kK)\to\Spec A\), induced by the injective integral homomorphism \(A\to A\otimes_kK\), and such maps are closed (the image of \(V(I)\) is \(V(I\cap A)\), by Prop. 2.5.10 applied to \(A/(I\cap A)\to(A\otimes K)/I\)); closedness is local on the target. Moreover \(p\) is surjective (Lemma A(i) of Ex. 2.7). So \(X=p(C')\cup p(C'')\) with both sets closed. They are disjoint: if \(z=p(y')=p(y'')\) with \(y'\in C'\), \(y''\in C''\), then \(y',y''\) lie in the fibre \(p^{-1}(z)\simeq\Spec(k(z)\otimes_kK)\), on which \(G\) acts transitively by (a). So \(y''=\sigma y'\) for some \(\sigma\), and then \(y''\in C'\) by \(G\)-stability of \(C'\), a contradiction. As \(X\) is connected and \(p(C')\ne\emptyset\), we get \(p(C'')=\emptyset\), i.e. \(C''=\emptyset\) and \(C'=X_K\). So every connected component of \(X_K\) is contained in \(\bigcup_\sigma\sigma(C)\), where each \(\sigma(C)\) is a connected component (images of connected components under homeomorphisms). Distinct connected components are disjoint, so every connected component equals some \(\sigma(C)\), which is the claimed transitivity. ∎

Exercise 2.11

Problem

Let \(X\) be an algebraic variety over a field \(k\).

  1. (a)

    Show that if \(X\) is connected and if \(X(k)\neq\emptyset\), then \(X\) is geometrically connected.

  2. (b)

    Show through an example that (a) is false if we replace “connected” by “irreducible”.

Solution

Write \(p_K:X_K\to X\) for the projection, for a field extension \(K/k\). Two general facts are used.

(F1) \(p_K\) is surjective. Indeed, for \(z\in X\) the fibre \(p_K^{-1}(z)\) is homeomorphic to \((X_K)_z=\operatorname{Spec}k(z)\times_k\operatorname{Spec}K=\operatorname{Spec}(k(z)\otimes_kK)\) (Prop. 1.16), which is non-empty because the tensor product of two non-zero vector spaces over a field is non-zero. If \(k(z)=k\), this fibre is \(\operatorname{Spec}K\), a single point.

(F2) Descent of closed sets. Let \(C\) be a closed subset of \(X_{\overline k}\). Then there are a finite extension \(K'/k\) inside \(\overline k\) and a closed subset \(Z\subseteq X_{K'}\) with \(C=q^{-1}(Z)\), where \(q:X_{\overline k}\to X_{K'}\) is the projection; and the same holds, with \(Z\) replaced by its preimage in \(X_{K''}\), for every finite \(K''\supseteq K'\). Indeed, give \(C\) its reduced structure and apply Lemma 2.6 to \(K=\overline k\): there are a finite \(K'\) and a (reduced) closed subvariety \(Z\) of \(X_{K'}\) with \(Z_{\overline k}=C\). The underlying set of \(Z_{\overline k}=Z\times_{X_{K'}}X_{\overline k}\) is \(q^{-1}(Z)\). For \(K''\supseteq K'\) finite, \(q=q'\circ q''\) with \(q'':X_{\overline k}\to X_{K''}\), \(q':X_{K''}\to X_{K'}\), so \(C=q''^{-1}(q'^{-1}(Z))\).

(a) By definition we must show that \(X_{\overline k}\) is connected. We proceed in two steps.

Step 1: \(X_{K'}\) is connected for every finite extension \(K'/k\). Let \(n=[K':k]\) and \(Y=X_{K'}\), \(p=p_{K'}\). Fix \(x\in X(k)\). By Prop. 2.18(b), \(x\) is a point of \(X\) with \(k(x)=k\), so by (F1) the fibre of \(p\) over \(x\) is a single point and \(k(x)\otimes_kK'=K'\).

The morphism \(p\) is affine: over an affine open \(U=\operatorname{Spec}A\) of \(X\) we have \(p^{-1}(U)=\operatorname{Spec}(A\otimes_kK')\). Hence \(\mathcal B:=p_*\mathcal O_Y=\mathcal O_X\otimes_kK'\) is a locally free \(\mathcal O_X\)-module of rank \(n\) (choose a \(k\)-basis of \(K'\)), and \(\mathcal O_Y(Y)=\mathcal B(X)\).

Let \(e\in\mathcal O_Y(Y)\) be an idempotent. Multiplication by \(e\) and by \(1-e\) are \(\mathcal O_X\)-linear, so \(\mathcal B=\mathcal M\oplus\mathcal N\) with \(\mathcal M=e\mathcal B\), \(\mathcal N=(1-e)\mathcal B\). For an affine open \(U=\operatorname{Spec}A\) the modules \(\mathcal M(U)\), \(\mathcal N(U)\) are direct summands of the free \(A\)-module \(A\otimes_kK'\simeq A^n\), hence finitely generated projective. The rank function of a finitely generated projective module is locally constant on \(\operatorname{Spec}A\); its value at \(z\) is \(r_{\mathcal M}(z)=\dim_{k(z)}\mathcal M\otimes k(z)\). So \(r_{\mathcal M}:X\to\{0,\dots,n\}\) is locally constant, hence constant, equal to some \(r\), because \(X\) is connected. Likewise \(r_{\mathcal N}=n-r\) is constant.

Now compute at \(x\): \(\mathcal B\otimes k(x)=K'\), and \(\mathcal M\otimes k(x)=\bar e\,K'\) where \(\bar e\in K'\) is the image of \(e\), an idempotent of the field \(K'\), hence \(0\) or \(1\). So \(r\in\{0,n\}\). If \(r=0\), then all stalks of \(\mathcal M\) are free of rank \(0\), i.e. \(\mathcal M=0\), so \(e=e\cdot e\in\mathcal M(X)\) is \(0\). If \(r=n\), then \(\mathcal N=0\) and \(1-e=0\). Thus \(\mathcal O_Y(Y)\) has no idempotents other than \(0,1\). Since \(Y\neq\emptyset\) and a decomposition \(Y=U\sqcup V\) into open and closed sets would give the idempotent \(1_U\), \(Y\) is connected.

Step 2: \(X_{\overline k}\) is connected. Suppose \(X_{\overline k}=C_1\sqcup C_2\) with \(C_1,C_2\) non-empty, closed (hence both open and closed). By (F2), applied to \(C_1,C_2\) and a common finite extension \(K'\) (enlarge one of the two), there are closed sets \(Z_1,Z_2\subseteq X_{K'}\) with \(C_i=q^{-1}(Z_i)\). Since \(q\) is surjective (F1 for \(X_{K'}\) over \(K'\), or directly), \(Z_1\cup Z_2=q(C_1\cup C_2)=X_{K'}\) and \(Z_1\cap Z_2=\emptyset\) (a point of \(Z_1\cap Z_2\) would have a preimage in \(C_1\cap C_2=\emptyset\)); also \(Z_i\neq\emptyset\) since \(C_i\neq\emptyset\). So \(X_{K'}\) is disconnected, contradicting Step 1. Hence \(X_{\overline k}\) is connected, i.e. \(X\) is geometrically connected.

(b) Let \(k=\mathbf R\) and \(X=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbf R[x,y]/(x^2+y^2)\). The polynomial \(x^2+y^2\) is irreducible in \(\mathbf R[x,y]\): otherwise it would be a product of two real linear forms and its real zero set would contain a real line, whereas \(x^2+y^2>0\) for \((x,y)\neq(0,0)\) in \(\mathbf R^2\). As \(\mathbf R[x,y]\) is factorial, \((x^2+y^2)\) is prime, so \(X\) is an integral (in particular irreducible) variety, and \((0,0)\in X(\mathbf R)\). But over \(\mathbf C\) we have \(x^2+y^2=(x+iy)(x-iy)\), so \(X_{\mathbf C}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbf C[x,y]/((x+iy)(x-iy))\) is the union of the two distinct lines \(V(x+iy)\) and \(V(x-iy)\), hence reducible. So \(X\) is irreducible with a rational point but not geometrically irreducible. (By (a), \(X\) is nevertheless geometrically connected: the two lines meet at the origin.) ∎

Exercise 2.12

Problem

Let \(X\) be an irreducible algebraic variety over a field \(k\). We call the irreducible components of \(X_{\overline k}\) the geometric irreducible components of \(X\). Let us assume \(X\) is integral. Show that the number of geometric irreducible components of \(X\) is equal to \([K(X)\cap k^s:k]\), where \(k^s\) is the separable closure of \(k\).

Solution

Meaning of the statement. Put \(L=K(X)\) and let \(k_1\subseteq L\) be the set of elements of \(L\) that are algebraic and separable over \(k\); this is a subfield (the separable closure of \(k\) in \(L\)), and it is what \(K(X)\cap k^s\) denotes (compare the proof of Cor. 2.14(d)). We show that \([k_1:k]\) is finite and that it equals the number of irreducible components of \(X_{\overline k}\).

Step 0: \(m:=[k_1:k]<\infty\). Let \(\operatorname{Spec}A\subseteq X\) be a non-empty affine open set, so \(L=\operatorname{Frac}A\). By Noether normalization \(L\) is finite over \(F=k(T_1,\dots,T_d)\). For a finite subextension \(k'\subseteq k_1\) of \(k\), the ring \(k'\otimes_kF\) is a localization of \(k'[T_1,\dots,T_d]\), hence a domain, and it is finite over \(F\) of dimension \([k':k]\), hence a field; it maps injectively to \(L\) (a ring homomorphism from a field), so \([k':k]\le[L:F]\). Thus \([k_1:k]\le[L:F]\).

Step 1: \(L\otimes_kk^s\) is a product of exactly \(m\) fields. Since \(k_1/k\) is finite separable, \(k_1=k(\theta)\) with minimal polynomial \(f\) of degree \(m\), and \(f=\prod_{i=1}^m(T-r_i)\) with distinct \(r_i\in k^s\). By the Chinese remainder theorem

\[ k_1\otimes_kk^s\simeq k^s[T]/(f)\simeq\prod_{i=1}^m k^s_{(i)}, \]

where \(k^s_{(i)}\) is \(k^s\) regarded as a \(k_1\)-algebra via \(\theta\mapsto r_i\). Hence

\[ L\otimes_kk^s\simeq L\otimes_{k_1}(k_1\otimes_kk^s)\simeq\prod_{i=1}^mL\otimes_{k_1}k^s_{(i)}. \]

Claim: if \(\Omega\) is a field extension of \(k_1\) which is algebraic and separable over \(k_1\), then \(L\otimes_{k_1}\Omega\) is a field. (Apply it to \(\Omega=k^s_{(i)}\), which is separable algebraic over the image of \(k_1\) since \(k^s/k\) is separable.) Proof of the claim. First let \(\Omega\) be finite over \(k_1\), say \(\Omega=k_1[T]/(P)\) with \(P\) monic, irreducible and separable over \(k_1\). Then \(L\otimes_{k_1}\Omega=L[T]/(P)\). Let \(Q\) be a monic divisor of \(P\) in \(L[T]\). In a splitting field of \(P\) over \(L\) we have \(P=\prod(T-r_j)\) with distinct \(r_j\), separable algebraic over \(k_1\), and \(Q=\prod_{j\in J}(T-r_j)\) for some subset \(J\). The coefficients of \(Q\) lie in \(L\) and are polynomials in the \(r_j\), hence are separable algebraic over \(k_1\), hence over \(k\). By definition of \(k_1\) they lie in \(k_1\). So \(Q\in k_1[T]\) divides the irreducible polynomial \(P\) of \(k_1[T]\), and \(Q=1\) or \(Q=P\). Hence \(P\) is irreducible in \(L[T]\) and \(L[T]/(P)\) is a field. For general \(\Omega\), write \(\Omega\) as the directed union of its finite subextensions \(\Omega_\alpha/k_1\); then \(L\otimes_{k_1}\Omega\) is the directed union of the subrings \(L\otimes_{k_1}\Omega_\alpha\) (tensoring inclusions of vector spaces over a field preserves injectivity), which are fields; a directed union of fields is a field. This proves the claim, and Step 1.

Step 2: two facts on localization. Let \(D\) be a domain which is a \(k\)-algebra, \(S=D\smallsetminus\{0\}\), \(E\) any \(k\)-algebra. Every \(s\in S\) acts injectively on \(E\otimes_kD\) (multiplication by \(s\) is an injective \(k\)-linear map of \(D\), and \(E\otimes_k-\) preserves injectivity). Hence \(E\otimes_kD\subseteq S^{-1}(E\otimes_kD)=E\otimes_k\operatorname{Frac}D\). Moreover, in any ring \(R\) with a multiplicative set \(S\) of non-zero-divisors, the minimal primes of \(R\) do not meet \(S\) (an element of a minimal prime \(\mathfrak q\) is a zero divisor, because \(R_{\mathfrak q}\) has a nilpotent maximal ideal), and so \(\mathfrak q\mapsto S^{-1}\mathfrak q\) is a bijection from the minimal primes of \(R\) onto the minimal primes of \(S^{-1}R\) (primes of \(S^{-1}R\) correspond order-preservingly to primes of \(R\) missing \(S\), and a prime missing \(S\) which contains a smaller prime also contains it missing \(S\)).

Step 3: the components of \(X_{k^s}\). Let \(K=k^s\) and \(p:X_K\to X\). Let \(\eta\) be the generic point of \(X\) and \(\operatorname{Spec}A\) a non-empty affine open of \(X\) (\(A\) a domain with fraction field \(L\)). By Step 2 (with \(D=A\), \(E=K\)), \(A\otimes_kK\subseteq L\otimes_kK\) is the localization at \(A\smallsetminus\{0\}\), and the minimal primes of \(A\otimes K\) correspond to those of \(L\otimes_kK\). By Step 1 the latter is a finite product of \(m\) fields, so it has exactly \(m\) primes, all minimal. Hence \(\operatorname{Spec}(A\otimes K)=p^{-1}(\operatorname{Spec}A)\) has exactly \(m\) irreducible components, and their generic points are the \(m\) primes \(w_1,\dots,w_m\) of \(A\otimes K\) missing \(A\smallsetminus\{0\}\), i.e. the points of \(X_K\) lying over \(\eta\). These \(m\) points form the set \(p^{-1}(\eta)\), which does not depend on the affine open chosen. For every non-empty affine open \(U\), the \(\overline{\{w_j\}}\cap p^{-1}(U)\) are the \(m\) irreducible components of \(p^{-1}(U)\), in particular they are pairwise non-comparable, and they cover \(p^{-1}(U)\). Since the \(p^{-1}(U)\) cover \(X_K\), we get \(X_K=\bigcup_j\overline{\{w_j\}}\) with no inclusions among the \(\overline{\{w_j\}}\) (an inclusion would persist after intersecting with some \(p^{-1}(U)\), where all \(w_j\) lie). Thus \(X_{k^s}\) has exactly \(m\) irreducible components.

Step 4: from \(k^s\) to \(\overline k\). \(\overline k/k^s\) is purely inseparable. Apply Prop. 2.7(c) to the variety \(X_{k^s}\) over \(k^s\): the projection \(X_{\overline k}=(X_{k^s})_{\overline k}\to X_{k^s}\) is a homeomorphism. Hence \(X_{\overline k}\) also has exactly \(m=[K(X)\cap k^s:k]\) irreducible components. ∎

Exercise 2.13

Problem

Let \(X\) be an algebraic variety over a field \(k\). Show that \(X\) is geometrically reduced (resp. irreducible; connected) if and only if \(X_{k'}\) is reduced (resp. irreducible; connected) for every finite extension \(k'\) of \(k\).

Solution

We take all finite extensions \(k'\) inside \(\overline k\). Let \(q_{k'}:X_{\overline k}\to X_{k'}\) be the projection; it is surjective (fibres are \(\operatorname{Spec}(\kappa\otimes_{k'}\overline k)\neq\emptyset\), Prop. 1.16).

“Only if”. Reduced: for an affine open \(\operatorname{Spec}A\) of \(X\), \(A\otimes_kk'\subseteq A\otimes_k\overline k\) (inclusion of vector spaces tensored with \(A\)), and a subring of a reduced ring is reduced. Irreducible, connected: \(X_{k'}\) is the continuous image of \(X_{\overline k}\) under \(q_{k'}\), and a continuous image of an irreducible (resp. connected) space is irreducible (resp. connected).

“If”. Reduced. Let \(\operatorname{Spec}A\) be an affine open of \(X\) and \(a\in A\otimes_k\overline k\) nilpotent. Then \(a\) lies in \(A\otimes_kk'\) for some finite \(k'\) (it is a finite sum of tensors), and it is nilpotent there, hence \(0\). So \(X_{\overline k}\) is covered by the reduced schemes \((\operatorname{Spec}A)_{\overline k}\).

Irreducible. Suppose \(X_{\overline k}=C_1\cup C_2\) with \(C_i\) closed and \(C_i\neq X_{\overline k}\). By (F2) of Exercise 2.11 (a consequence of Lemma 2.6, proved there) there are a common finite extension \(k'\) and closed sets \(Z_i\subseteq X_{k'}\) with \(C_i=q_{k'}^{-1}(Z_i)\). Since \(q_{k'}\) is surjective, \(Z_1\cup Z_2=X_{k'}\), and \(Z_i\neq X_{k'}\) because \(C_i\neq X_{\overline k}\). Thus \(X_{k'}\) is reducible, a contradiction. (Also \(X_{\overline k}\neq\emptyset\) since \(X\neq\emptyset\) in this case.)

Connected. Suppose \(X_{\overline k}=C_1\sqcup C_2\) with \(C_i\) non-empty and closed. As above get a finite \(k'\) and closed \(Z_i\subseteq X_{k'}\) with \(C_i=q_{k'}^{-1}(Z_i)\). Then \(Z_1\cup Z_2=X_{k'}\), \(Z_1\cap Z_2=\emptyset\) (a point in the intersection would have a preimage in \(C_1\cap C_2\)), and \(Z_i\neq\emptyset\). So \(X_{k'}\) is disconnected, a contradiction. ∎

Exercise 2.14

Problem

Let \(X\) be an algebraic variety over a field \(k\). Let us suppose \(X\) is geometrically reduced (resp. integral; irreducible; connected). We want to show that for any field extension \(K/k\), \(X_K\) is reduced (resp. integral; irreducible; connected).

  1. (a)

    Show that we may assume that \(k\) is algebraically closed and \(K\) finitely generated over \(k\).

  2. (b)

    Let \(Y\) be an integral algebraic variety over \(k\) such that \(K(Y)=K\). Applying Proposition 2.15 to \(Y\), show that \(X_K\) is reduced.

  3. (c)

    Let \(P(S)\) be a monic polynomial in \(k(T_1,\dots,T_d)[S]\). Let \(P=Q_1Q_2\) be a decomposition of \(P\) in \(K(T_1,\dots,T_d)[S]\) in monic polynomials. Show that \(Q_i\in k(T_1,\dots,T_d)[S]\) (consider the sub-\(k\)-algebra \(A\) of \(K\) generated by the elements of \(K\) which appear in the coefficients of the \(Q_i\), and apply a homomorphism \(A\to k\) to \(Q_i\)).

  4. (d)

    Let us suppose \(X\) is integral. Applying Proposition 2.15 to \(X\), show that \(K(X)\otimes_kK\) is integral. Deduce from this that \(X_K\) is integral.

  5. (e)

    Show assertion (d) for irreducible algebraic varieties and for connected algebraic varieties.

Solution

Remark on the plan. I follow the ingredients of the outline (Prop. 2.15 and the specialization argument of (c)), but in an order that avoids reducing to \(K\) finitely generated: the specialization argument is applied directly to an arbitrary \(K\) over an algebraically closed \(k\), so the reduction (a) only needs “\(k\) algebraically closed”. Throughout, “\(P\)” (the property) stands for one of: reduced, integral, irreducible, connected. Two simple facts:

(N) If \(N\) is a nil ideal of a \(k\)-algebra \(B\) and \(E\) is any \(k\)-algebra, then \(E\otimes_kN\to E\otimes_kB\) has image consisting of nilpotent elements (a sum of tensors \(e\otimes n\), each nilpotent, of commuting nilpotents), so \(\operatorname{Spec}(E\otimes B/N)\to\operatorname{Spec}(E\otimes B)\) is a homeomorphism. Hence \((X_{\operatorname{red}})_K\to X_K\) is a homeomorphism for every \(K/k\) (apply to an affine cover).

(S) For every extension \(K/k\), \(p_K:X_K\to X\) is surjective (Prop. 1.16, as in Exercise 2.11), and \(A\otimes_kK\subseteq A\otimes_k\Omega\) for \(K\subseteq\Omega\).

(a) Reduction to \(k\) algebraically closed. Let \(K/k\) be arbitrary, and let \(\Omega\) be an algebraically closed extension of \(K\). The algebraic closure of \(k\) in \(\Omega\) is an algebraic closure \(\overline k\) of \(k\), and \(X_\Omega=(X_{\overline k})_\Omega\). The variety \(Y:=X_{\overline k}\) over \(\overline k\) has property \(P\) by hypothesis. Suppose we know the theorem for algebraically closed base fields; then \(Y_\Omega=X_\Omega\) has \(P\). Now \(X_\Omega\to X_K\) is surjective, so \(X_K\) is irreducible, resp. connected, if \(X_\Omega\) is; and \(A\otimes_kK\subseteq A\otimes_k\Omega\) shows that \(X_K\) is reduced if \(X_\Omega\) is. Integral means reduced and irreducible. So it suffices to treat algebraically closed \(k\) and arbitrary \(K/k\). (The reduction to \(K\) finitely generated is not needed.) From now on \(k\) is algebraically closed. Then “geometrically \(P\)” just means \(P\).

(c) Key lemma. Let \(F=k(T_1,\dots,T_d)\) with \(k\) algebraically closed, \(K/k\) any extension, and \(P\in F[S]\) monic and irreducible over \(F\). Then \(P\) is irreducible in \(K(T_1,\dots,T_d)[S]\).

Proof. Suppose \(P=Q_1Q_2\) in \(K(T)[S]\) with \(Q_i\) monic of degree \(n_i\ge1\). Choose \(u\in K[T]\smallsetminus\{0\}\) such that all coefficients of \(Q_1,Q_2\) lie in \(K[T][1/u]\), and \(d_0\in k[T]\smallsetminus\{0\}\) such that the coefficients of \(P\) lie in \(k[T][1/d_0]\). Let \(A\subseteq K\) be the sub-\(k\)-algebra generated by all coefficients (in \(K\)) of \(u\) and of the numerators of the coefficients of the \(Q_i\) (written over the denominator \(u\)). It is a finitely generated \(k\)-algebra and a domain. Let \(\beta\ne0\) be one coefficient of \(u\) and replace \(A\) by \(A_\beta\subseteq K\). Choose a maximal ideal \(\mathfrak m\) of \(A_\beta\). By the Nullstellensatz \(A_\beta/\mathfrak m\) is a finite extension of the algebraically closed field \(k\), so it is \(k\), giving a \(k\)-algebra homomorphism \(\varphi:A_\beta\to k\). Extend it coefficientwise to \(\tilde\varphi:A_\beta[T]\to k[T]\). It is the identity on \(k[T]\), and \(\tilde\varphi(u)\ne0\) because \(\beta\) is a unit of \(A_\beta\), so \(\varphi(\beta)\neq0\). Hence \(\tilde\varphi\) extends to a ring homomorphism \(R:=A_\beta[T][1/(ud_0)]\to k(T)\), which is the identity on \(k[T][1/d_0]\), in particular fixes the coefficients of \(P\). All coefficients of \(P,Q_1,Q_2\) lie in \(R\). Applying \(\tilde\varphi\) to \(P=Q_1Q_2\) in \(R[S]\) gives \(P=\tilde\varphi(Q_1)\tilde\varphi(Q_2)\) in \(F[S]\) with monic factors of degrees \(n_1,n_2\ge1\), contradicting the irreducibility of \(P\) over \(F\). \(\square\)

(In the form stated in (c): if \(P=Q_1Q_2\) with \(P\) irreducible over \(F\), then \(Q_1\) or \(Q_2\) equals \(1\), so both lie in \(F[S]\). For general monic \(P\), factor \(P\) into irreducibles \(P_j\) over \(F\); each \(P_j\) stays irreducible over \(K(T)\), so by unique factorization the monic factors \(Q_i\) are products of some of the \(P_j\) and lie in \(F[S]\).)

Domain statement. Let \(k\) be algebraically closed, \(L\) a finitely generated field extension of \(k\) (the function field of an integral variety \(Y\) over \(k\)) and \(K/k\) any extension. Then \(L\otimes_kK\) is a domain.

Proof. Since \(Y\) is integral and geometrically reduced (\(k=\overline k\)), Prop. 2.15 gives \(L=F[S]/(P)\) with \(F=k(T_1,\dots,T_d)\) and \(P\in F[S]\) monic, irreducible, separable. Then \(L\otimes_kK=(F\otimes_kK)[S]/(P)\). The ring \(F\otimes_kK\) is the localization of \(k[T]\otimes_kK=K[T]\) at \(k[T]\smallsetminus\{0\}\), so it is a domain contained in \(K(T)\). Since \(P\) is monic, division with remainder by \(P\) shows \((F\otimes K)[S]/(P)\hookrightarrow K(T)[S]/(P)\) (if \(g\in(F\otimes K)[S]\) is divisible by \(P\) in \(K(T)[S]\), the quotient lies in \((F\otimes K)[S]\)). By the key lemma \(P\) is irreducible in \(K(T)[S]\), so \(K(T)[S]/(P)\) is a field, and \(L\otimes_kK\) is a subring of a field. \(\square\)

(b) Reduced. Let \(X\) be reduced over \(k=\overline k\), \(K/k\) arbitrary, \(\operatorname{Spec}A\) an affine open of \(X\). As \(A\) is reduced and Noetherian, with minimal primes \(\mathfrak p_1,\dots,\mathfrak p_r\), we have \(A\hookrightarrow\prod_iA/\mathfrak p_i\hookrightarrow\prod_iL_i\), \(L_i=\operatorname{Frac}(A/\mathfrak p_i)\), the function field of the integral variety \(\operatorname{Spec}A/\mathfrak p_i\). Tensoring with \(K\) (exact over \(k\)) gives \(A\otimes_kK\hookrightarrow\prod_iL_i\otimes_kK\), and each \(L_i\otimes_kK\) is a domain by the domain statement. So \(A\otimes_kK\) is reduced, and \(X_K\) is reduced. (In the language of the outline: this is (b) with \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}A/\mathfrak p_i\).)

(d) Integral. Let \(X\) be integral over \(k=\overline k\), \(L=K(X)\). For each non-empty affine open \(U=\operatorname{Spec}A\) of \(X\) we have \(A\otimes_kK\subseteq L\otimes_kK\) and the latter is a domain, so \(A\otimes_kK\) is a domain: \(U_K=p_K^{-1}(U)\) is integral (non-empty by (S)). For two non-empty affine opens \(U,U'\) of \(X\) the intersection \(U\cap U'\) is non-empty, hence \(p_K^{-1}(U\cap U')\neq\emptyset\) by (S). A space covered by irreducible open subsets that pairwise intersect is irreducible. So \(X_K\) is irreducible and reduced, i.e. integral. By (a), this proves the integral case for arbitrary \(k\) (note that in the general case the property used is that \(X_{\overline k}\) is integral).

(e) Irreducible. Let \(k=\overline k\) and \(X\) irreducible. Then \(X_{\operatorname{red}}\) is integral over \(k\), so \((X_{\operatorname{red}})_K\) is integral by (d), in particular irreducible. By (N) \((X_{\operatorname{red}})_K\to X_K\) is a homeomorphism, so \(X_K\) is irreducible. By (a), irreducible case done.

(e) Connected. Let \(k=\overline k\) and \(X\) connected, with irreducible components \(X_1,\dots,X_n\) (finitely many, \(X\) being Noetherian). Each \(X_i\) with its reduced structure is irreducible, so by the previous paragraph \((X_i)_K\), a closed subscheme of \(X_K\) with underlying set \(p_K^{-1}(X_i)\), is irreducible, hence connected. Moreover \(X_K=\bigcup_ip_K^{-1}(X_i)\) and \(p_K^{-1}(X_i)\cap p_K^{-1}(X_j)=p_K^{-1}(X_i\cap X_j)\) is non-empty iff \(X_i\cap X_j\neq\emptyset\), by surjectivity (S). Because \(X\) is connected, the graph on \(\{1,\dots,n\}\) with an edge \(i\!-\!j\) iff \(X_i\cap X_j\neq\emptyset\) is connected (otherwise \(X\) would be the disjoint union of two non-empty closed sets, each a union of components). If \(X_K=U\sqcup V\) with \(U,V\) open and closed, every connected set \(p_K^{-1}(X_i)\) lies in \(U\) or in \(V\); adjacent ones lie on the same side since they meet; thus all lie on the same side and the other set is empty. So \(X_K\) is connected. By (a), done. ∎

Exercise 2.15

Problem

Let \(X\) be a geometrically integral algebraic variety over a field \(k\). Show that for any integral algebraic variety \(Y\) over \(k\), the fibered product \(X\times_{\operatorname{Spec}k}Y\) is integral. Show the same assertion after subsequently replacing “integral” everywhere in hypothesis for \(X\) and \(Y\) by “reduced”, “irreducible”, and “connected”.

Solution

Precisely, we prove four statements, with \(Z=X\times_kY\) and \(p:Z\to Y\) the second projection: (i) \(X\) geometrically reduced, \(Y\) reduced \(\Rightarrow\) \(Z\) reduced; (ii) \(X\) geometrically integral, \(Y\) integral \(\Rightarrow\) \(Z\) integral; (iii) \(X\) geometrically irreducible, \(Y\) irreducible \(\Rightarrow\) \(Z\) irreducible; (iv) \(X\) geometrically connected, \(Y\) connected \(\Rightarrow\) \(Z\) connected. (Varieties are non-empty in (iii), (iv).) We use Exercise 2.14: for every extension \(K/k\), \(X_K\) is reduced (resp. integral, irreducible, connected) if \(X\) is geometrically so.

Preliminaries. Let \(U=\operatorname{Spec}A\subseteq X\), \(V=\operatorname{Spec}B\subseteq Y\) be affine opens, so \(U\times_kV=\operatorname{Spec}(A\otimes_kB)\); these cover \(Z\). (1) If \(B\) is a domain, \(K=\operatorname{Frac}B\), \(S=B\smallsetminus\{0\}\), then every \(s\in S\) is a non-zero-divisor on \(A\otimes_kB\) (multiplication by \(s\) is injective on \(B\), and \(A\otimes_k-\) preserves injectivity). Hence \(A\otimes_kB\subseteq S^{-1}(A\otimes_kB)=A\otimes_kK\). (2) If \(N\) is the nilradical of \(Y\), then as in (N) of Exercise 2.14, \(X\times_kY_{\operatorname{red}}\to X\times_kY\) is a homeomorphism. (3) \(p\) is surjective (the fibre over \(y\) is \(X_{k(y)}\neq\emptyset\), by Prop. 1.16), and so are all projections of fibre products over \(k\) of non-empty varieties; moreover, for non-empty \(k\)-algebras \(A,B\) we have \(A\otimes_kB\neq0\), so the product of two non-empty varieties is non-empty.

(i) Let \(B\) be reduced, with minimal primes \(\mathfrak q_i\), and \(K_i=\operatorname{Frac}(B/\mathfrak q_i)\). Then \(B\hookrightarrow\prod_iK_i\) and \(A\otimes_kB\hookrightarrow\prod_iA\otimes_kK_i\). Now \(A\otimes_kK_i\) is the ring of the affine open \(U_{K_i}\) of \(X_{K_i}\), which is reduced by Exercise 2.14. So \(A\otimes B\) is reduced for all affine \(U,V\), and \(Z\) is reduced.

(ii) Let \(B\) be a domain with \(K=\operatorname{Frac}B=K(Y)\). By (1), \(A\otimes_kB\subseteq A\otimes_kK\), the ring of \(U_K\subseteq X_K\), and \(X_K\) is integral by Exercise 2.14, so \(A\otimes_kK\) is a domain (it is non-zero). Hence \(U\times V\) is integral for all non-empty affine opens \(U\), \(V\). Two non-empty opens \(U\times V\), \(U'\times V'\) meet, since \((U\cap U')\times(V\cap V')\neq\emptyset\) by (3) (\(X\), \(Y\) irreducible). A space covered by irreducible open sets which pairwise meet is irreducible; so \(Z\) is irreducible, and reduced by (i), hence integral.

(iii), (iv): the generic fibre is dense. By (2) we may replace \(Y\) by \(Y_{\operatorname{red}}\) in (iii) (same underlying space for \(Z\)); so let \(Y\) be integral with generic point \(\xi\), \(K=k(\xi)=K(Y)\). By Prop. 1.16 and the transitivity of fibre products, \(G:=p^{-1}(\xi)\) is homeomorphic to \(Z\times_Y\operatorname{Spec}K=X_K\). Claim: \(G\) is dense in \(Z\). Let \(O\subseteq Z\) be non-empty open; it contains a non-empty basic open \(D(f)\subseteq U\times V=\operatorname{Spec}(A\otimes B)\) with \(f\in A\otimes B\) not nilpotent. By (1), \(f\) is not nilpotent in \(A\otimes K\) either, so there is a prime \(\mathfrak P\) of \(A\otimes K=S^{-1}(A\otimes B)\) with \(f\notin\mathfrak P\). It corresponds to a prime of \(A\otimes B\) missing \(S\), i.e. whose contraction to \(B\) is \(0\); this is a point of \(D(f)\cap G\). So \(G\) meets every non-empty open set.

(iii) Let \(X\) be geometrically irreducible and \(Y\) irreducible, w.l.o.g. integral. By Exercise 2.14, \(X_K\) is irreducible, so \(G\) is irreducible. As \(G\) is dense in \(Z\), \(Z=\overline G\) is irreducible.

(iv) Let \(X\) be geometrically connected and \(Y\) connected, with irreducible components \(Y_1,\dots,Y_n\) (reduced structures; \(Y\) is Noetherian). Put \(Z_i=X\times_kY_i\subseteq Z\), a closed subscheme with underlying set \(p^{-1}(Y_i)\). By the density argument applied to \(Y_i\) (integral), \(Z_i\) contains the dense connected subset \(G_i\simeq X_{K(Y_i)}\) (connected by Exercise 2.14), hence \(Z_i\) is connected (the closure of a connected set is connected). Further \(Z_i\cap Z_j=p^{-1}(Y_i\cap Y_j)\) is non-empty iff \(Y_i\cap Y_j\neq\emptyset\) (surjectivity of \(p\)). Since \(Y\) is connected, the graph on the components is connected, so, exactly as at the end of Exercise 2.14, \(Z=\bigcup_iZ_i\) is connected. ∎

Exercise 2.16

Problem

Let \(A\) be an integral domain with field of fractions \(K\), and let \(B\) be a finitely generated \(A\)-algebra. We suppose that \(B\otimes_AK\) is finite over \(K\).

  1. (a)

    For any \(b\in B\), show that there exists a \(g\in A\smallsetminus\{0\}\) such that \(gb\) is integral over \(A\).

  2. (b)

    Show that there exists an \(h\in A\smallsetminus\{0\}\) such that \(B_h\) is finite over \(A_h\).

Solution

Let \(S=A\smallsetminus\{0\}\), so \(B\otimes_AK=S^{-1}B\).

(a) \(S^{-1}B\) is a finite \(K\)-algebra, so \(b/1\) is integral over \(K\): there are \(c_1,\dots,c_n\in K\) with \((b/1)^n+c_1(b/1)^{n-1}+\dots+c_n=0\) in \(S^{-1}B\). Write \(c_i=a_i/s\) with \(a_i\in A\) and a common \(s\in S\). Then \(sb^n+a_1b^{n-1}+\dots+a_n=0\) in \(S^{-1}B\), so there is \(t\in S\) with \(t(sb^n+a_1b^{n-1}+\dots+a_n)=0\) in \(B\). Put \(g=ts\in S\). Then

\[ g\,b^n+ta_1b^{n-1}+\dots+ta_n=0\quad\text{in }B. \]

Multiplying by \(g^{n-1}\) gives

\[ (gb)^n+ta_1(gb)^{n-1}+ta_2g\,(gb)^{n-2}+\dots+ta_ng^{n-1}=0, \]

a monic equation for \(gb\) with coefficients \(ta_ig^{i-1}\in A\). So \(gb\) is integral over \(A\).

(b) Let \(b_1,\dots,b_m\) generate \(B\) as an \(A\)-algebra. By (a) choose \(g_i\in S\) with \(g_ib_i\) integral over \(A\), and put \(h=g_1\cdots g_m\in S\). In \(B_h\) the element \(b_i/1=(g_ib_i)/g_i\) is integral over \(A_h\) because \(g_ib_i\) is integral over \(A\) and \(1/g_i\in A_h\). Now \(B_h\) is generated as an \(A_h\)-algebra by \(b_1/1,\dots,b_m/1\), finitely many integral elements, so it is a finite \(A_h\)-module. ∎

Exercise 2.17

Problem

Let \(Y\) be an irreducible scheme with generic point \(\xi\), and let \(f:X\to Y\) be a dominant morphism of finite type.

  1. (a)

    Let \(x\) be a closed point of \(X_\xi\). Let \(Z\) be the reduced subscheme \(\overline{\{x\}}\) of \(X\). Show that there exists a dense open subset \(V\) of \(Y\) such that \(f^{-1}(V)\cap Z\to V\) is surjective (use Exercises 2.16 and 2.1.8).

  2. (b)

    Deduce from this that \(f(X)\) contains a dense open subset of \(Y\).

  3. (c)

    Show that \(f(X)\) is a constructible subset of \(Y\), that is \(f(X)\) is a finite disjoint union of sets \(Z_i\), with each \(Z_i\) the intersection of an open subset and a closed subset.

Solution

Remark on hypotheses. Parts (a), (b) hold as stated for any irreducible scheme \(Y\). For (c) I assume that \(Y\) is Noetherian (or at least locally Noetherian and quasi-compact), the setting of the cited Hartshorne exercise; the proof is a Noetherian induction and I do not claim (c) for non-Noetherian \(Y\). In (c) I also drop the assumptions “\(Y\) irreducible” and “\(f\) dominant” (they cannot be kept in a Noetherian induction): I prove that for every finite type morphism \(f:X\to Y\) with \(Y\) Noetherian, \(f(X)\) is constructible.

Preliminary: \(\xi\in f(X)\), i.e. \(X_\xi\neq\emptyset\). Let \(V_0=\operatorname{Spec}R\) be a non-empty affine open of \(Y\) (it contains \(\xi\)). Then \(f^{-1}(V_0)=\bigcup_{i=1}^N\operatorname{Spec}C_i\) is a finite union of affine opens (\(f\) is quasi-compact). The set \(f(X)\cap V_0=f(f^{-1}V_0)\) is dense in \(V_0\), being the trace on the open \(V_0\) of the dense set \(f(X)\). Since \(\operatorname{Spec}R\) is irreducible and \(f(f^{-1}V_0)\) is the finite union of the images of \(\operatorname{Spec}C_i\), one of these images, say that of \(\operatorname{Spec}C=\operatorname{Spec}C_1\), is dense. Then \(\ker(R\to C)\) is contained in every prime of \(R\), hence in the nilradical \(\mathfrak p_0\) of \(R\), which is the prime corresponding to \(\xi\). Let \(T=R\smallsetminus\mathfrak p_0\). The ring \(T^{-1}C\) is non-zero (otherwise \(t\cdot1=0\) in \(C\) for some \(t\in T\), so \(t\in\ker\subseteq\mathfrak p_0\)). The ideal \(\mathfrak p_0T^{-1}C\) consists of nilpotents, so it is proper, and \(T^{-1}C/\mathfrak p_0T^{-1}C\ne0\) has a prime ideal; it gives a prime of \(C\) whose contraction to \(R\) is \(\mathfrak p_0\). So \(\xi\in f(X)\) and \(X_\xi\simeq f^{-1}(\xi)\neq\emptyset\) (Prop. 1.16). Moreover \(X_\xi\) is of finite type over \(\kappa=k(\xi)\) (Prop. 2.4(c)), so it has closed points, and for a closed point \(x\) (closed in an affine open \(\operatorname{Spec}C'\) of \(X_\xi\), i.e. a maximal ideal of a finitely generated \(\kappa\)-algebra) \(k(x)\) is a finite extension of \(\kappa\) (Nullstellensatz); also \(k(x)\) is the residue field of \(x\) as a point of \(X\).

(a) Keep \(V_0=\operatorname{Spec}R\) and \(\mathfrak p_0\) as above; \(\bar R=R/\mathfrak p_0\) is a domain with \(\operatorname{Frac}\bar R=\kappa\). The point \(x\) lies in \(f^{-1}(V_0)\); choose an affine open \(\operatorname{Spec}C\subseteq f^{-1}(V_0)\) containing \(x\). \(C\) is a finitely generated \(R\)-algebra (finite type). Let \(\mathfrak q\subset C\) be the prime of \(x\). The closure of \(x\) in \(\operatorname{Spec}C\) is \(V(\mathfrak q)\), so \(Z\cap\operatorname{Spec}C=\operatorname{Spec}D\) with \(D=C/\mathfrak q\) a domain with \(\operatorname{Frac}D=k(x)\). Since \(f(x)=\xi\), the kernel of \(R\to D\) is \(\mathfrak q\cap R=\mathfrak p_0\); so \(\bar R\hookrightarrow D\) and \(D\) is a finitely generated \(\bar R\)-algebra. Then \(D\otimes_{\bar R}\kappa\) is the localization of \(D\) at \(\bar R\smallsetminus\{0\}\), a \(\kappa\)-subalgebra of \(k(x)\), which is finite-dimensional over \(\kappa\). So \(D\otimes_{\bar R}\kappa\) is finite over \(\kappa\), and Exercise 2.16(b) (with \(A=\bar R\), \(B=D\)) gives \(h\in\bar R\smallsetminus\{0\}\) with \(D_h\) finite, i.e. integral, over \(\bar R_h\). The map \(\bar R_h\to D_h\) is injective (\(D\) is a domain containing \(\bar R\)). By the lying-over theorem for integral extensions (Exercise 2.1.8), \(\operatorname{Spec}D_h\to\operatorname{Spec}\bar R_h\) is surjective.

Lift \(h\) to \(\tilde h\in R\smallsetminus\mathfrak p_0\) and let \(V=D(\tilde h)\subseteq V_0\). It is a non-empty open subset of the irreducible scheme \(Y\) (it contains \(\xi\)), hence dense. Under the homeomorphism \(\operatorname{Spec}\bar R\to\operatorname{Spec}R\) we have \(\operatorname{Spec}\bar R_h=V\) as sets. Let \(y\in V\). By surjectivity there is a prime of \(D_h\) over \(y\), i.e. a point \(w\) of \(\operatorname{Spec}D\subseteq Z\) with \(f(w)=y\in V\). Thus \(w\in f^{-1}(V)\cap Z\) and \(f^{-1}(V)\cap Z\to V\) is surjective.

(b) By the preliminary, \(X_\xi\) has a closed point \(x\); with \(Z\) and \(V\) as in (a), \(V=f(f^{-1}(V)\cap Z)\subseteq f(X)\). So \(f(X)\) contains the dense open set \(V\).

(c) Let \(Y\) be Noetherian. We prove by Noetherian induction on the closed subsets \(T\subseteq Y\) the statement \(\mathcal S(T)\): for every finite type morphism \(f:X\to Y\) with \(f(X)\subseteq T\), \(f(X)\) is constructible. (Finite unions of locally closed sets are the same as finite disjoint unions of locally closed sets, since complements and finite intersections of such sets are again finite unions of locally closed sets.) Suppose \(\mathcal S(T')\) holds for all closed \(T'\subsetneq T\) (vacuous if \(T=\emptyset\), where the statement is trivial).

Reduction to \(T\) irreducible. Let \(T=T_1\cup\dots\cup T_r\) be the irreducible components. If \(r\ge2\), put \(X_i=f^{-1}(T_i)\) with its reduced structure, \(f_i:X_i\to Y\) the restriction. \(X_i\to X\) is a closed immersion, hence of finite type (Prop. 2.4(a)), so \(f_i\) is of finite type (Prop. 2.4(b)), \(f_i(X_i)\subseteq T_i\subsetneq T\), and \(f(X)=\bigcup f_i(X_i)\) is constructible by the induction hypothesis.

The irreducible case. Let \(T\) be irreducible, with the reduced structure; it is an integral Noetherian scheme and \(T\to Y\) is a closed immersion. Let \(X'=f^{-1}(T)_{\operatorname{red}}\). Then \(X'\) is reduced, so \(X'\to Y\) factors through \(T\) (universal property of the reduced closed subscheme); \(X'\) is of finite type over \(Y\) (closed immersion followed by \(f\)), hence Noetherian (Exercise 2.1), hence \(X'\to T\) is quasi-compact, and by Prop. 2.4(e) \(f':X'\to T\) is of finite type. Also \(f'(X')=f(X)\). Let \(T'=\overline{f(X)}\subseteq T\). If \(T'\neq T\), then \(f(X)\subseteq T'\subsetneq T\) and \(\mathcal S(T')\) gives constructibility. If \(T'=T\), then \(f'\) is dominant, and by (b) there is a dense open \(V\subseteq T\) contained in \(f(X)\). Let \(T''=T\smallsetminus V\subsetneq T\) (closed) and \(X''=f^{-1}(T'')_{\operatorname{red}}\), \(f'':X''\to Y\) of finite type as above with \(f''(X'')=f(X)\cap T''\subseteq T''\). Then

\[ f(X)=V\ \cup\ (f(X)\cap T''), \]

where \(V\) is open in the closed subset \(T\) of \(Y\), hence locally closed in \(Y\), and \(f(X)\cap T''=f''(X'')\) is constructible by \(\mathcal S(T'')\). So \(f(X)\) is constructible. This proves \(\mathcal S(T)\) for all closed \(T\), and in particular, for \(T=Y\), the claim (and the case of the exercise). ∎

Exercise 2.18

Problem

Give an analogous version of Corollary 2.26(b) for projective varieties.

Solution

The exercise is open-ended; here is the statement I prove. Let \(k=\mathbf F_p\), \(\overline k\) an algebraic closure, and \(X=V_+(I)\) a closed subvariety of \(\mathbf P^n_k=\operatorname{Proj}k[T_0,\dots,T_n]\), \(I\) a homogeneous ideal. By Prop. 2.18(e), \(X(\overline k)=\{[t_0:\dots:t_n]\in\mathbf P^n(\overline k)\mid P(t)=0\ \forall P\in I\}\). Let \(\overline F_X:X(\overline k)\to X(\overline k)\), \(s\mapsto F_X\circ s\).

Claim. (b\('\)) \(\overline F_X([t_0:\dots:t_n])=[t_0^p:\dots:t_n^p]\). (c\('\)) \(X(k)\) is identified with the fixed points of \(\overline F_X\); explicitly, \([t]\) is fixed iff \([t]\in\mathbf P^n(\mathbf F_p)\).

Proof of (b\('\)). Let \(s:\operatorname{Spec}\overline k\to\mathbf P^n_k\) correspond to \([t]\). Choose \(i\) with \(t_i\neq0\); then \(s\) factors through \(D_+(T_i)=\operatorname{Spec}k[T_0/T_i,\dots,T_n/T_i]\) and corresponds to the homomorphism \(T_j/T_i\mapsto t_j/t_i\) (this is the identification of Prop. 2.18(e), Example 2.3.32). By Lemma 2.22(a) applied to the morphism \(s\), \(F_{\mathbf P^n}\circ s=s\circ F_{\operatorname{Spec}\overline k}\), and \(F_{\operatorname{Spec}\overline k}\) corresponds to the Frobenius \(\varphi:a\mapsto a^p\) of \(\overline k\). So \(F_{\mathbf P^n}\circ s\) corresponds to \(T_j/T_i\mapsto\varphi(t_j/t_i)=t_j^p/t_i^p\), i.e. to the point \([t_0^p:\dots:t_n^p]\) (note \(t_i^p\neq0\)). For the closed immersion \(\iota:X\to\mathbf P^n_k\), Lemma 2.22(a) gives \(\iota\circ F_X=F_{\mathbf P^n}\circ\iota\), so \(\iota\circ\overline F_X(s)=F_{\mathbf P^n}\circ(\iota\circ s)\). Since \(s\mapsto\iota\circ s\) is the identification \(X(\overline k)\subseteq\mathbf P^n(\overline k)\) (injective, as \(\iota\) is a monomorphism), (b\('\)) follows. As a check, \([t^p]\) is again in \(X(\overline k)\): every \(P\in I\) has coefficients in \(\mathbf F_p\), so \(P(t^p)=P(t)^p=0\).

Proof of (c\('\)). \([t]\) is fixed iff \([t^p]=[t]\), i.e. there is \(\lambda\in\overline k^*\) with \(t_j^p=\lambda t_j\) for all \(j\). Take \(i\) with \(t_i\neq0\) and rescale \(t\) so that \(t_i=1\) (replacing \(t\) by \(ct\) replaces \(\lambda\) by \(c^{p-1}\lambda\)); then \(\lambda=1\), i.e. \(t_j^p=t_j\) for all \(j\), i.e. all \(t_j\in\mathbf F_p\). Conversely such a point is fixed. So the fixed points in \(X(\overline k)\) are those \([t]\in X(\overline k)\cap\mathbf P^n(\mathbf F_p)\), which by Prop. 2.18(e) (with \(K=k=\mathbf F_p\)) and 2.18(c) is exactly \(X(k)\subseteq X(\overline k)\). ∎

Exercise 2.19

Problem

Show that assertions (b) and (c) of Corollary 2.26 remain true after replacing \(k\) by the field of \(q=p^f\) elements and \(F_X\) by \(F_X^f\).

Solution

Let \(k=\mathbf F_q\), \(q=p^f\), \(X\) an algebraic variety over \(k\) (in particular an \(\mathbf F_p\)-scheme), \(\overline k\) an algebraic closure. First, \(F_X^f\) is a morphism of \(k\)-schemes: for the structural morphism \(\pi:X\to\operatorname{Spec}k\) we have \(\pi\circ F_X^f=F_{\operatorname{Spec}k}^f\circ\pi\) (Lemma 2.22(a)) and \(F_{\operatorname{Spec}k}^f\) corresponds to \(a\mapsto a^q\), which is the identity of \(\mathbf F_q\). So \(s\mapsto F_X^f\circ s\) defines a map \(\overline F:X(\overline k)\to X(\overline k)\). Let \(\varphi:\overline k\to\overline k\), \(a\mapsto a^p\).

For \(s\in X(\overline k)\), Lemma 2.22(a) (applied to the morphism \(s\) of \(\mathbf F_p\)-schemes) gives

\[ F_X^f\circ s=s\circ F_{\operatorname{Spec}\overline k}^f, \]

and \(F_{\operatorname{Spec}\overline k}^f\) corresponds to \(\varphi^f:a\mapsto a^q\).

(b) Let \(X=V(I)\subseteq\operatorname{Spec}k[T_1,\dots,T_n]\), so \(X=\operatorname{Spec}k[T]/I\) is affine and \(s\) corresponds to a \(k\)-homomorphism \(s^\#:k[T]/I\to\overline k\), with \(t_i=s^\#(T_i)\). The morphism \(s\circ F^f_{\operatorname{Spec}\overline k}\) corresponds to \(\varphi^f\circ s^\#\), which sends \(T_i\) to \(t_i^q\). Hence

\[ \overline F(t_1,\dots,t_n)=(t_1^q,\dots,t_n^q) \]

under the identification of Prop. 2.18(d).

(c) (For arbitrary \(X\), not only affine.) By Prop. 2.18(b), \(s\) corresponds to a pair \((x,\theta)\) with \(x\in X\) and \(\theta:k(x)\to\overline k\) a \(k\)-homomorphism, \(x\) being the image point of \(s\) and \(\theta\) induced by \(s^\#_x:\mathcal O_{X,x}\to\overline k\). The point \(\overline F(s)=s\circ F^f_{\operatorname{Spec}\overline k}\) has the same image point \(x\), and its map \(\mathcal O_{X,x}\to\overline k\) is \(\varphi^f\circ s^\#_x\), so it corresponds to \((x,\varphi^f\circ\theta)\). Therefore \(s\) is fixed by \(\overline F\) iff \(\theta(a)^q=\theta(a)\) for all \(a\in k(x)\), iff \(\theta(k(x))\subseteq\mathbf F_q=k\) (the roots of \(T^q-T\) in \(\overline k\) form the subfield \(\mathbf F_q\)). In that case \(\theta\) is an injective \(k\)-linear map from \(k(x)\) into the one-dimensional space \(k\), so \(k(x)=k\) and \(\theta=\operatorname{id}\); conversely \(k(x)=k\) gives \(\theta=\operatorname{id}\) (a \(k\)-homomorphism \(k\to\overline k\)). By Prop. 2.18(b),(c), the pairs \((x,\theta)\) with \(k(x)=k\) are exactly the image of \(X(k)\hookrightarrow X(\overline k)\). So the fixed points of \(\overline F\) are exactly \(X(k)\). (The same argument with \(f=1\) proves Cor. 2.26(c) for all varieties over \(\mathbf F_p\).) ∎

Exercise 2.20

Problem

Let \(S\) be a scheme over \(\mathbf F_p\). Let \(X\) be an \(S\)-scheme.

  1. (a)

    Show that the relative Frobenius \(F_{X/S}:X\to X^{(p)}\) commutes with base change \(T\to S\) (i.e. \(F_{X/S}\times\operatorname{Id}_T=F_{X_T/T}\)).

  2. (b)

    Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of \(S\)-schemes. Show that \(f\) canonically induces a morphism of \(S\)-schemes \(f^{(p)}:X^{(p)}\to Y^{(p)}\) such that \(f^{(p)}\circ F_{X/S}=F_{Y/S}\circ f\). Note that in the special case when \(Y=S\), \(f^{(p)}\) is just the structural morphism of \(X^{(p)}\). Let us suppose that \(f\) satisfies a property (P) which is stable by base change (e.g., closed immersion). Show that \(f^{(p)}\) also satisfies (P).

  3. (c)

    Let us suppose that \(S\) is the spectrum of a field. Show that the morphism \(\phi:X^{(p)}\to X\) induces an injective homomorphism \(\mathcal O_X\to\phi_*\mathcal O_{X^{(p)}}\). Deduce from this that if \(X^{(p)}\) is integral, then so is \(X\). Show that the converse is false in general.

Solution

Notation of the text: \(X\) has structural morphism \(\pi_X\); \(X^{(p)}=X\times_{S}S\) where the second factor is the \(S\)-scheme \(F_S:S\to S\); \(\phi_X:X^{(p)}\to X\) and \(q_X:X^{(p)}\to S\) are the projections; \(F_{X/S}\) is the unique \(S\)-morphism with \(\phi_X\circ F_{X/S}=F_X\) and \(q_X\circ F_{X/S}=\pi_X\). All schemes below are \(\mathbf F_p\)-schemes (e.g. \(T\) is, via \(T\to S\)), so Lemma 2.22 applies. We argue with \(Z\)-valued points for an arbitrary scheme \(Z\) (Yoneda).

(a) Let \(g:T\to S\) be the given morphism and \(X_T=X\times_ST\) with projections \(a:X_T\to X\), \(b:X_T\to T\). We have two schemes:

\begin{align*} (X_T)^{(p)}(Z)&=\{(u,t')\mid u:Z\to X_T,\ t':Z\to T,\ b\circ u=F_T\circ t'\},\\ (X^{(p)})_T(Z)&=\{(w,t)\mid w:Z\to X^{(p)},\ t:Z\to T,\ q_X\circ w=g\circ t\}. \end{align*}

Write \(u=(x,t_1)\) with \(x:Z\to X\), \(t_1:Z\to T\), \(\pi_Xx=gt_1\); then the condition \(b u=F_Tt'\) says \(t_1=F_Tt'\), so \((X_T)^{(p)}(Z)=\{(x,t')\mid\pi_X\circ x=g\circ F_T\circ t'=F_S\circ g\circ t'\}\) (using \(gF_T=F_Sg\), Lemma 2.22(a)). Write \(w=(x,s)\) with \(x:Z\to X\), \(s:Z\to S\), \(\pi_Xx=F_Ss\); then \(q_Xw=s\), so \((X^{(p)})_T(Z)=\{(x,t)\mid\pi_X\circ x=F_S\circ g\circ t\}\). These are the same functor of \(Z\), giving a canonical isomorphism \(\theta:(X_T)^{(p)}\simeq(X^{(p)})_T\), under which \((u,t')=((x,F_Tt'),t')\) corresponds to \((x,t')\). This is the identification with respect to which the statement is meant.

Now take a \(Z\)-point \((x,t)\) of \(X_T\) (so \(\pi_Xx=gt\)). On one hand \((F_{X/S}\times\operatorname{Id}_T)(x,t)=(F_{X/S}\circ x,t)\), which as a point of \((X^{(p)})_T\) corresponds to the pair \((\phi_X\circ F_{X/S}\circ x,t)=(F_X\circ x,t)\). On the other hand, \(F_{X_T/T}\) is characterized by \(\phi_{X_T}\circ F_{X_T/T}=F_{X_T}\) and \(q_{X_T}\circ F_{X_T/T}=b\). So \(F_{X_T/T}(x,t)=(u',t')\) with \(u'=F_{X_T}\circ(x,t)\) and \(t'=b(x,t)=t\). Since \(a\circ F_{X_T}=F_X\circ a\) (Lemma 2.22(a)), \(u'=(F_X\circ x,F_T\circ t)\), and under \(\theta\) the point \((u',t)\) corresponds to the pair \((F_X\circ x,t)\). The two points agree, so \(F_{X/S}\times\operatorname{Id}_T=F_{X_T/T}\).

(b) Define \(f^{(p)}=f\times\operatorname{Id}_S:X\times_{S,F_S}S\to Y\times_{S,F_S}S\) (this makes sense since \(\pi_Yf=\pi_X\)); then \(q_Y\circ f^{(p)}=q_X\), so \(f^{(p)}\) is an \(S\)-morphism, and \(\phi_Y\circ f^{(p)}=f\circ\phi_X\). To prove \(f^{(p)}\circ F_{X/S}=F_{Y/S}\circ f\) we compare projections to \(Y^{(p)}=Y\times_{S,F_S}S\):

\begin{align*} \phi_Yf^{(p)}F_{X/S}&=f\phi_XF_{X/S}=fF_X,& \phi_YF_{Y/S}f&=F_Yf=fF_X,\\ q_Yf^{(p)}F_{X/S}&=q_XF_{X/S}=\pi_X,& q_YF_{Y/S}f&=\pi_Yf=\pi_X, \end{align*}

using Lemma 2.22(a). By the uniqueness in the universal property of the fibered product, they are equal. If \(Y=S\) (with \(\operatorname{Id}_S\)), then \(Y^{(p)}=S\times_{S,F_S}S\simeq S\) and \(f^{(p)}=\pi_X\times\operatorname{Id}_S:X^{(p)}\to S\) is \(q_X\), the structural morphism of \(X^{(p)}\).

Now let (P) be stable under base change and satisfied by \(f\). We have canonical isomorphisms

\[ X\times_YY^{(p)}=X\times_Y(Y\times_{S,F_S}S)\simeq X\times_{S,F_S}S=X^{(p)} \]

(transitivity of fibered products, using \(\pi_Yf=\pi_X\)), under which the second projection \(X\times_YY^{(p)}\to Y^{(p)}\) becomes \(f\times\operatorname{Id}_S=f^{(p)}\). Thus \(f^{(p)}\) is the base change of \(f\) by \(\phi_Y:Y^{(p)}\to Y\), and has (P).

(c) Now \(S=\operatorname{Spec}k\), \(\operatorname{char}k=p\). Then \(X^{(p)}=X\times_kk'\), where \(k'=\operatorname{Spec}k\) is a \(k\)-scheme via \(F_S\), i.e. \(k\to k\), \(a\mapsto a^p\), and \(\phi=\phi_X\) is the base change of \(\operatorname{Spec}k'\to\operatorname{Spec}k\) (writing \(k'\) for \(k\) with the \(k\)-algebra structure \(a\mapsto a^p\)). For an affine open \(U=\operatorname{Spec}A\) of \(X\), \(\phi^{-1}(U)=\operatorname{Spec}(A\otimes_kk')\) and \(\phi^\#:A\to A\otimes_kk'\) is \(a\mapsto a\otimes1\). As \(k'\) is a non-zero \(k\)-vector space, \(1\in k'\) is part of a \(k\)-basis, so \(A\to A\otimes_kk'\) is injective (indeed split). A section of \(\mathcal O_X\) over an arbitrary open set which maps to \(0\) vanishes on every affine open subset, so it is \(0\): \(\mathcal O_X\to\phi_*\mathcal O_{X^{(p)}}\) is injective.

Suppose \(X^{(p)}\) integral. By Lemma 2.24, \(\phi\) is a homeomorphism, so \(X\neq\emptyset\) is irreducible. For \(U\) open in \(X\), \(\mathcal O_X(U)\hookrightarrow\mathcal O_{X^{(p)}}(\phi^{-1}U)\), and the latter is a domain when \(U\neq\emptyset\); so every \(\mathcal O_X(U)\) is a domain, and \(X\) is integral.

The converse is false. Let \(p\) be a prime, \(k=\mathbf F_p(t)\) and \(K=k[x]/(x^p-t)\), a field since \(x^p-t\) is Eisenstein at the prime \(t\) of \(\mathbf F_p[t]\), hence irreducible over \(\mathbf F_p(t)\) by Gauss' lemma. Then \(X=\operatorname{Spec}K\) is an integral variety over \(k\). Its Frobenius twist is \(X^{(p)}=\operatorname{Spec}(K\otimes_kk')\) where \(k\to k'=k\) is \(a\mapsto a^p\). Now

\[ K\otimes_kk'=k'[x]/(x^p-t^p)=k[x]/((x-t)^p), \]

because the image of \(t\) in \(k'\) is \(t^p\), and \(x^p-t^p=(x-t)^p\) in characteristic \(p\). This ring has the non-zero nilpotent element \(x-t\), so \(X^{(p)}\) is not reduced, hence not integral. (This is consistent with Cor. 2.27: \(X\) is not geometrically reduced.) ∎

Exercise 2.21

Problem

Let \(B\) be a finitely generated algebra over a ring \(A\). Show that there exist a subring \(A_0\) of \(A\) that is finitely generated over \(\mathbf Z\) and a finitely generated \(A_0\)-algebra \(B_0\) such that \(B=B_0\otimes_{A_0}A\). If \(B\) is homogeneous, we can take \(B_0\) homogeneous.

Solution

The statement is false as printed unless \(B\) is of finite presentation over \(A\) (automatic if \(A\) is Noetherian, which is the situation of the Remark following the exercise). Here is a counterexample. Let \(A=\mathbf F_p[x_1,x_2,\dots]\) (infinitely many variables), \(J=(x_1,x_2,\dots)\) and \(B=A/J\simeq\mathbf F_p\), which is generated by \(1\) over \(A\). Suppose \(A_0\subseteq A\) is finitely generated over \(\mathbf Z\) and \(B_0\) is a finitely generated \(A_0\)-algebra with \(B_0\otimes_{A_0}A\simeq B\) as \(A\)-algebras. Then \(A_0\) is Noetherian (Hilbert basis theorem), \(B_0=A_0[T_1,\dots,T_n]/I_0\) with \(I_0=(g_1,\dots,g_m)\) finitely generated, and \(B\simeq A[T_1,\dots,T_n]/(g_1,\dots,g_m)\) is a finitely presented \(A\)-algebra. But then \(J\) would be finitely generated: choose \(a_i\in A\) with \(a_i\mapsto\) the class of \(T_i\) in \(B\) (possible as \(A\to B\) is onto) and let \(\operatorname{ev}:A[T]\to A\) be the substitution \(T_i\mapsto a_i\), which is onto. The composite \(A[T]\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}}A\to B\) agrees with the quotient map \(A[T]\to B\) (both are \(A\)-algebra maps with the same values on the \(T_i\)), so \(\operatorname{ev}^{-1}(J)=(g_1,\dots,g_m)\) and \(J=\operatorname{ev}((g_1,\dots,g_m))=(g_1(a),\dots,g_m(a))\) is finitely generated, which is false. So no such \(A_0,B_0\) exist.

Correct statement and proof. Let \(B\) be a finitely presented \(A\)-algebra, i.e. \(B=A[T_1,\dots,T_n]/(g_1,\dots,g_m)\) (this holds whenever \(A\) is Noetherian, since then every ideal of \(A[T_1,\dots,T_n]\) is finitely generated). Let \(A_0\) be the subring of \(A\) generated by the (finitely many) coefficients of \(g_1,\dots,g_m\); it is finitely generated over \(\mathbf Z\). Then \(g_j\in A_0[T]\); put \(B_0=A_0[T_1,\dots,T_n]/(g_1,\dots,g_m)\), a finitely generated \(A_0\)-algebra. By right exactness of \(-\otimes_{A_0}A\),

\[ B_0\otimes_{A_0}A=A[T_1,\dots,T_n]/(g_1,\dots,g_m)A[T_1,\dots,T_n]=B. \]

Homogeneous case. Here “\(B\) homogeneous” means \(B=A[T_0,\dots,T_n]/I\) with \(I\) a homogeneous ideal, graded by degree. If \(I\) is finitely generated (finite presentation, e.g. \(A\) Noetherian), then it is generated by finitely many homogeneous elements: the homogeneous components of finitely many generators lie in \(I\) and generate \(I\). Choose \(g_1,\dots,g_m\) homogeneous generators and define \(A_0\), \(B_0=A_0[T_0,\dots,T_n]/(g_1,\dots,g_m)\) as above. The \(g_j\) are homogeneous with coefficients in \(A_0\), so \(B_0\) is a homogeneous \(A_0\)-algebra (graded quotient), and the isomorphism \(B_0\otimes_{A_0}A\simeq B\) is an isomorphism of graded \(A\)-algebras (the grading on \(B_0\otimes_{A_0}A\) being the one induced by \((B_0)_d\otimes A\), as in the text). ∎

3.3 Some global properties of morphisms

Conventions. A closed immersion is a morphism \(f:X\to Y\) which is a homeomorphism onto a closed subset of \(Y\) and such that \(f^\#:\mathcal O_Y\to f_*\mathcal O_X\) is surjective. “Algebraic variety over \(k\)” means a scheme of finite type over \(k\); dimension and irreducible components only depend on the underlying topological space, so in Ex. 3.4 closed subvarieties are given their reduced structure. Honest notes. In Ex. 3.5 one needs \(X\neq\varnothing\). The criterion in Ex. 3.13(b) is false as printed when \(S\) is not separated (a counterexample is given) and is proved with the extra condition that \(y\) lies over the same point of \(S\) as \(x\), which is automatic if \(S\) is separated. In Ex. 3.14 the hypotheses “birational” and “\(S\) Noetherian” are not needed.

Exercise 3.1

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of schemes. We suppose that there exist open subsets \(Y_i\) of \(Y\) such that \(X=\bigcup_i f^{-1}(Y_i)\), and that the restrictions \(f:f^{-1}(Y_i)\to Y_i\) are closed immersions. Show that if \(f(X)\) is closed in \(Y\), then \(f\) is a closed immersion.

Solution

Put \(Y'=\bigcup_iY_i\); then \(f(X)\subseteq Y'\).

\(f\) is injective. If \(f(x)=f(x')=y\), then \(y\in Y_i\) for some \(i\), so \(x,x'\in f^{-1}(Y_i)\), where \(f\) is injective; thus \(x=x'\).

\(f\) is a closed map. Let \(F\subseteq X\) be closed. For each \(i\), \(F\cap f^{-1}(Y_i)\) is closed in \(f^{-1}(Y_i)\), and \(f:f^{-1}(Y_i)\to Y_i\) is a closed immersion, hence a closed map; so \(f(F)\cap Y_i=f(F\cap f^{-1}(Y_i))\) is closed in \(Y_i\). Since the \(Y_i\) form an open cover of \(Y'\), \(f(F)\) is closed in \(Y'\). Let \(C\) be the closure of \(f(F)\) in \(Y\). Since \(f(X)\) is closed in \(Y\), \(C\subseteq f(X)\subseteq Y'\), so \(C\) is also the closure of \(f(F)\) in \(Y'\), which is \(f(F)\). Thus \(f(F)\) is closed in \(Y\).

An injective continuous closed map is a homeomorphism onto its image, and \(f(X)\) is closed by hypothesis.

\(f^\#:\mathcal O_Y\to f_*\mathcal O_X\) is surjective. It suffices to check this on stalks at every \(y\in Y\). If \(y\notin f(X)\), then \(Y\setminus f(X)\) is an open neighbourhood of \(y\) with \(f^{-1}(Y\setminus f(X))=\varnothing\), so \((f_*\mathcal O_X)_y=0\) and there is nothing to prove. If \(y\in f(X)\), then \(y\in Y_i\) for some \(i\), and on \(Y_i\) the sheaf \(f_*\mathcal O_X\) is the sheaf \((f|_{f^{-1}(Y_i)})_*\mathcal O_{f^{-1}(Y_i)}\), for which \(\mathcal O_{Y_i}\to(f|)_*\mathcal O_{f^{-1}(Y_i)}\) is surjective by hypothesis. Hence \(f\) is a closed immersion. ∎

Exercise 3.2

Problem

Let \(X\) be a scheme. Show that the following properties are equivalent: (i) \(X\) is separated; (ii) \(X\) is separated over an affine scheme; (iii) for any scheme \(Y\), every morphism of schemes \(X\to Y\) is separated.

Solution

(i)\(\Rightarrow\)(iii). Let \(u:X\to Y\) be any morphism. The structure morphism \(X\to\Spec\Z\) equals \(X\xrightarrow{u}Y\to\Spec\Z\) and is separated by (i). By Prop. 3.9(e) (if \(g\circ u\) is separated, then \(u\) is separated; no hypothesis on \(g\)), \(u\) is separated.

(iii)\(\Rightarrow\)(ii). Take \(Y=\Spec\Z\), which is affine.

(ii)\(\Rightarrow\)(i). Let \(X\to\Spec A\) be separated. The morphism \(\Spec A\to\Spec\Z\) is separated (Prop. 3.4), so the composite \(X\to\Spec\Z\) is separated by Prop. 3.9(b). That is, \(X\) is separated. ∎

Exercise 3.3

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a surjective closed morphism between two Noetherian schemes (or, more generally, topological spaces). Show that we have the inequality \(\dim X\ge\dim Y\) (reduce to the case when \(X\) is irreducible and use induction on \(\dim X\)).

Solution

We only use that \(f\) is a continuous, surjective, closed map of topological spaces with \(X\) Noetherian. Dimension is the supremum of the lengths \(n\) of chains \(Z_0\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq Z_n\) of irreducible closed subsets. We prove by induction on \(n\ge0\):

Claim \(P(n)\). If \(f:X\to Y\) is a continuous, closed, surjective map with \(X\) Noetherian and \(Y\) contains a chain \(Y_0\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq Y_n\) of irreducible closed subsets, then \(X\) contains such a chain of length \(n\).

Applying \(P(n)\) to chains of \(Y\) of every length gives \(\dim X\ge\dim Y\) (also when \(\dim Y=\infty\)).

\(P(0)\): \(Y_0\ne\varnothing\) and \(f\) is surjective, so \(X\neq\varnothing\) and contains an irreducible closed subset (a point closure).

\(P(n-1)\Rightarrow P(n)\). Let \(Z=f^{-1}(Y_n)\), a closed subset of \(X\), hence Noetherian, with finitely many irreducible components \(Z_1,\dots,Z_m\). The restriction \(Z\to Y_n\) is surjective and closed (a closed subset of \(Z\) is closed in \(X\), its image is closed in \(Y\) and contained in \(Y_n\)). Hence \(Y_n=\bigcup_jf(Z_j)\) is a finite union of closed subsets; as \(Y_n\) is irreducible, \(Y_n=f(Z_j)\) for some \(j\). Put \(W=Z_j\cap f^{-1}(Y_{n-1})\). It is closed in \(X\), hence Noetherian, and \(f(W)=Y_{n-1}\) because \(Y_{n-1}\subseteq Y_n=f(Z_j)\). So \(f|_W:W\to Y_{n-1}\) is surjective and closed, and \(Y_0\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq Y_{n-1}\) is a chain in \(Y_{n-1}\). By \(P(n-1)\), \(W\) contains a chain \(T_0\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq T_{n-1}\) of irreducible closed subsets (closed in \(W\), hence in \(X\)). Since \(f(W)=Y_{n-1}\ne Y_n=f(Z_j)\), we have \(W\subsetneq Z_j\), so \(T_0\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq T_{n-1}\subsetneq Z_j\) is a chain of length \(n\) in \(X\) (\(Z_j\) is irreducible and closed). ∎

Exercise 3.4

Problem

Let \(k\) be a field. Let \(Z=\mathbb A^n_k\) or \(\mathbb P^n_k\). Let us consider two closed subvarieties \(X,Y\) of \(Z\), pure of respective dimensions \(q,r\).

  1. (a)

    Show that the irreducible components of \(X\times_kY\) are of dimension \(q+r\).

  2. (b)

    Show that \(\dim(X\cap Y)\ge q+r-n\) if \(X\cap Y\ne\varnothing\) (identify \(X\cap Y\) with the subset \(\Delta(Z)\cap(X\times_kY)\) of \(Z\times_kZ\) and use Corollary 2.5.26).

  3. (c)

    If \(Z=\mathbb P^n_k\) and \(q+r\ge n\), show that \(X\cap Y\neq\varnothing\) (if \(X=V_+(I)\), \(Y=V_+(J)\), consider \(V(I)\cap V(J)\) in \(\mathbb A^{n+1}_k\) and use Corollary 2.5.21).

  4. (d)

    Show that every projective plane curve \(X\) (i.e. \(X\) is a closed subvariety of \(\mathbb P^2_k\), pure of dimension \(1\)) is connected.

Solution

Varieties are nonempty. We use Prop. 2.5.19: for an integral variety, every nonempty open subset has the same dimension, equal to the transcendence degree of the function field.

(a) Let \(X_1,\dots,X_a\) and \(Y_1,\dots,Y_b\) be the irreducible components of \(X\), \(Y\) (reduced structures). As sets \(X\times_kY=\bigcup_{i,j}X_i\times_kY_j\) (the \(X_i\times_kY_j\to X\times_kY\) are closed immersions), so every irreducible component of \(X\times_kY\) is an irreducible component of some \(X_i\times_kY_j\). So it suffices to treat \(X,Y\) integral of dimensions \(q,r\). Let \(C\) be an irreducible component of \(X\times_kY\). It meets an affine open subset \(\Spec(A\otimes_kB)\), with \(\Spec A\subseteq X\), \(\Spec B\subseteq Y\) nonempty affine opens (\(A,B\) domains of finitely generated type over \(k\), of dimensions \(q,r\) by Prop. 2.5.19). Then \(C\cap\Spec(A\otimes B)=V(P)\) for a minimal prime \(P\) of \(R:=A\otimes_kB\), and \(\dim C=\dim R/P=\operatorname{trdeg}_k\operatorname{Frac}(R/P)\) (Prop. 2.5.19 applied to \(C_{\rm red}\) and its open subset). We show \(\operatorname{trdeg}_k\operatorname{Frac}(R/P)=q+r\).

\(R\) is a free \(B\)-module (as \(A\) is a free \(k\)-module), so \(B\to R\) is flat and injective. Flat extensions satisfy going-down, so \(P\cap B\) is a minimal prime of \(B\), i.e. \(P\cap B=0\). Let \(L=\operatorname{Frac}(B)\) and invert \(1\otimes(B\setminus0)\): then \(R\) is replaced by \(R'=A\otimes_kL\) and \(P\) by a minimal prime \(P'\) of \(R'\) with \(\operatorname{Frac}(R/P)=\operatorname{Frac}(R'/P')\). Choose a transcendence basis \(y_1,\dots,y_r\) of \(L\) over \(k\) and put \(F=k(y_1,\dots,y_r)\); \(L\) is finite over \(F\). The ring \(A_1:=A\otimes_kF\) is a localization of the polynomial ring \(A[y_1,\dots,y_r]\), hence a domain with fraction field \(\operatorname{Frac}(A)(y_1,\dots,y_r)\) of transcendence degree \(q+r\) over \(k\). Now \(R'=A_1\otimes_FL\) is free over \(A_1\), so again by going-down \(P'\cap A_1=0\) and \(A_1\hookrightarrow R'/P'\). Moreover \(R'/P'\) is generated over \(A_1\) by elements of \(L\), which are algebraic over \(F\), so \(R'/P'\) is integral over \(A_1\). Hence \(\operatorname{Frac}(R'/P')\) is algebraic over \(\operatorname{Frac}(A_1)\), and has transcendence degree \(q+r\). This proves (a).

(b) Lemma. Let \(T\) be an irreducible closed subset (reduced structure) of dimension \(d\) of an affine variety \(V\) and \(f_1,\dots,f_m\in\mathcal O(V)\). Then every irreducible component of \(T\cap V(f_1,\dots,f_m)\) has dimension \(\ge d-m\).

Induction on \(m\), \(m=0\) being trivial. Let \(E\) be an irreducible component of \(T\cap V(f_1,\dots,f_m)\). It lies in an irreducible component \(E'\) of \(T\cap V(f_1,\dots,f_{m-1})\), with \(\dim E'\ge d-m+1\), and \(E\) is a component of \(E'\cap V(f_m)\) (it is maximal irreducible in the bigger set). If \(f_m|_{E'}=0\) then \(E=E'\). Otherwise \(f_m|_{E'}\in\mathcal O(E')\) is non-nilpotent (\(E'\) is reduced), and Cor. 2.5.26 gives \(\dim E=\dim E'-1\ge d-m\).

Case \(Z=\mathbb A^n_k=\Spec k[x_1,\dots,x_n]\). Then \(Z\times_kZ=\Spec k[x,y]\) and \(\Delta(Z)=V(x_1-y_1,\dots,x_n-y_n)\). Let \(P=X\times_kY\), a closed subset of \(Z\times Z\) with image \(p_1^{-1}(X)\cap p_2^{-1}(Y)\). The diagonal \(\Delta\) is a closed immersion, and \(\Delta(z)\in p_1^{-1}(X)\cap p_2^{-1}(Y)\) iff \(z\in X\cap Y\). Hence \(\Delta\) is a homeomorphism from \(X\cap Y\) onto \(\Delta(Z)\cap P=P\cap V(f_1,\dots,f_n)\) with \(f_i=x_i-y_i\). Let \(C\) be an irreducible component of \(X\cap Y\) and \(C'\subseteq P\cap V(f_1,\dots,f_n)\) the corresponding component, so \(\dim C=\dim C'\). Choose an irreducible component \(D\) of \(P\) containing \(C'\); by (a), \(\dim D=q+r\). Then \(C'\) is a component of \(D\cap V(f_1,\dots,f_n)\), so by the Lemma \(\dim C\ge q+r-n\).

Case \(Z=\mathbb P^n_k\). Let \(C\) be an irreducible component of \(X\cap Y\) and \(z\in C\); say \(z\in U_i=D_+(T_i)\simeq\mathbb A^n_k\). Then \(X\cap U_i\), \(Y\cap U_i\) are nonempty closed subvarieties of \(U_i\) which are pure of dimensions \(q,r\) (Prop. 2.5.19 applied to each component), and \(C\cap U_i\) is an irreducible component of \((X\cap U_i)\cap(Y\cap U_i)\) with \(\dim(C\cap U_i)=\dim C\). The affine case gives \(\dim C\ge q+r-n\).

(c) Let \(B=k[T_0,\dots,T_n]\) and replace \(I,J\) by their radicals, which are homogeneous. As \(X,Y\neq\varnothing\), \(I,J\subseteq\mathfrak m_0:=(T_0,\dots,T_n)\) (a proper homogeneous prime is contained in \(B_+\)). Let \(\mathfrak p_1,\dots,\mathfrak p_s\) be the minimal primes of \(I\); they are homogeneous (Lemma 2.3.35(a)) and different from \(\mathfrak m_0\) (as \(X\ne\varnothing\), some prime \(\mathfrak q\in\operatorname{Proj}B\) contains \(I\), and \(\mathfrak q\subsetneq\mathfrak m_0\), so \(\mathfrak m_0\) is not minimal over \(I\)), and \(V_+(\mathfrak p_i)\) are the irreducible components of \(X\), of dimension \(q\). By Cor. 2.5.21, \(\dim V(\mathfrak p_i)=\dim B/\mathfrak p_i=q+1\). So \(V(I)\subseteq\mathbb A^{n+1}_k\) is a closed subvariety pure of dimension \(q+1\), and likewise \(V(J)\) is pure of dimension \(r+1\). Both contain the origin \(\mathfrak m_0\). Apply (b) in \(\mathbb A^{n+1}_k\): every irreducible component of \(V(I)\cap V(J)=V(I+J)\) has dimension \(\ge(q+1)+(r+1)-(n+1)=q+r-n+1\ge1\). Let \(\mathfrak P\) be a minimal prime of \(I+J\); it is homogeneous (Lemma 2.3.35(a)) and \(\dim B/\mathfrak P\ge1\), so \(\mathfrak P\ne\mathfrak m_0\). A homogeneous prime containing \(B_+=\mathfrak m_0\) equals \(\mathfrak m_0\), so \(\mathfrak P\in\operatorname{Proj}B\) and \(\mathfrak P\in V_+(I)\cap V_+(J)=X\cap Y\).

(d) Suppose \(X=X_1\sqcup X_2\) with \(X_1,X_2\) nonempty, disjoint and closed. Each \(X_i\) is a union of irreducible components of \(X\), hence a closed subvariety of \(\mathbb P^2_k\) pure of dimension \(1\). Apply (c) with \(n=2\), \(q=r=1\) (so \(q+r\ge n\)): \(X_1\cap X_2\ne\varnothing\), a contradiction. ∎

Exercise 3.5

Problem

Let \(X\to S\) be a proper morphism. Let \(f:X\to Y\) be an open immersion of \(S\)-schemes. Let us suppose that \(Y\) is separated over \(S\) and connected. Show that \(f\) is an isomorphism.

Solution

The statement needs \(X\neq\varnothing\) (if \(X=\varnothing\) the empty map is an open immersion into any \(Y\ne\varnothing\) and is not an isomorphism); we assume it. The composite \(X\xrightarrow{f}Y\to S\) is proper and \(Y\to S\) is separated, so \(f\) is proper (Prop. 3.16(e)), in particular a closed morphism. Thus \(f(X)\) is open (open immersion) and closed (closed morphism) and nonempty in the connected space \(Y\), so \(f(X)=Y\). A surjective open immersion is an isomorphism onto the open subscheme \(f(X)=Y\). ∎

Exercise 3.6

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a separated morphism. Show that any section of \(f\) is a closed immersion (apply Proposition 3.9(f) to \(X\times_XY\to X\times_YY\)).

Solution

Let \(s:Y\to X\) with \(f\circ s=\mathrm{Id}_Y\). Regard \(X\) as a scheme over \(Y\) via \(f\) (separated by hypothesis), and consider the \(X\)-scheme \(Y\) (structure morphism \(s\)) and the \(X\)-scheme \(X\) (structure morphism \(\mathrm{Id}_X\)). Prop. 3.9(f) (with base \(Y\), separated scheme \(X\) over it, \(X_1=Y\) and \(X_2=X\) over \(X\)) says that the canonical morphism

\[ \varphi: Y\times_XX\to Y\times_YX \]

is a closed immersion; \(\varphi\) is characterized by \(\mathrm{pr}_1\circ\varphi=p_1\) and \(\mathrm{pr}_2\circ\varphi=p_2\), where \(p_1,p_2\) are the projections of \(Y\times_XX\) and \(\mathrm{pr}_1,\mathrm{pr}_2\) those of \(Y\times_YX\). Now \(p_1:Y\times_XX\to Y\) is an isomorphism (the base change of \(\mathrm{Id}_X\)) with \(p_2=s\circ p_1\), and \(\mathrm{pr}_2:Y\times_YX\to X\) is an isomorphism (base change of \(\mathrm{Id}_Y\)). Therefore

\[ \mathrm{pr}_2\circ\varphi\circ p_1^{-1}=p_2\circ p_1^{-1}=s, \]

so \(s\) is the composite of an isomorphism, the closed immersion \(\varphi\) and an isomorphism, hence a closed immersion. ∎

Exercise 3.7

Problem

Let \(X\) be a \(Y\)-scheme. Show that \(X\) is separated over \(Y\) if and only if for every \(Y\)-scheme \(Y'\), the sections of \(X\times_YY'\to Y'\) are closed immersions (hint: \(\Delta_{X/Y}\) is a section of the second projection \(X\times_YX\to X\)).

Solution

(\(\Rightarrow\)) If \(X\to Y\) is separated, then \(X\times_YY'\to Y'\) is separated for every \(Y'\) (Prop. 3.9(c)), and its sections are closed immersions by Ex. 3.6.

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Take \(Y'=X\) (a \(Y\)-scheme via the structure morphism). Then \(X\times_YY'=X\times_YX\to Y'=X\) is the second projection, and \(\Delta_{X/Y}\) is a section of it since \(\mathrm{pr}_2\circ\Delta=\mathrm{Id}_X\). By hypothesis \(\Delta_{X/Y}\) is a closed immersion, i.e. \(X\to Y\) is separated. ∎

Exercise 3.8

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism with \(X\) affine and \(Y\) separated. Show that \(f^{-1}(V)\) is affine for every affine open subset \(V\) of \(Y\) (use Proposition 3.9(f)).

Solution

Here “\(Y\) separated” means separated over \(\Spec\Z\). Let \(V\subseteq Y\) be an affine open subset; \(X\) and \(V\) are \(Y\)-schemes (via \(f\) and the inclusion). Prop. 3.9(f) (base \(\Spec\Z\), separated scheme \(Y\)) says that the canonical morphism

\[ X\times_YV\to X\times_{\Z}V \]

is a closed immersion. The scheme \(X\times_{\Z}V\) is affine, being a fibred product of affine schemes, and a closed subscheme of an affine scheme is affine. Finally \(X\times_YV\) is identified with the open subscheme \(f^{-1}(V)\) of \(X\). Hence \(f^{-1}(V)\) is affine. ∎

Exercise 3.9

Problem

Let \(S\) be a locally Noetherian scheme, \(s\in S\). Let \(Y\) be a scheme of finite type over \(\Spec\mathcal O_{S,s}\). Show that there exist an open neighborhood \(U\) of \(s\) and a scheme of finite type \(X\to U\) such that \(Y=X\times_U\Spec\mathcal O_{S,s}\). Moreover, if \(Y\) is separated, we can take \(X\to U\) separated.

Solution

Choose an affine open neighbourhood \(\Spec A\) of \(s\); \(A\) is Noetherian, \(s\) corresponds to a prime \(\mathfrak p\) and \(R:=\mathcal O_{S,s}=A_{\mathfrak p}=\varinjlim_{h\notin\mathfrak p}A_h\). All neighbourhoods \(U\) below are of the form \(D(g)=\Spec A_g\), \(g\in A\setminus\mathfrak p\); the rings \(A_g\) are Noetherian. For an \(A_g\)-algebra \(C\) we write \(C_h=C\otimes_{A_g}A_{gh}\) and \(C_R=C\otimes_{A_g}R=\varinjlim_hC_h\). Finite type algebras over \(A_g\) are finitely presented.

Step 0: an algebra lemma. Let \(D,C\) be \(A_g\)-algebras of finite type.

  1. (i)

    Every \(R\)-algebra homomorphism \(\varphi:D_R\to C_R\) is \(\varphi_0\otimes R\) for some \(A_{gh}\)-algebra homomorphism \(\varphi_0:D_h\to C_h\), \(h\notin\mathfrak p\).

  2. (ii)

    If \(\varphi_1,\varphi_2:D\to C\) become equal over \(R\), they become equal over \(A_{gh}\) for some \(h\notin\mathfrak p\).

  3. (iii)

    If \(\varphi:D\to C\) is such that \(\varphi_R\) is an isomorphism, then \(\varphi_h\) is an isomorphism for some \(h\notin\mathfrak p\).

  4. (iv)

    If \(O_1,O_2\) are open subsets of \(\Spec C\) with \((O_1)_R\subseteq(O_2)_R\) (preimages in \(\Spec C_R\)), then \(O_1\cap\Spec C_h\subseteq O_2\cap\Spec C_h\) for some \(h\notin\mathfrak p\) (in particular equal if equal over \(R\)).

Proof. Write \(D=A_g[x_1,\dots,x_m]/(P_1,\dots,P_l)\). (i) Write \(\varphi(x_i)=c_i/h\) with \(c_i\in C\) and a common \(h\). The elements \(P_j(c_1/h,\dots,c_m/h)\in C_h\) vanish in \(C_R=\varinjlim C_h\), hence in \(C_{hh'}\) for some \(h'\). Then \(x_i\mapsto c_i/h\) defines \(\varphi_0\) on \(D_{hh'}\), and \(\varphi_0\otimes R=\varphi\) as both agree on generators. (ii) The finitely many elements \(\varphi_1(x_i)-\varphi_2(x_i)\) vanish in \(C_R\), hence in some \(C_{h_i}\); take \(h=\prod h_i\). (iii) By (i) the inverse of \(\varphi_R\) is \(\psi_0\otimes R\) with \(\psi_0:C_h\to D_h\); then \(\psi_0\varphi_h\) and \(\varphi_h\psi_0\) become the identities over \(R\), hence over some \(A_{ghh'}\) by (ii). (iv) We may assume \(O_1=D(c)\) (a finite union of such, \(\Spec C\) being Noetherian). Then \(D(c)_R\subseteq(O_2)_R=\bigcup_jD(c_j')_R\) means that \(c_j'\) generate the unit ideal in \(C_{c}\otimes R\), say \(1=\sum u_jc_j'\); this identity already holds in \(C_c\otimes A_{gh}\) for some \(h\).

Step 1: bi-principal opens. If \(U_1=\Spec B_1\), \(U_2=\Spec B_2\) are affine opens of a scheme and \(y\in U_1\cap U_2\), there is an open neighbourhood \(W\) of \(y\) principal in both. Indeed, choose \(a\in B_1\) with \(y\in D_{U_1}(a)\subseteq U_1\cap U_2\); \(D_{U_1}(a)\) is an open subset of \(U_2\), so there is \(b\in B_2\) with \(y\in D_{U_2}(b)\subseteq D_{U_1}(a)\). Now \(b\) restricts to an element \(b|\in\mathcal O(D_{U_1}(a))=(B_1)_a\), say \(b|=b'/a^N\) with \(b'\in B_1\). Then \(D_{U_2}(b)=D_{U_1}(a)\cap D(b|)=D_{U_1}(ab')\), which is principal in both.

\(Y\) is quasi-compact; cover it by finitely many affine opens \(Y_\alpha=\Spec B_\alpha\), with \(B_\alpha\) finitely generated \(R\)-algebras (hence Noetherian). Each \(Y_\alpha\cap Y_\beta\) is an open subset of the Noetherian space \(Y_\alpha\), so quasi-compact; by the preceding paragraph it is a finite union of opens \(W_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=D_{Y_\alpha}(b_{\alpha\beta\gamma})=D_{Y_\beta}(b_{\beta\alpha\gamma})\) (\(\gamma\in\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}\)). The identity of \(W_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\) induces isomorphisms of \(R\)-algebras \(\rho_{\alpha\beta\gamma}:(B_\beta)_{b_{\beta\alpha\gamma}}\to(B_\alpha)_{b_{\alpha\beta\gamma}}\), inverse to the corresponding \(\rho_{\beta\alpha\gamma}\).

Step 2: descent. Choose \(g\notin\mathfrak p\) and finitely presented \(A_g\)-algebras \(B^0_\alpha\) with \(B^0_\alpha\otimes R=B_\alpha\) and lifts \(b^0_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\in B^0_\alpha\) of the \(b_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\) (possible since each is a finite set of finitely many elements, with finitely many relations). Put \(X_\alpha=\Spec B^0_\alpha\) and \(X_{\alpha\beta}=\bigcup_\gamma D(b^0_{\alpha\beta\gamma})\subseteq X_\alpha\). Using Lemma (i),(iii) (the rings \((B^0_\alpha)_{b^0}\) are of finite type over \(A_g\) and their \(R\)-base change is \((B_\alpha)_b\)) and shrinking \(g\) finitely often, we find \(A_g\)-isomorphisms \(\rho^0_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\) with \(\rho^0\otimes R=\rho\) and \(\rho^0_{\beta\alpha\gamma}=(\rho^0_{\alpha\beta\gamma})^{-1}\); let \(\theta^0_{\alpha\beta\gamma}:D(b^0_{\alpha\beta\gamma})\to D(b^0_{\beta\alpha\gamma})\subseteq X_\beta\) be the isomorphism of schemes they define. Two such maps for \(\gamma,\gamma'\) agree on \(D(b^0_{\alpha\beta\gamma}b^0_{\alpha\beta\gamma'})\) after \(\otimes R\) (both are induced by the identity of \(Y\)), these are morphisms from an affine scheme to the affine scheme \(X_\beta\), i.e. ring homomorphisms, so they agree after shrinking \(g\) (Lemma (ii)). Thus they glue to \(\theta_{\alpha\beta}:X_{\alpha\beta}\to X_{\beta\alpha}\), an isomorphism with inverse \(\theta_{\beta\alpha}\) and \(\theta_{\alpha\beta}\otimes R=\mathrm{Id}\) on \(Y_\alpha\cap Y_\beta\). The cocycle conditions \(\theta_{\alpha\beta}(X_{\alpha\beta}\cap X_{\alpha\delta})=X_{\beta\alpha}\cap X_{\beta\delta}\) and \(\theta_{\beta\delta}\theta_{\alpha\beta}=\theta_{\alpha\delta}\) on \(X_{\alpha\beta}\cap X_{\alpha\delta}\) are finitely many equalities between open subsets of the \(X_\beta\) and between morphisms from affine pieces (finitely many principal opens of \(X_\alpha\)) to affine schemes; all hold after \(\otimes R\), hence after shrinking \(g\) by Lemma (iv) and (ii). Replacing \(U\) by \(D(g)\) so that all this holds, the \(X_\alpha\) glue to a scheme \(X\to U:=\Spec A_g\) which is of finite type (finitely many \(X_\alpha\) of finite type), and \(X\times_U\Spec R\) is obtained by gluing the \(Y_\alpha\) along \(Y_\alpha\cap Y_\beta\) by the identity, i.e. it is \(Y\).

Step 3: separatedness. Suppose \(Y\) separated over \(R\). Let \(G=\{\mathfrak q\in\Spec A_g:\mathfrak q\subseteq\mathfrak p\}\) be the set of generizations of \(s\), closed under generization. For a \(U\)-scheme \(T\), the map \(T_R\to T\) is a homeomorphism onto \(\pi_T^{-1}(G)\) with the subspace topology (it is \(\Spec S^{-1}C\to\Spec C\) on affine opens). Put \(P=X\times_UX\), so \(P_R=Y\times_RY\). Since \(\Delta_{Y/R}=\Delta_{X/U}\times_UR\), the set \(D:=\Delta(X)\cap\pi_P^{-1}(G)\) is the image of \(\Delta_{Y/R}\), which is closed in \(\pi_P^{-1}(G)\) by hypothesis.

Let \(Z\) be the closure of \(\Delta(X)\) in \(P\). The open set \(O=\bigcup_\alpha X_\alpha\times_UX_\alpha\) contains \(\Delta(X)\) and \(\Delta(X)\cap(X_\alpha\times_UX_\alpha)=\Delta(X_\alpha)\) is closed in it by Prop. 3.4; so \(\Delta(X)=F\cap O\) with \(F\) closed, hence \(Z\subseteq F\) and \(Z\cap O=\Delta(X)\). Therefore \(T:=Z\setminus\Delta(X)=Z\setminus O\) is closed in \(P\).

\(T\cap\pi_P^{-1}(G)=\varnothing\). The space \(X\) is Noetherian with irreducible components having generic points \(x_1,\dots,x_m\), and \(Z=\bigcup_j\overline{\{\Delta(x_j)\}}\). If \(z\in Z\cap\pi_P^{-1}(G)\), then \(z\in\overline{\{\Delta(x_j)\}}\) for some \(j\); so \(\Delta(x_j)\) is a generization of \(z\) and lies in \(\pi_P^{-1}(G)\), thus in \(D\). As \(D\) is closed in \(\pi_P^{-1}(G)\) and \(z\in\overline{\{\Delta(x_j)\}}\cap\pi_P^{-1}(G)\), we get \(z\in D\subseteq\Delta(X)\), so \(z\notin T\).

The Noetherian space \(T\) has finitely many irreducible components \(T_i\) with generic points \(\tau_i\), and \(\pi_P(\tau_i)=\mathfrak q_i\notin G\), i.e. \(\mathfrak q_i\not\subseteq\mathfrak p\). Pick \(h_i\in\mathfrak q_i\setminus\mathfrak p\) and \(h=\prod h_i\). Then \(\pi_P(T_i)\subseteq V(\mathfrak q_i)\) misses \(D(h)\), so \(T\cap\pi_P^{-1}(D(h))=\varnothing\). Over \(D(h)\) we get that \(\Delta(X)\cap\pi_P^{-1}(D(h))=Z\cap\pi_P^{-1}(D(h))\) is closed, i.e. the diagonal of \(X\times_UD(h)\to D(h)\) has closed image, so this morphism is separated by Cor. 3.5. Replace \(U\) by \(D(h)\); still \(X_R=Y\). ∎

Exercise 3.10

Problem

(Graph of a morphism) Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of \(S\)-schemes. We define the graph \(\Gamma_f\) of \(f\) as being the image of \((\mathrm{Id}_X,f):X\to X\times_SY\).

  1. (a)

    Show that \((\mathrm{Id}_X,f)\) is a section of the projection morphism \(X\times_SY\to X\). Deduce from this that \(\Gamma_f\) is a closed subset of \(X\times_SY\) if \(Y\) is separated over \(S\).

  2. (b)

    Let us suppose \(X\) is reduced and let us endow \(\Gamma_f\) with the structure of a reduced closed subscheme. Show that if \(Y\) is separated over \(S\), then the projection \(X\times_SY\to X\) induces an isomorphism from \(\Gamma_f\) onto \(X\).

Solution

(a) Let \(p:X\times_SY\to X\) be the first projection. By definition \(p\circ(\mathrm{Id}_X,f)=\mathrm{Id}_X\), so \(\gamma:=(\mathrm{Id}_X,f)\) is a section of \(p\). If \(Y\to S\) is separated, so is its base change \(p:X\times_SY\to X\) (Prop. 3.9(c)), and by Ex. 3.6 the section \(\gamma\) is a closed immersion. In particular \(\Gamma_f=\gamma(X)\) is closed.

(b) Let \(X\) be reduced and \(Y/S\) separated; \(\gamma:X\to P:=X\times_SY\) is a closed immersion, defining a closed subscheme \(\Gamma'\) of \(P\) with \(\Gamma'\simeq X\) via \(\gamma\). This subscheme is reduced and has underlying set \(\Gamma_f\). The reduced closed subscheme structure on a closed subset is unique (its ideal sheaf is the radical ideal of the set). Hence \(\Gamma'=\Gamma_f\) and \(\gamma\) induces an isomorphism \(j:X\to\Gamma_f\). Then \(p|_{\Gamma_f}\circ j=p\circ\gamma=\mathrm{Id}_X\), so \(p|_{\Gamma_f}=j^{-1}\) is an isomorphism \(\Gamma_f\to X\). ∎

Exercise 3.11

Problem

Let \(X\) be a \(Y\)-scheme.

  1. (a)

    Show that for any \(Y\)-scheme \(Z\), \(X_{\rm red}\times_YZ\to X\times_YZ\) is a closed immersion and a homeomorphism on the underlying topological spaces. Deduce from this that \(X\to Y\) separated \(\Leftrightarrow X_{\rm red}\to Y\) separated \(\Leftrightarrow X_{\rm red}\to Y_{\rm red}\) separated.

  2. (b)

    Let \(\{F_i\}_i\) be a finite number of closed subsets of \(X\) such that \(X=\bigcup_iF_i\). Let us endow the \(F_i\) with the structure of reduced closed subschemes. Show that \(X\to Y\) is separated if and only if \(F_i\to Y\) is separated for every \(i\).

  3. (c)

    Let us suppose \(X\) is of finite type over \(Y\). Show that \(X\to Y\) proper \(\Leftrightarrow X_{\rm red}\to Y\) proper \(\Leftrightarrow X_{\rm red}\to Y_{\rm red}\) proper. Also show that (b) is true for proper morphisms (by supposing \(X\to Y\) of finite type).

Solution

(a) Let \(i:X_{\rm red}\to X\) be the canonical closed immersion. We have \(X_{\rm red}\times_YZ=X_{\rm red}\times_X(X\times_YZ)\), so \(i\times\mathrm{Id}_Z\) is a base change of \(i\), hence a closed immersion (Prop. 1.23 and Rem. 1.20). To see it is a homeomorphism, work on affine opens: with \(X=\Spec B\), \(Z=\Spec C\) over \(\Spec A\), the morphism is given by \(B\otimes_AC\to(B/\mathfrak N)\otimes_AC\) (\(\mathfrak N\) the nilradical), which is surjective with kernel the image of \(\mathfrak N\otimes C\), an ideal generated by nilpotent elements and so contained in the nilradical. A closed immersion defined by an ideal inside the nilradical is a homeomorphism (\(V(I)=\Spec\) when \(I\subseteq\mathrm{Nil}\)).
