Equivalences. If \(X\to Y\) is separated, then \(X_{\rm red}\to X\to Y\) is a composite of separated morphisms (closed immersions are separated, Prop. 3.9(a),(b)). Conversely, let \(X_{\rm red}\to Y\) be separated. The morphism \(i\times i:X_{\rm red}\times_YX_{\rm red}\to X\times_YX\) is a composite of two morphisms as in the first part, so a closed immersion, and \(\Delta_X\circ i=(i\times i)\circ\Delta_{X_{\rm red}}\). As \(i\) is surjective, \(\Delta_X(X)=(i\times i)(\Delta_{X_{\rm red}}(X_{\rm red}))\) is closed in \(X\times_YX\) (image of a closed subset by a closed immersion). By Cor. 3.5, \(X\to Y\) is separated. Next, \(X_{\rm red}\to Y\) factors through \(Y_{\rm red}\), and \(Y_{\rm red}\to Y\) is a closed immersion, hence separated. If \(X_{\rm red}\to Y_{\rm red}\) is separated then so is the composite \(X_{\rm red}\to Y\) (Prop. 3.9(b)); if \(X_{\rm red}\to Y\) is separated, so is \(X_{\rm red}\to Y_{\rm red}\) (Prop. 3.9(e)).

(b) Let \(j_i:F_i\to X\) be the closed immersions. If \(X\to Y\) is separated, so is \(F_i\to X\to Y\). Conversely suppose all \(F_i\to Y\) separated. The morphism \(j_i\times j_i:F_i\times_YF_i\to X\times_YX\) is a closed immersion (composite of base changes of \(j_i\)), and \(\Delta_X\circ j_i=(j_i\times j_i)\circ\Delta_{F_i}\). Hence \(\Delta_X(F_i)=(j_i\times j_i)(\Delta_{F_i}(F_i))\) is closed. Then \(\Delta_X(X)=\bigcup_i\Delta_X(F_i)\) is a finite union of closed sets, hence closed. By Cor. 3.5, \(X\to Y\) is separated.

(c) Recall proper = finite type + separated + universally closed. Let \(X\to Y\) be of finite type.

First equivalence. If \(X\to Y\) is proper, so is \(X_{\rm red}\to X\to Y\) (closed immersions are proper, Prop. 3.16(a),(b)). Conversely, let \(X_{\rm red}\to Y\) be proper. Then \(X\to Y\) is of finite type by hypothesis and separated by (a). For any \(Y\)-scheme \(Z\), let \(h:X_{\rm red}\times_YZ\to X\times_YZ\) be the homeomorphism above. The projection of \(X_{\rm red}\times_YZ\) to \(Z\) is closed and equals \(\mathrm{pr}_Z\circ h\); so for a closed \(C\subseteq X\times_YZ\), \(\mathrm{pr}_Z(C)=\mathrm{pr}_Z(h(h^{-1}C))\) is closed. So \(X\to Y\) is universally closed, hence proper.

Second equivalence. If \(X_{\rm red}\to Y\) is proper, then since \(Y_{\rm red}\to Y\) is separated, \(X_{\rm red}\to Y_{\rm red}\) is proper by Prop. 3.16(e). If \(X_{\rm red}\to Y_{\rm red}\) is proper, then composing with the closed immersion \(Y_{\rm red}\to Y\) (proper), \(X_{\rm red}\to Y\) is proper (Prop. 3.16(b)).

Finite closed covers. Let \(X=\bigcup_iF_i\), \(F_i\) reduced closed subschemes. If \(X\to Y\) is proper, each \(F_i\to X\to Y\) is proper. Conversely let all \(F_i\to Y\) be proper. Then \(X\to Y\) is of finite type (hypothesis) and separated by (b). For a \(Y\)-scheme \(Z\) let \(C\subseteq X\times_YZ\) be closed. The morphisms \(F_i\times_YZ\to X\times_YZ\) are closed immersions whose images \(E_i\) (preimages of \(F_i\) under the projection to \(X\)) cover \(X\times_YZ\). Since \(F_i\times_YZ\to Z\) is closed and \(C\cap E_i\) corresponds to a closed subset of \(F_i\times_YZ\), the set \(\mathrm{pr}_Z(C\cap E_i)\) is closed. Hence \(\mathrm{pr}_Z(C)=\bigcup_i\mathrm{pr}_Z(C\cap E_i)\) is closed. So \(X\to Y\) is universally closed and thus proper. ∎

Exercise 3.12

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a surjective morphism. We suppose that \(Y\) is connected and that all of the fibers \(X_y\) are connected.

  1. (a)

    Show that if \(X\) is proper over \(Y\), then \(X\) is connected.

  2. (b)

    Study the example \(\Spec k[T_1,T_2]/T_1(T_1T_2-1)\to\Spec k[T_1]\), where \(k\) is a field. Deduce from this that (a) is false if \(f\) is not proper.

Solution

(a) Suppose \(X=X_1\sqcup X_2\) with \(X_1,X_2\) open and closed (hence both closed). Since \(f\) is proper it is a closed map, so \(f(X_1)\) and \(f(X_2)\) are closed in \(Y\). For \(y\in Y\) the fibre \(X_y\) is nonempty (surjectivity); its underlying space is \(f^{-1}(y)\) with the subspace topology, so \(f^{-1}(y)=(f^{-1}(y)\cap X_1)\sqcup(f^{-1}(y)\cap X_2)\) is a decomposition into disjoint open and closed subsets of the connected set \(f^{-1}(y)\). Hence \(f^{-1}(y)\) lies entirely in \(X_1\) or entirely in \(X_2\), so \(y\) belongs to exactly one of \(f(X_1)\), \(f(X_2)\). Thus \(Y=f(X_1)\sqcup f(X_2)\) is a disjoint union of two closed sets. As \(Y\) is connected, one of them is empty, say \(f(X_2)=\varnothing\), so \(X_2=\varnothing\). Therefore \(X\) is connected.

(b) Put \(X=\Spec k[T_1,T_2]/(T_1(T_1T_2-1))\) and \(Y=\Spec k[T_1]=\mathbb A^1_k\), which is connected. Fibres: for the point \(y=(T_1)\) the residue field is \(k\) and \(X_y=\Spec k[T_2]\), a line, connected. For every other point \(y\) of \(\mathbb A^1_k\) (generic point or closed point \((\pi)\), \(\pi\ne T_1\) irreducible), \(T_1\) is a unit in \(\kappa=k(y)\) and \(X_y=\Spec\kappa[T_2]/(T_1(T_1T_2-1))=\Spec\kappa[T_2]/(T_2-T_1^{-1})=\Spec\kappa\), a point. So \(f\) is surjective with connected fibres. But \(X\) is not connected: the ideals \((T_1)\) and \((T_1T_2-1)\) of \(k[T_1,T_2]\) are comaximal (\(T_1\cdot T_2-(T_1T_2-1)=1\)), so by the Chinese remainder theorem

\[ k[T_1,T_2]/(T_1(T_1T_2-1))\simeq k[T_2]\times k[T_1,T_1^{-1}], \]

and \(X\) is the disjoint union of two nonempty open and closed pieces. Hence the conclusion of (a) fails; by (a), \(f\) is therefore not proper (indeed the image of the closed piece \(V(T_1T_2-1)\) is \(D(T_1)\), which is not closed in \(Y\)). ∎

Exercise 3.13

Problem

(Rational maps) Let \(X,Y\) be schemes over \(S\) which are integral, with \(Y\to S\) separated. A rational map from \(X\) to \(Y\), denoted by \(X\dashrightarrow Y\), is an equivalence class of morphisms of \(S\)-schemes from a non-empty open subscheme of \(X\) to \(Y\). Two such morphisms \(U\to Y\), \(V\to Y\) are called equivalent if they coincide on \(U\cap V\). Let us fix a rational map \(X\dashrightarrow Y\).

  1. (a)

    Show that in every equivalence class, there exists a unique element \(f:U\to Y\) such that \(U\) is maximal for the inclusion relation, and that every element \(g:V\to Y\) of the class verifies \(g=f|_V\). We call \(U\) the domain of definition of \(X\dashrightarrow Y\). We then denote the rational map associated to \(f\) by \(f:X\dashrightarrow Y\).

  2. (b)

    Let us suppose that \(f\) is dominant, that \(X,Y\) are of finite type over \(S\), and that \(S\) is locally Noetherian. Let \(x\in X\). Show that \(f\) is defined at \(x\) (i.e., \(x\in U\)) if and only if there exists a \(y\in Y\) such that the image of \(\mathcal O_{Y,y}\) under \(K(Y)\to K(X)\) is a local ring dominated by \(\mathcal O_{X,x}\).

  3. (c)

    Let \(\Gamma_f\) be the closure in \(X\times_SY\) of the graph of \(f:U\to Y\). We call it the graph of the rational map \(f\). We endow \(\Gamma_f\) with the structure of a reduced closed subscheme. Show that \(\Gamma_f\) is integral and that the projection \(p:X\times_SY\to X\) induces a birational morphism from \(\Gamma_f\) to \(X\).

  4. (d)

    Show that there exist a birational morphism \(g:Z\to X\) and a morphism \(\tilde f:Z\to Y\) such that \(\tilde f=f\circ g\) on \(g^{-1}(U)\) (we say that we have eliminated the indetermination of \(X\dashrightarrow Y\)). Moreover, if \(Y\) is proper (resp. projective) over \(S\), we can choose \(g\) proper (resp. projective).

Solution

Preliminary: equivalence is an equivalence relation. Reflexivity and symmetry are clear. Let \(f:U\to Y\), \(g:V\to Y\), \(h:W\to Y\) with \(f=g\) on \(U\cap V\) and \(g=h\) on \(V\cap W\). Then \(f=h\) on \(U\cap V\cap W\), which is a nonempty open (as \(X\) is irreducible) and thus dense subset of \(U\cap W\). Since \(U\cap W\) is reduced and \(Y/S\) is separated, Prop. 3.11 gives \(f=h\) on \(U\cap W\).

(a) Let \(\mathcal C\) be the class and \(U_0\) the union of the domains of its members. Any two members agree on the intersection of their domains (by definition of equivalence, as the class is an equivalence class), so the members glue to a morphism of \(S\)-schemes \(f:U_0\to Y\). Then \(f|_V=g\) for each member \(g:V\to Y\), so \(f\in\mathcal C\) and \(f\) has maximal domain. If \(f':U'\to Y\) is another member with maximal domain, then \(U'\subseteq U_0\) and \(f'=f|_{U'}\); maximality of \(U'\) forces \(U'=U_0\) and \(f'=f\).

(c) Let \(U\) be the domain of \(f\), nonempty, dense and irreducible. Since \(Y/S\) is separated, \(\gamma=(\mathrm{Id}_U,f):U\to U\times_SY\) is a closed immersion (Ex. 3.10(a)); its image \(\Gamma_U\) is closed in the open subset \(p^{-1}(U)=U\times_SY\) of \(X\times_SY\). Let \(\Gamma_f=\overline{\Gamma_U}\). It is irreducible (closure of the image of an irreducible space), and reduced by definition, so it is integral. Moreover \(\Gamma_f\cap p^{-1}(U)=\Gamma_U\) (closure in an open subset \(=\overline{\Gamma_U}\cap p^{-1}(U)\), and \(\Gamma_U\) is closed there). Hence the open subscheme \(\Gamma_f\cap p^{-1}(U)\) of \(\Gamma_f\) is the reduced closed subscheme of \(U\times_SY\) supported on \(\Gamma_U\), and by Ex. 3.10(b) (applied to \(f|_U\), \(U\) reduced) \(p\) restricts to an isomorphism \(\Gamma_f\cap p^{-1}(U)\to U\), with inverse \(\gamma\). As \(p^{-1}(U)\cap\Gamma_f\) and \(U\) are dense open in the integral schemes \(\Gamma_f\), \(X\), the morphism \(p|_{\Gamma_f}:\Gamma_f\to X\) is birational (an isomorphism above the dense open subset \(U\), so also an isomorphism of function fields).

(d) Take \(Z=\Gamma_f\), \(g=p|_{\Gamma_f}\) and \(\tilde f=q|_{\Gamma_f}\), \(q:X\times_SY\to Y\) the second projection. By (c), \(g\) is birational. Over \(U\), \(g^{-1}(U)=\Gamma_U\) with \(g^{-1}=\gamma\) (identified with \(U\)), so \(\tilde f\circ\gamma=q\circ\gamma=f\), i.e. \(\tilde f=f\circ g\) on \(g^{-1}(U)\). If \(Y\to S\) is proper (resp. projective), then so is its base change \(p:X\times_SY\to X\) (Prop. 3.16(c), Cor. 3.32(c)), and \(g\) is the composite of the closed immersion \(\Gamma_f\to X\times_SY\) and \(p\); closed immersions are proper and projective (Prop. 3.16(a), Cor. 3.32(a)) and compositions preserve both (Prop. 3.16(b), Cor. 3.32(b)). So \(g\) is proper (resp. projective).

(b): the statement, a counterexample, and a corrected version. Let \(f:U\to Y\) be dominant, so \(f(\eta_X)=\eta_Y\) (Exercise 2.4.11) and \(f\) induces \(\varphi:K(Y)=\mathcal O_{Y,\eta_Y}\to\mathcal O_{X,\eta_X}=K(X)\), independent of the representative. “The image of \(\mathcal O_{Y,y}\) is a local ring dominated by \(\mathcal O_{X,x}\)” means \(\varphi(\mathcal O_{Y,y})\subseteq\mathcal O_{X,x}\) and \(\varphi(\mathfrak m_y)\subseteq\mathfrak m_x\).

As printed, the “if” direction is false when \(S\) is not separated. Let \(S\) be the affine line over \(k\) with doubled origin \(0_1,0_2\) (locally Noetherian), \(X=S_1\) and \(Y=S_2\) the two open copies of \(\mathbb A^1_k\) in \(S\), with the inclusions as structure morphisms. Both are integral and of finite type over \(S\), and \(Y\to S\) is separated (an open immersion). The inclusion \(U=S_1\setminus\{0_1\}=S_2\setminus\{0_2\}\to Y\) is an \(S\)-morphism, dominant, with \(K(Y)=K(X)=k(T)\) and \(\varphi=\mathrm{id}\). For \(x=0_1\) and \(y=0_2\) we have \(\mathcal O_{Y,y}=k[T]_{(T)}=\mathcal O_{X,x}\) and \(\varphi\) is a local isomorphism. But \(f\) is not defined at \(x\): an \(S\)-morphism \(V\to Y\) with \(V\ni0_1\) open in \(X\) would have to satisfy that \(V\to Y\to S\) equals the inclusion \(V\subseteq S_1\subseteq S\), whose image contains \(0_1\), while the image of \(Y\to S\) does not contain \(0_1\).

Corrected statement. \(f\) is defined at \(x\) if and only if there is \(y\in Y\) lying over the same point of \(S\) as \(x\) such that \(\varphi(\mathcal O_{Y,y})\subseteq\mathcal O_{X,x}\) and \(\varphi(\mathfrak m_y)\subseteq\mathfrak m_x\). If \(S\) is separated, the condition on the image in \(S\) is automatic: let \(T=\Spec\mathcal O_{X,x}\), \(a:T\to X\to S\) and \(b:T\to\Spec\mathcal O_{Y,y}\to Y\to S\) (the latter using the local homomorphism \(\mathcal O_{Y,y}\to\mathcal O_{X,x}\) induced by \(\varphi\)). They coincide on the generic point of \(T\) because \(\varphi\) is compatible with \(S\); the set \((a,b)^{-1}(\Delta_S(S))\) is closed in \(T\) and contains the generic point, so it is \(T\); evaluating at the closed point gives a point \(\Delta_S(s)\) whose two projections are the images of \(x\) and of \(y\) in \(S\), and these projections coincide (Rem. 3.3), so \(x\) and \(y\) lie over the same point of \(S\). The hypotheses “finite type” and “locally Noetherian” are not needed below beyond finite generation of affine algebras.

(\(\Rightarrow\)) If \(x\in U\) and \(y=f(x)\), then \(y\) lies over the same point of \(S\) as \(x\) since \(f\) is an \(S\)-morphism. The local homomorphism \(f^\#_x:\mathcal O_{Y,y}\to\mathcal O_{X,x}\) is compatible with the maps to the fraction fields (the generic points specialize to \(x\), \(y\)), so it is the restriction of \(\varphi\); local means \(\varphi(\mathfrak m_y)\subseteq\mathfrak m_x\).

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Let \(t\in S\) be the common image of \(x\) and \(y\). Choose an affine open \(S'=\Spec\Lambda\ni t\), \(\Lambda\) Noetherian, an affine open \(Y'=\Spec B\ni y\) over \(S'\) and an affine open \(\Spec A\ni x\) over \(S'\), so \(B=\Lambda[b_1,\dots,b_m]\) is a finitely generated \(\Lambda\)-algebra with \(B\subseteq K(Y)\), \(A\subseteq K(X)\) (integrality). By hypothesis \(\varphi(b_i)\in\mathcal O_{X,x}=A_{\mathfrak p_x}\), and since \(\varphi\) is \(S\)-compatible, \(\varphi\) maps \(\Lambda\) into the image of \(\Lambda\) in \(A\). So there is \(g\in A\setminus\mathfrak p_x\) with \(\varphi(B)\subseteq A_g\), giving a \(\Lambda\)-algebra homomorphism \(B\to A_g\) and an \(S\)-morphism \(F:D(g)\to Y'\subseteq Y\), with \(F(\eta_X)=\eta_Y\). We show \(F\) is equivalent to \(f\). The open set \(W_0=D(g)\cap U\cap f^{-1}(Y')\) contains \(\eta_X\); choose a nonempty affine open \(W_1=\Spec A_1\subseteq W_0\). Then \(F|_{W_1}\) and \(f|_{W_1}\) correspond to \(\Lambda\)-algebra homomorphisms \(B\to A_1\) whose composites with \(A_1\hookrightarrow K(X)\) both equal \(\varphi|_B\). So they are equal: \(F=f\) on \(W_1\). As \(W_1\) is dense in \(D(g)\cap U\), \(X\) is reduced and \(Y/S\) separated, Prop. 3.11 yields \(F=f\) on \(D(g)\cap U\). Thus \(F\) belongs to the class of \(f\) and by (a) \(D(g)\subseteq U\), so \(x\in U\). ∎

Exercise 3.14

Problem

Let \(X,Y\) be integral separated schemes over a Noetherian scheme \(S\). Let \(f:X\dashrightarrow Y\) be a birational map (i.e., \(f\) comes from an isomorphism from a non-empty open subset \(U\subseteq X\) onto an open subset of \(Y\)). Show that \(f\) is defined everywhere if and only if \(\Gamma_f\to X\) is an isomorphism.

Solution

Let \(U_{\max}\) be the domain of definition of \(f\), and \(p:\Gamma_f\to X\) the projection (Ex. 3.13(c)). Only the separatedness of \(Y\) over \(S\) is used (it is what Ex. 3.10 and 3.13 need); the facts that \(f\) is birational and that \(S\) is Noetherian are not used.

(\(\Rightarrow\)) Suppose \(U_{\max}=X\), so \(f:X\to Y\) is a morphism. The graph \(\gamma(X)\) of \(f\), \(\gamma=(\mathrm{Id}_X,f)\), is closed in \(X\times_SY\) (Ex. 3.10(a)), contains the graph \(\Gamma_{U'}\) of the restriction to any dense open \(U'\), and \(\Gamma_{U'}=\gamma(U')\) is dense in \(\gamma(X)\simeq X\) (as \(\gamma\) is a homeomorphism onto its image and \(U'\) is dense in \(X\)). So \(\Gamma_f=\overline{\Gamma_{U'}}=\gamma(X)\), the reduced closed subscheme on it. By Ex. 3.10(b) (\(X\) reduced, \(Y/S\) separated), \(p:\Gamma_f\to X\) is an isomorphism.

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Suppose \(p:\Gamma_f\to X\) is an isomorphism. Put \(\tilde f=q\circ p^{-1}:X\to Y\), a morphism of \(S\)-schemes. By Ex. 3.13(d) (with \(Z=\Gamma_f\), \(g=p\)), \(\tilde f\circ p=q|_{\Gamma_f}=f\circ p\) on \(p^{-1}(U_{\max})\); hence \(\tilde f|_{U_{\max}}=f\). So \(\tilde f\) is a member of the class of \(f\) defined on all of \(X\), and by Ex. 3.13(a) the domain of definition is \(X\). ∎

Exercise 3.15

Problem

We say that a morphism of schemes \(f:X\to Y\) is finite (resp. integral) if for every affine open subset \(V\) of \(Y\), \(f^{-1}(V)\) is affine and \(\mathcal{O}_X(f^{-1}(V))\) is finite (resp. integral) over \(\mathcal{O}_Y(V)\). Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism such that there exists an affine open covering \(Y=\bigcup_iY_i\) with \(f^{-1}(Y_i)\) affine and \(\mathcal{O}_X(f^{-1}(Y_i))\) finite over \(\mathcal{O}_Y(Y_i)\). We want to show that \(f\) is finite. (a) Show that we can reduce to the case when \(Y\) is affine and that \(Y_i=D(h_i)\) is a principal open subset. (b) Show that \(X\) then verifies condition (3.2), Subsection 2.3.1. Deduce from this that the canonical morphism \(X\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_X(X)\) is an isomorphism (hence \(X\) is affine). (c) Show that \(\mathcal{O}_X(X)\) is finite over \(\mathcal{O}_Y(Y)\).

Solution

Conventions: results of the same chapter are cited by their number (“Prop. 3.9”, “Prop. 1.2” for Section 3.1, “Prop. 2.4” for Section 3.2), results of earlier chapters with the chapter prefix (“Prop. 2.3.25”); “Ex. 2.5” means Exercise 2.5 of Section 3.2, “Ex. 1.8” is an exercise of Section 3.1, “Ex. 2.3.17” is Exercise 3.17 of Section 2.3 (Chapter 2), and “Ex. 3.17” is an exercise of the present section. The \(\mathcal{O}_Y(W)\)-module structure on \(\mathcal{O}_X(f^{-1}(W))\) is always given by \(f^\#(W)\). For a ring homomorphism \(\psi:A\to B\) and \(g\in A\), the morphism \(\operatorname{Spec}B\to\operatorname{Spec}A\) pulls \(D(g)\) back to \(D(\psi(g))\), and \(\mathcal{O}(D(\psi(g)))=B_{\psi(g)}\) is the localization of the \(A\)-algebra \(B\) at (the image of) \(g\) (Prop. 2.3.14, Lemma 2.3.7).

(a) Reduction. Let \(V\) be an affine open subset of \(Y\). We must show that \(f^{-1}(V)\) is affine and \(\mathcal{O}_X(f^{-1}(V))\) is finite over \(\mathcal{O}_Y(V)\).

Claim: \(V\) is a finite union of open sets \(D\) such that \(D=D_V(h)\) for some \(h\in\mathcal{O}_Y(V)\) and also \(D=D_{Y_i}(g)\) for some \(i\) and some \(g\in\mathcal{O}_Y(Y_i)\). Let \(y\in V\) and choose \(i\) with \(y\in Y_i\). The principal open sets form a base of the affine scheme \(Y_i\), so there is \(g\in\mathcal{O}_Y(Y_i)\) with \(y\in D_{Y_i}(g)\subseteq V\cap Y_i\). Now \(D_{Y_i}(g)\) is open in the affine scheme \(V\), so there is \(h\in\mathcal{O}_Y(V)\) with \(y\in D_V(h)\subseteq D_{Y_i}(g)\). Write \(h|_{D_{Y_i}(g)}=a/g^m\in\mathcal{O}_Y(Y_i)_g\) with \(a\in\mathcal{O}_Y(Y_i)\). Then \(D_V(h)\) is the set of points of \(D_{Y_i}(g)=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_Y(Y_i)_g\) where \(a/g^m\) is a unit, that is \(D_V(h)=D_{Y_i}(ag)\). Since \(V\) is quasi-compact (Ex. 2.1.6), finitely many such sets cover \(V\). This proves the claim.

Let \(D=D_V(h)=D_{Y_i}(g)\) be one of these sets and \(X_i=f^{-1}(Y_i)=\operatorname{Spec}B_i\), \(A_i=\mathcal{O}_Y(Y_i)\). By the preliminary remark, \(f^{-1}(D)=D_{X_i}(g)\) is affine, with ring \((B_i)_g\), which is finite over \((A_i)_g=\mathcal{O}_Y(D)\) (a localization of a finite module is finite: generators stay generators). So the hypothesis of the exercise holds for the morphism \(f^{-1}(V)\to V\) with the covering \(\{D_V(h_k)\}_{k=1}^n\) of \(V\). This is the required reduction: it suffices to prove the following statement.

(S) Let \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}A\) be affine and \(h_1,\dots,h_n\in A\) with \(D(h_1)\cup\dots\cup D(h_n)=Y\) (equivalently \((h_1,\dots,h_n)=A\)). Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism such that \(X_k:=f^{-1}(D(h_k))\) is affine and \(\mathcal{O}_X(X_k)\) is finite over \(A_{h_k}\) for every \(k\). Then \(X\) is affine and \(B:=\mathcal{O}_X(X)\) is finite over \(A\).

(b) Write \(h_k\) also for \(f^\#(Y)(h_k)\in B\). Then \(X_k=X_{h_k}\) in the notation of Def. 2.3.11. Indeed, let \(x\in X\) and \(y=f(x)\). The homomorphism \(f^\#_x:\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}\to\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\) is local, so the preimage of \(\mathfrak{m}_x\) is \(\mathfrak{m}_y\), and therefore \((h_k)_x\) is a unit of \(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\) iff \((h_k)_y\notin\mathfrak m_y\) iff \(y\in D(h_k)\).

Condition (3.2) holds: the \(X_k\) form a finite covering of \(X\) by affine open sets, and \(X_k\cap X_l=f^{-1}(D(h_kh_l))\) is the principal open subset of the affine scheme \(X_k\) defined by (the restriction of) \(h_l\), hence is affine (Lemma 2.3.7) and has the trivial finite affine covering by itself.

By Prop. 2.3.12, restriction induces isomorphisms of \(A\)-algebras \(B_{h_k}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{O}_X(X_{h_k})=\mathcal{O}_X(X_k)\).

Let \(\pi:X\to\operatorname{Spec}B\) be the canonical morphism, corresponding to \(\operatorname{id}_B\) under Prop. 2.3.25. By the construction in Prop. 2.3.25, \(\pi(x)\) is the prime ideal of \(B\) which is the preimage of \(\mathfrak{m}_x\) under \(B\to\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\). So for \(b\in B\) we have \(\pi(x)\in D(b)\) iff \(b_x\notin\mathfrak m_x\), i.e. \(\pi^{-1}(D(b))=X_b\). In particular \(\pi^{-1}(D(h_k))=X_k\). The restriction \(\pi_k:X_k\to D(h_k)=\operatorname{Spec}B_{h_k}\) is a morphism of affine schemes. The ring homomorphism \(\pi^\#(D(h_k)):B_{h_k}\to\mathcal{O}_X(X_k)\) compatible with restrictions satisfies \(\pi^\#(D(h_k))\circ(B\to B_{h_k})=(\text{restriction }B\to\mathcal{O}_X(X_k))\), because \(\pi^\#(Y')=\operatorname{id}_B\) for \(Y'=\operatorname{Spec}B\). By the universal property of localization, it is the isomorphism of Prop. 2.3.12. A morphism between affine schemes inducing an isomorphism on rings of global sections is an isomorphism (Prop. 2.3.25). So \(\pi_k\) is an isomorphism for every \(k\).

The elements \(h_k\in B\) generate the unit ideal of \(B\) (they do already in \(A\)), so the sets \(D(h_k)\) cover \(\operatorname{Spec}B\). As \(\pi\) is an isomorphism over each member of an open covering of the target, it is an isomorphism. Hence \(X\simeq\operatorname{Spec}B\) is affine.

(c) We use the following lemma, which we also use in Ex. 3.16.

Lemma L. Let \(A\) be a ring, \(h_1,\dots,h_n\in A\) with \((h_1,\dots,h_n)=A\), and \(M\) an \(A\)-module such that \(M_{h_k}\) is a finite \(A_{h_k}\)-module for each \(k\). Then \(M\) is a finite \(A\)-module.

Proof. A generator of \(M_{h_k}\) has the form \(m/h_k^e\) with \(m\in M\); since \(h_k^{-e}\in A_{h_k}\), the elements \(m/1\) also generate. So there are finitely many \(m_{kj}\in M\) whose images generate \(M_{h_k}\) for each \(k\). Let \(M'\) be the submodule generated by all \(m_{kj}\), and \(Q=M/M'\). By exactness of localization \(Q_{h_k}=M_{h_k}/M'_{h_k}=0\). For \(q\in Q\) there is \(e\) (the same for all \(k\)) with \(h_k^eq=0\) for all \(k\). The elements \(h_k^e\) again generate the unit ideal (a high power of \((h_1,\dots,h_n)\) lies in \((h_1^e,\dots,h_n^e)\)), so \(1=\sum_kc_kh_k^e\) and \(q=\sum c_kh_k^eq=0\). So \(M=M'\) is finite. \(\square\)

Apply this to \(M=B\) viewed as \(A\)-module: \(B_{h_k}\simeq\mathcal{O}_X(X_k)\) is finite over \(A_{h_k}\). So \(B\) is finite over \(A\). This proves (S). Together with (a), \(f\) is finite. ∎

Exercise 3.16

Problem

Present a statement analogous to that of Exercise 3.15 by replacing finite morphism with integral morphism.

Solution

Statement. Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of schemes such that there is an affine open covering \(Y=\bigcup_iY_i\) with \(f^{-1}(Y_i)\) affine and \(\mathcal{O}_X(f^{-1}(Y_i))\) integral over \(\mathcal{O}_Y(Y_i)\). Then \(f\) is integral, i.e. for every affine open subset \(V\) of \(Y\), \(f^{-1}(V)\) is affine and \(\mathcal{O}_X(f^{-1}(V))\) is integral over \(\mathcal{O}_Y(V)\).

Proof. We follow Ex. 3.15. In (a) of that proof, the only place where finiteness is used is that a localization \((B_i)_g\) of a finite \((A_i)_g\)-module is finite; here \((B_i)_g\) is integral over \((A_i)_g\), because \(b/g^m\) satisfies the equation obtained from an integral equation of \(b\) over \(A_i\) by dividing the coefficients by powers of \(g\). So we reduce to the situation (S) with “integral” instead of “finite”. Part (b) is unchanged (it never uses finiteness). So \(X\simeq\operatorname{Spec}B\), \(B=\mathcal{O}_X(X)\), and \(B_{h_k}\simeq\mathcal{O}_X(X_k)\) is integral over \(A_{h_k}\) for all \(k\).

It remains to show that \(B\) is integral over \(A\). Let \(b\in B\) and \(M=A[b]\subseteq B\) the \(A\)-subalgebra generated by \(b\). Since localization is exact, \(M_{h_k}\) is the subalgebra \(A_{h_k}[b/1]\) of \(B_{h_k}\). As \(b/1\) is integral over \(A_{h_k}\), this subalgebra is a finite \(A_{h_k}\)-module. By Lemma L, \(M=A[b]\) is a finite \(A\)-module, so \(b\) is integral over \(A\) (a finitely generated \(A\)-module \(A[b]\) which is a ring containing \(b\) gives an integral equation by the determinant trick). Hence \(B\) is integral over \(A\). ∎

Exercise 3.17

Problem

Show that the following properties are true. (a) Any finite morphism is of finite type and quasi-finite. (b) The class of finite morphisms verifies hypotheses (1)–(3) of Lemma 3.15. (c) Let \(\rho:A\to B\) be a finite ring homomorphism. Then for any ideal \(I\) of \(B\), we have \((\operatorname{Spec}\rho)(V(I))=V(\rho^{-1}(I))\). (d) Any finite morphism is proper (see also Exercise 3.22).

Solution

(a) Let \(f:X\to Y\) be finite. Cover \(Y\) by affine opens \(Y_j=\operatorname{Spec}A_j\). Then \(f^{-1}(Y_j)=\operatorname{Spec}B_j\) with \(B_j\) finite, hence finitely generated, over \(A_j\). By Prop. 2.2 (the criterion for finite type), \(f\) is of finite type.

Quasi-finiteness (Ex. 2.8): let \(y\in Y\) and choose \(Y_j\ni y\). The fibre is \(X_y=f^{-1}(Y_j)\times_{Y_j}\operatorname{Spec}k(y)=\operatorname{Spec}D\) with \(D=B_j\otimes_{A_j}k(y)\) (Prop. 1.2). This is a finite-dimensional \(k(y)\)-algebra, so it is Artinian, it has only finitely many prime ideals, and \(D\simeq\prod_{\mathfrak q}D_{\mathfrak q}\) is the product of its (local) localizations. Thus \(X_y\) is a finite set, and for \(x\in X_y\) the local ring \(\mathcal{O}_{X_y,x}=D_{\mathfrak q}\) is a direct factor of \(D\), hence finite over \(k(y)\). So \(f\) is quasi-finite.

(b) (1) Closed immersions are finite. Let \(i:Z\to Y\) be a closed immersion and \(V=\operatorname{Spec}A\) an affine open of \(Y\). The restriction \(i^{-1}(V)\to V\) is a closed immersion, so by Prop. 2.3.20 \(i^{-1}(V)\simeq\operatorname{Spec}A/J\) is affine, the ring map \(A\to\mathcal{O}_Z(i^{-1}V)\) being the surjection \(A\to A/J\). Thus \(\mathcal{O}_Z(i^{-1}V)\) is generated by \(1\) as an \(A\)-module.

(2) Composition. Let \(f:X\to Y\) and \(g:Y\to Z\) be finite and \(W=\operatorname{Spec}C\) an affine open of \(Z\). Then \(g^{-1}(W)=\operatorname{Spec}B\) is affine with \(B\) finite over \(C\), and \(f^{-1}(g^{-1}(W))=\operatorname{Spec}D\) is affine since \(\operatorname{Spec}B\) is an affine open of \(Y\), with \(D\) finite over \(B\). So \(D\) is a finite \(C\)-module (a product of generators is a system of generators).

(3) Base change. Let \(f:X\to Y\) be finite, \(g:Y'\to Y\) a morphism, \(X'=X\times_YY'\) and \(f':X'\to Y'\). Let \(V'\) be an affine open subset of \(Y'\). Cover \(V'\) by affine opens \(V'_k=\operatorname{Spec}A'_k\) such that \(g(V'_k)\subseteq V_k\) for an affine open \(V_k=\operatorname{Spec}A_k\) of \(Y\). Then \(f'^{-1}(V'_k)=f^{-1}(V_k)\times_{V_k}V'_k\) (Ex. 1.6(b)) \(=\operatorname{Spec}(B_k\otimes_{A_k}A'_k)\), where \(B_k=\mathcal{O}_X(f^{-1}(V_k))\) is finite over \(A_k\). Hence \(B_k\otimes_{A_k}A'_k\) is finite over \(A'_k=\mathcal{O}_{Y'}(V'_k)\). Ex. 3.15, applied to \(f'^{-1}(V')\to V'\) and the covering \(\{V'_k\}\), shows that \(f'^{-1}(V')\) is affine with ring finite over \(\mathcal{O}_{Y'}(V')\). As \(V'\) was arbitrary, \(f'\) is finite.

(c) The inclusion \(\subseteq\) is clear: if \(\mathfrak q\supseteq I\) then \(\rho^{-1}(\mathfrak q)\supseteq\rho^{-1}(I)\). Conversely let \(\mathfrak p\) be a prime of \(A\) containing \(J:=\rho^{-1}(I)\). The induced map \(\bar\rho:A/J\to B/I\) is injective and finite. Put \(R=A/J\) localized at \(\mathfrak p/J\), which is a local ring with maximal ideal \(\mathfrak m=\mathfrak pR\), and \(B'=S^{-1}(B/I)\) with \(S\) the image of \(A\setminus\mathfrak p\). Since localization is exact, \(R\to B'\) is injective, so \(B'\neq0\) (as \(R\neq0\)), and \(B'\) is a finite \(R\)-module. By Nakayama, \(\mathfrak mB'\neq B'\). Let \(\mathfrak n\) be a maximal ideal of \(B'\) containing \(\mathfrak mB'\). Then \(\mathfrak n\cap R\) is a proper ideal containing \(\mathfrak m\), so equals \(\mathfrak m\). Let \(\mathfrak q\) be the preimage of \(\mathfrak n\) in \(B\) (a prime containing \(I\)). Then \(\rho^{-1}(\mathfrak q)\) is the preimage of \(\mathfrak n\cap R=\mathfrak m\) in \(A\), namely \(\mathfrak p\). So \(\mathfrak p\in(\operatorname{Spec}\rho)(V(I))\).

(d) Let \(f:X\to Y\) be finite. It is of finite type by (a). Separated: this is local on \(Y\) (Def. 3.2), so we may assume \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}A\) affine; then \(X\) is affine, and \(f\) is separated by Prop. 3.4. Universally closed: finite morphisms are stable under base change by (b), so it is enough to show that every finite morphism \(f\) is closed. Being closed is local on \(Y\) (if \(F\subseteq X\) is closed, then \(f(F)\cap V=f(F\cap f^{-1}V)\) is closed in \(V\) for all \(V\) in an open covering of \(Y\), so \(f(F)\) is closed). So let \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}A\), \(X=\operatorname{Spec}B\), \(B\) finite over \(A\). A closed subset of \(X\) is \(V(I)\), and \(f(V(I))=V(\rho^{-1}(I))\) is closed by (c). Hence \(f\) is proper. ∎

Exercise 3.18

Problem

Let \(K\) be a number field. We know that the ring of integers \(\mathcal{O}_K\) of \(K\) is finite over \(\mathbb{Z}\). Show that if \(X\) is a separated connected scheme containing \(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_K\) as an open subscheme, then \(X=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_K\) (use Exercises 3.17(d) and 3.5).

Solution

Let \(U=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_K\) and \(j:U\to X\) the open immersion; we regard \(X\) as a scheme over \(S=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}\) (“separated” means separated over \(\mathbb{Z}\)). The structural morphism \(U\to S\) is finite (the preimage of an affine open \(D(n)\subseteq S\) is \(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_K)_n\), finite over \(\mathbb{Z}_n\); or use Ex. 3.15 with the covering \(\{S\}\)), hence proper (Ex. 3.17(d)). We recall the argument of Ex. 3.5. The composite \(U\xrightarrow{j}X\to S\) is proper and \(X\to S\) is separated, so \(j\) is proper (Prop. 3.16(e)). In particular \(j\) is closed, so \(j(U)\) is open (an open immersion) and closed in \(X\). It is non-empty (\(\mathcal{O}_K\neq0\)), and \(X\) is connected, hence \(j(U)=X\). An open immersion which is surjective is an isomorphism. So \(U=X\), i.e. \(X=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_K\). ∎

Exercise 3.19

Problem

Let \(X\) be a proper algebraic variety over a field \(k\). Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of \(k\)-schemes with \(Y\) affine. Show that \(f(X)\) is a finite set of closed points (hint: \(f\) factors into \(X\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_X(X)\to Y\)).

Solution

Write \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}C\), where \(C\) is a \(k\)-algebra via the structure morphism. As sets \(f(X)=f(i(X_{\mathrm{red}}))\) where \(i:X_{\mathrm{red}}\to X\) is the canonical closed immersion, which is a homeomorphism (Prop. 2.4.2 and its proof). The reduced scheme \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\) is proper over \(k\) (Prop. 3.16(a),(b)). We may therefore replace \(X\) by \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\) and \(f\) by \(f\circ i\), and assume \(X\) reduced. (We do this because Cor. 3.19 is available only for reduced varieties; for general \(X\) one can use Remark 3.20 instead.)

Put \(B=\mathcal{O}_X(X)\). By Cor. 3.19, \(B\) is a finite-dimensional \(k\)-vector space. Let \(\pi:X\to\operatorname{Spec}B\) be the canonical morphism (\(\rho(\pi)=\operatorname{id}_B\)) and \(\varphi=\rho(f):C\to B\) (Prop. 2.3.25). Since \(f\) is a morphism of \(k\)-schemes, \(\varphi\) is a \(k\)-algebra homomorphism (apply the naturality of \(\rho\) to the structure morphisms). Then \(\operatorname{Spec}(\varphi)\circ\pi\) and \(f\) both correspond to \(\varphi\) (for the first one: \(\rho(\operatorname{Spec}\varphi\circ\pi)=\rho(\pi)\circ\rho(\operatorname{Spec}\varphi)=\operatorname{id}_B\circ\varphi\)), so by injectivity of \(\rho\) (Prop. 2.3.25)

\[ f=\operatorname{Spec}(\varphi)\circ\pi. \]

Hence \(f(X)\subseteq\operatorname{Spec}(\varphi)(\operatorname{Spec}B)\).

Now \(\operatorname{Spec}B\) is a finite set: every prime \(\mathfrak q\) of \(B\) is maximal (\(B/\mathfrak q\) is a domain of finite dimension over \(k\), hence a field), distinct maximal ideals \(\mathfrak m_1,\dots,\mathfrak m_r\) are pairwise comaximal, and by the Chinese remainder theorem \(B\to\prod B/\mathfrak m_l\) is surjective, so \(r\le\dim_kB\). Therefore \(f(X)\) is finite. Finally, if \(\mathfrak q\in\operatorname{Spec}B\) and \(\mathfrak p=\varphi^{-1}(\mathfrak q)\), then \(C/\mathfrak p\) embeds as a \(k\)-subalgebra of the finite extension field \(B/\mathfrak q\) of \(k\). A domain of finite dimension over \(k\) is a field, so \(\mathfrak p\) is maximal, i.e. a closed point of \(Y\). Every point of \(f(X)\) is of this form. ∎

Exercise 3.20

Problem

(a) Show that Corollary 3.32 is true for quasi-projective morphisms (use Exercise 2.3(c)). (b) Show that a morphism of finite type from an affine scheme to an affine scheme is quasi-projective.

Solution

Recall (Def. 3.35) that \(h:X\to W\) is quasi-projective if it is the composite of an open immersion \(a:X\to Z\) of finite type and a projective morphism \(Z\to W\). Cor. 3.32 consists of the statements: (3.32a) closed immersions are quasi-projective; (3.32b) compositions of quasi-projective morphisms are quasi-projective; (3.32c) quasi-projective morphisms are stable under base change; (3.32d) if \(X\to S\) and \(Y\to S\) are quasi-projective, so is \(X\times_SY\to S\); (3.32e) if \(X\to Y\to Z\) has quasi-projective composite and \(Y\to Z\) is separated, then \(X\to Y\) is quasi-projective.

The proof of Cor. 3.32 for projective morphisms deduces (d) and (e) from (a)–(c) by Lemma 3.15 (note that (b) of that lemma has to be read, as in its proof and in Prop. 3.16(e), as: if \(g\circ f\) has \(P\) and \(g\) is separated, then \(f\) has \(P\)). Hence it is enough to prove (3.32a)–(3.32c) for quasi-projective morphisms: then Lemma 3.15(a),(b) gives (3.32d) and (3.32e), since the hypotheses (1)–(3) of the lemma are exactly (3.32a)–(3.32c).

A lemma on immersions (this is the content of Ex. 2.3 which we need). Recall that an immersion is an open immersion followed by a closed immersion.

Lemma R. Let \(Q\) be a scheme, \(\Omega\subseteq Q\) an open subscheme, \(\kappa:X\to\Omega\) a closed immersion and \(e=\omega\circ\kappa:X\to Q\), where \(\omega:\Omega\to Q\) is the inclusion. Assume that \(e\) is of finite type. Then \(e=j\circ g\) where \(j:\overline X\to Q\) is a closed immersion and \(g:X\to\overline X\) is an open immersion of finite type.

Proof. The morphism \(e\) is quasi-compact (it is of finite type). Let \(\overline{X}\) be its scheme-theoretic closure (Ex. 2.3.17): \(\overline X=V(\mathcal{J})\) with \(\mathcal{J}=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal{O}_Q\to e_*\mathcal{O}_X)\) is a scheme, \(j:\overline X\to Q\) is a closed immersion, and \(e=j\circ g\) for a morphism \(g:X\to\overline X\). For \(V\subseteq\Omega\) open, \(e_*\mathcal{O}_X(V)=\mathcal{O}_X(\kappa^{-1}V)\), so \(\mathcal{J}|_\Omega=\operatorname{Ker}(\kappa^\#)=:\mathcal{J}_\kappa\). Since \(\kappa\) is a closed immersion, \(\kappa\) identifies \(X\) with the closed subscheme \((V(\mathcal{J}_\kappa),\mathcal{O}_\Omega/\mathcal{J}_\kappa)\) of \(\Omega\) (Prop. 2.2.24). This closed subscheme of \(\Omega\) is the open subscheme \(\overline X\cap\Omega=j^{-1}(\Omega)\) of \(\overline X\). So \(\kappa\) factors as \(X\xrightarrow{\kappa'}j^{-1}(\Omega)\to\Omega\) with \(\kappa'\) an isomorphism. By Ex. 1.6(a) (a closed immersion is a monomorphism), \(g=\kappa'\) followed by the inclusion \(j^{-1}(\Omega)\subseteq\overline X\). Hence \(g\) is an open immersion.

It remains to see that \(g\) is quasi-compact; then \(g\) is of finite type by Prop. 2.4(e), since \(j\circ g=e\) is of finite type. Let \(Q_l\) be an affine open covering of \(Q\). The sets \(\overline X_l=j^{-1}(Q_l)\) are affine (Prop. 2.3.20) and cover \(\overline X\), and \(g^{-1}(\overline X_l)=e^{-1}(Q_l)\) is quasi-compact. Let \(V\subseteq\overline X\) be an affine open subset. As in the proof of Prop. 2.2, \(V\cap\overline X_l\) is a union of open sets which are principal in \(V\) and in \(\overline X_l\); by quasi-compactness of \(V\), \(V\) is a finite union of such sets \(W=D_{\overline X_l}(s)\). Then \(g^{-1}(W)\) is the principal open subset of the quasi-compact scheme \(T=g^{-1}(\overline X_l)\) defined by \(g^\#(s)\). Write \(T=T_1\cup\dots\cup T_m\) as a finite union of affine opens (Ex. 2.3.14). Then \(g^{-1}(W)\cap T_c=D_{T_c}(g^\#(s)|_{T_c})\) is affine, so \(g^{-1}(W)\) is a finite union of affine opens, hence quasi-compact. Therefore \(g^{-1}(V)\) is quasi-compact. \(\square\)

Part (a), step 1: closed immersions (3.32a). A closed immersion \(i:X\to Y\) is \(i\circ\operatorname{id}_X\), where \(\operatorname{id}_X\) is an open immersion of finite type and \(i\) is projective (Cor. 3.32(a)).

Part (a), step 2: base change (3.32c). Let \(h=b\circ a\) with \(a:X\to Z\) an open immersion of finite type and \(b:Z\to Y\) projective, and let \(Y'\to Y\) be a morphism. Put \(Z'=Z\times_YY'\) and \(X'=X\times_YY'=X\times_ZZ'\). The projection \(X'\to Z'\) is the base change of the open immersion \(a\), hence an open immersion (it is the inclusion of the open subscheme which is the preimage of \(a(X)\), Ex. 1.6(b)); it is of finite type by Prop. 2.4(c). The morphism \(Z'\to Y'\) is projective by Cor. 3.32(c). So \(X'\to Y'\) is quasi-projective.

Part (a), step 3: composition (3.32b). Let \(X\xrightarrow{a}Z\xrightarrow{b}Y\) and \(Y\xrightarrow{c}Z'\xrightarrow{d}W\) with \(a,c\) open immersions of finite type and \(b,d\) projective. We must show that \(d\circ c\circ b\circ a\) is quasi-projective. Write \(b=\pi\circ i\) with \(i:Z\to\mathbb{P}^n_Y\) a closed immersion and \(\pi:\mathbb{P}^n_Y\to Y\) the projection. Put \(Q=\mathbb{P}^n_{Z'}\) with projection \(\pi_Q:Q\to Z'\). Since \(\mathbb{P}^n_Y=\mathbb{P}^n_{Z'}\times_{Z'}Y\) (Prop. 1.4(b)), the canonical morphism \(c':\mathbb{P}^n_Y\to Q\) is the base change of \(c\), hence an open immersion, of finite type by Prop. 2.4(c), and \(\pi_Q\circ c'=c\circ\pi\). Define \(e=c'\circ i\circ a:X\to Q\). It is of finite type, as a composite of \(a\), of the closed immersion \(i\) (Prop. 2.4(a)) and of \(c'\) (Prop. 2.4(b)).

The set \(a(X)\) is open in \(Z\), and \(i\) is a homeomorphism onto a closed subset, so \(a(X)=i^{-1}(\Omega_0)\) for some open \(\Omega_0\subseteq\mathbb{P}^n_Y\). The restriction of \(i\) over \(\Omega_0\) is a closed immersion \(i^{-1}(\Omega_0)\to\Omega_0\), and \(i^{-1}(\Omega_0)\) is the open subscheme \(a(X)\simeq X\) of \(Z\). Thus \(i\circ a=(\text{inclusion of }\Omega_0)\circ\kappa\) with \(\kappa:X\to\Omega_0\) a closed immersion, and \(e=c'|_{\Omega_0}\circ\kappa\) where \(\Omega=c'(\Omega_0)\) is open in \(Q\) and \(c'|_{\Omega_0}:\Omega_0\to\Omega\) is an isomorphism. So Lemma R applies to \(e\) (with \(\Omega\) and the closed immersion \(c'|_{\Omega_0}\circ\kappa:X\to\Omega\)): \(e=j\circ g\), with \(j:\overline X\to Q\) a closed immersion and \(g:X\to\overline X\) an open immersion of finite type.

Now \(\pi_Q\circ j:\overline X\to Z'\) is projective by definition, so \(d\circ\pi_Q\circ j:\overline X\to W\) is projective (Cor. 3.32(b)). And

\[ d\circ c\circ b\circ a=d\circ c\circ\pi\circ i\circ a=d\circ\pi_Q\circ c'\circ i\circ a=d\circ\pi_Q\circ j\circ g. \]

So \(d\circ c\circ b\circ a\) is the open immersion \(g\) of finite type followed by a projective morphism, i.e. quasi-projective. This proves (3.32a)–(3.32c), and hence (3.32d), (3.32e) by Lemma 3.15 as explained. This completes part (a) of the exercise.

Part (b). Let \(X=\operatorname{Spec}B\to Y=\operatorname{Spec}A\) be of finite type. Then \(B=A[t_1,\dots,t_n]/I\) for some \(n\) (Def. 2.1 with \(U=X\)). The surjection \(A[T_1,\dots,T_n]\to B\) gives a closed immersion \(\kappa:X\to\mathbb{A}^n_A=\operatorname{Spec}A[T_1,\dots,T_n]=D_+(T_0)\subseteq Q:=\mathbb{P}^n_A\). Put \(e=\omega\circ\kappa:X\to Q\), with \(\omega\) the open immersion \(D_+(T_0)\subseteq Q\). We check that \(e\) is of finite type. The scheme \(Q\) is separated over \(A\) (Cor. 3.10), hence separated, since \(\operatorname{Spec}A\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}\) is separated (Prop. 3.4, 3.9(b)). So the intersection of \(D_+(T_0)\) with any affine open \(V\) of \(Q\) is affine (Prop. 3.6), and \(e^{-1}(V)=\kappa^{-1}(V\cap D_+(T_0))\) is affine (Prop. 2.3.20). Thus \(e\) is quasi-compact. The composite of \(e\) with the structure morphism \(Q\to Y\) is \(X\to Y\), which is of finite type, and \(Q\to Y\) is of finite type (Cor. 3.10). By Prop. 2.4(e), \(e\) is of finite type. By Lemma R, \(e=j\circ g\) with \(g:X\to\overline X\) an open immersion of finite type and \(j:\overline X\to Q\) a closed immersion. Then \(\overline X\to Q\to Y\) is projective and \(X\to Y\) is the composite of \(g\) and this projective morphism. So \(X\to Y\) is quasi-projective. ∎

Exercise 3.21

Problem

Let \(k\) be a field. Show that two closed subsets of dimension \(1\) in \(\mathbb{P}^2_k\) always have a non-empty intersection (use Exercise 3.4). Deduce from this that \(\mathbb{P}^1_k\times_k\mathbb{P}^1_k\not\simeq\mathbb{P}^2_k\).

Solution

Let \(S=k[T_0,T_1,T_2]\), so \(\mathbb{P}^2_k=\operatorname{Proj}S\), and let \(S_+\) be the irrelevant ideal. We prove directly the case \(n=2\), \(q=r=1\) of Ex. 3.4(c), without the diagonal argument.

Step 1: an irreducible closed subset \(X_0\) of dimension \(1\) of \(\mathbb{P}^2_k\) equals \(V_+(F)\) for a homogeneous prime element \(F\) of \(S\). Let \(P\) be the homogeneous prime corresponding to the generic point of \(X_0\); then \(X_0=V_+(P)\simeq\operatorname{Proj}(S/P)\) and \(P\not\supseteq S_+\). By Cor. 2.5.21, \(\dim\operatorname{Spec}(S/P)=\dim X_0+1=2\). As \(S\) is a finitely generated integral domain over \(k\) of dimension \(3\) (Cor. 2.5.17), Prop. 2.5.23(a) gives \(\operatorname{ht}P=3-2=1\). In particular \(P\neq0\). As \(P\) is homogeneous, it contains a non-zero homogeneous element, and a prime factor \(F\) of it (which is homogeneous, being a factor of a homogeneous polynomial; \(S\) is factorial) lies in \(P\). Then \(0\subsetneq(F)\subseteq P\) with \((F)\) prime, and \(\operatorname{ht}P=1\) forces \((F)=P\). So \(X_0=V_+(F)\) with \(\deg F\ge1\).

Step 2. Let \(X,Y\subseteq\mathbb{P}^2_k\) be closed subsets of dimension \(1\). The space \(\mathbb{P}^2_k\) is Noetherian, so \(X\) and \(Y\) have finitely many irreducible components, and each has an irreducible component \(X_0\) (resp. \(Y_0\)) of dimension \(1\). By Step 1, \(X_0=V_+(F)\) and \(Y_0=V_+(G)\) with \(F,G\) homogeneous of positive degree. The ideal \((F,G)\subseteq S_+\) is proper homogeneous, so it has a minimal prime \(\mathfrak Q\) over it. The prime \(\mathfrak Q\) is homogeneous: the ideal \(\mathfrak Q^h\) generated by the homogeneous elements of \(\mathfrak Q\) is prime by Lemma 2.3.35(a), and \((F,G)\subseteq\mathfrak Q^h\subseteq\mathfrak Q\) because \((F,G)\) is generated by homogeneous elements of \(\mathfrak Q\), so \(\mathfrak Q^h=\mathfrak Q\) by minimality. By Krull's theorem (Cor. 2.5.14(a)), \(\operatorname{ht}\mathfrak Q\le2\). If \(\mathfrak Q\supseteq S_+\), then \(\mathfrak Q=S_+\) (\(S/S_+=k\) is a field), but \(\operatorname{ht}S_+=3\) (the chain \(0\subset(T_0)\subset(T_0,T_1)\subset S_+\) of primes), a contradiction. So \(\mathfrak Q\in\operatorname{Proj}S\), and \(\mathfrak Q\in V_+(F)\cap V_+(G)=X_0\cap Y_0\subseteq X\cap Y\). Hence \(X\cap Y\neq\emptyset\).

Step 3: \(\mathbb{P}^1_k\times_k\mathbb{P}^1_k\not\simeq\mathbb{P}^2_k\). Let \(a,b\in\mathbb{P}^1_k=\operatorname{Proj}k[U_0,U_1]\) be the closed points given by the homogeneous primes \((U_1)\) and \((U_0)\); they are distinct and have residue field \(k\). Let \(p_1:Z=\mathbb{P}^1_k\times_k\mathbb{P}^1_k\to\mathbb{P}^1_k\) be the first projection, so \(Z=\mathbb{P}^1_{\mathbb{P}^1_k}\) over \(\mathbb{P}^1_k\). The sets \(C_a=p_1^{-1}(a)\) and \(C_b=p_1^{-1}(b)\) are closed in \(Z\) and disjoint. By Prop. 1.16 and Example 1.14, \(C_a\) is homeomorphic to the fibre \(Z_a=\mathbb{P}^1_{k(a)}=\mathbb{P}^1_k\), so \(\dim C_a=1\) (Example 2.5.28), and likewise \(\dim C_b=1\). If there were an isomorphism \(Z\simeq\mathbb{P}^2_k\), its image of \(C_a,C_b\) would be two disjoint closed subsets of dimension \(1\) of \(\mathbb{P}^2_k\), contradicting Step 2. ∎

Exercise 3.22

Problem

Let \(f:\operatorname{Spec}B\to\operatorname{Spec}A\) be a finite morphism. We want to show that \(f\) is projective. (a) Let us first suppose that \(B=A[T]/(P(T))\), where \(P(T)=\sum_{0\le i\le n}a_iT^i\) with \(a_n=1\). Let \(\tilde P(T,S)=\sum_{0\le i\le n}a_iT^iS^{n-i}\). Show that the natural morphism \(\operatorname{Spec}B\to\operatorname{Proj}A[T,S]/(\tilde P(T,S))\) is an isomorphism. (b) Conclude in the general case.

Solution

(a) We may assume \(n\ge1\) (if \(n=0\) then \(P=1\), \(B=0\), and both sides are empty). Let \(R=A[T,S]/(\tilde P)\), graded by \(\deg T=\deg S=1\) (\(\tilde P\) is homogeneous of degree \(n\)), and \(R_+=(T,S)R\).

\(D_+(S)=\operatorname{Proj}R\). Let \(\mathfrak P\in\operatorname{Proj}R\) be a homogeneous prime with \(S\in\mathfrak P\). In \(R\) we have \(0=\tilde P=T^n+\sum_{i<n}a_iT^iS^{n-i}\equiv T^n \pmod{S}\) (all other terms contain \(S\), and \(a_n=1\)). So \(T^n\in\mathfrak P\), hence \(T\in\mathfrak P\), so \(\mathfrak P\supseteq R_+\), which is excluded in \(\operatorname{Proj}R\). Thus \(\operatorname{Proj}R=D_+(S)\).

\(R_{(S)}\simeq B\). By Prop. 2.3.38, \(D_+(S)\simeq\operatorname{Spec}R_{(S)}\). Put \(t=T/S\). Then \(L:=A[T,S,S^{-1}]=A[t][S,S^{-1}]\), graded by powers of \(S\), with degree-\(0\) part \(A[t]\). The ideal \((\tilde P)L\) is generated by \(\tilde P/S^n=\sum a_it^i=P(t)\in A[t]\), a homogeneous element of degree \(0\). So \(R_S=L/P(t)L=(A[t]/(P(t)))[S,S^{-1}]\), whose degree-\(0\) part is \(R_{(S)}=A[t]/(P(t))\simeq A[T]/(P(T))=B\), with \(A\)-algebra structure the obvious one.

Therefore the natural morphism \(\operatorname{Spec}B\simeq\operatorname{Spec}R_{(S)}\simeq D_+(S)=\operatorname{Proj}R\) is an isomorphism. Moreover \(\operatorname{Proj}R=V_+(\tilde P)\) is a closed subscheme of \(\mathbb{P}^1_A=\operatorname{Proj}A[T,S]\) (Lemma 2.3.41). So \(\operatorname{Spec}B\to\operatorname{Spec}A\) factors as a closed immersion into \(\mathbb{P}^1_A\) followed by the projection: it is projective.

(b) Let \(B\) be finite over \(A\), generated as an \(A\)-module by \(b_1,\dots,b_m\); these generate \(B\) as \(A\)-algebra. Each \(b_l\) is integral over \(A\) (determinant trick), so there is a monic polynomial \(P_l\in A[T_l]\) of degree \(n_l\ge1\) with \(P_l(b_l)=0\) (multiply by \(T_l\) if necessary to have \(n_l\ge1\)). Put \(B_l=A[T_l]/(P_l)\) and \(B'=A[T_1,\dots,T_m]/(P_1(T_1),\dots,P_m(T_m))\simeq B_1\otimes_A\cdots\otimes_AB_m\). The \(A\)-algebra homomorphism \(B'\to B\), \(T_l\mapsto b_l\), is well-defined and surjective. Hence \(\operatorname{Spec}B\to\operatorname{Spec}B'\) is a closed immersion, and \(\operatorname{Spec}B'=\operatorname{Spec}B_1\times_{\operatorname{Spec}A}\cdots\times_{\operatorname{Spec}A}\operatorname{Spec}B_m\) (Prop. 1.2). By (a) each \(\operatorname{Spec}B_l\to\operatorname{Spec}A\) is projective, hence so is the fibre product \(\operatorname{Spec}B'\to\operatorname{Spec}A\) (Cor. 3.32(d)). Finally \(\operatorname{Spec}B\to\operatorname{Spec}B'\) is a closed immersion, hence projective, and the composite \(\operatorname{Spec}B\to\operatorname{Spec}A\) is projective by Cor. 3.32(b). ∎

Exercise 3.23

Problem

Let \(X\) be a quasi-projective scheme over a scheme \(S\), and let \(G\) be a finite group acting on the \(S\)-scheme \(X\). Show that the quotient scheme \(X/G\) exists (use Exercise 2.3.21), and that \(G\) acts transitively on the fibers of \(X\to X/G\). Moreover, the canonical morphism \(X\to X/G\) is a finite morphism if \(S\) is locally Noetherian.

Solution

Let \(\pi:X\to S\) be the structural morphism; “\(G\) acts on the \(S\)-scheme \(X\)” means that each \(\sigma\in G\) acts by an automorphism \(\varphi_\sigma\) of \(X\) with \(\pi\circ\varphi_\sigma=\pi\). We recall from Ex. 2.3.20 and Ex. 2.3.21 what we need. Let \(A\) be a ring and \(G\) a finite group acting on \(A\) by ring automorphisms.

(i) \(A\) is integral over \(A^G\): for \(a\in A\) the monic polynomial \(\prod_{\sigma\in G}(T-\sigma a)\) has coefficients in \(A^G\) and vanishes at \(a\). Consequently \(p:\operatorname{Spec}A\to\operatorname{Spec}A^G\) is surjective, and (incomparability for integral extensions) two primes \(\mathfrak q\subseteq\mathfrak q'\) of \(A\) with the same contraction to \(A^G\) are equal.

(ii) The fibres of \(p\) are the \(G\)-orbits (Ex. 2.3.20(a)). Indeed, \(p\circ\varphi_\sigma=p\) since \(\sigma\) fixes \(A^G\). Conversely let \(\mathfrak q_1\cap A^G=\mathfrak q_2\cap A^G=:P_0\). For \(b\in\mathfrak q_2\), the product \(\prod_\sigma\sigma(b)\) lies in \(\mathfrak q_2\cap A^G=P_0\subseteq\mathfrak q_1\), so some \(\sigma(b)\in\mathfrak q_1\). Thus \(\mathfrak q_2\subseteq\bigcup_{\sigma}\sigma^{-1}(\mathfrak q_1)\) and by prime avoidance \(\mathfrak q_2\subseteq\tau(\mathfrak q_1)\) for some \(\tau\in G\). Both primes contract to \(P_0\), so \(\mathfrak q_2=\tau(\mathfrak q_1)\) by (i).

(iii) (Ex. 2.3.21(c) and its proof) If every point of \(X\) has a \(G\)-stable affine open neighbourhood, then \(X/G\) exists. Concretely: \(q:X\to X/G\) is the map to the orbit space, \(X/G\) is covered by the open sets \(V_i=q(U_i)\simeq\operatorname{Spec}A_i^G\), where \(\{U_i=\operatorname{Spec}A_i\}\) is a covering of \(X\) by \(G\)-stable affine open sets, \(q^{-1}(V_i)=U_i\), and \(q|_{U_i}:U_i\to V_i\) is \(\operatorname{Spec}\) of the inclusion \(A_i^G\subseteq A_i\).

Existence. Let \(x\in X\) and \(s=\pi(x)\). Choose an affine open \(S_0=\operatorname{Spec}R\ni s\) and put \(X_0=\pi^{-1}(S_0)\). It is \(G\)-stable because \(\pi\varphi_\sigma=\pi\), and \(X_0\to S_0\) is quasi-projective: it is the base change of \(X\to S\) by \(S_0\to S\) (this is Cor. 3.32(c) for quasi-projective morphisms, proved in Ex. 3.20(a)). The orbit \(F=Gx\) is a finite subset of \(X_0\) (all its points lie over \(s\)). By Prop. 3.36(b), \(F\) is contained in an affine open subset \(U\) of \(X_0\). Put \(U'=\bigcap_{\sigma\in G}\varphi_\sigma(U)\). It is open, \(G\)-stable, and contains \(F\) (for \(\tau\in G\), \(\tau x\in\sigma(U)\) since \(\sigma^{-1}\tau x\in F\subseteq U\)). It is affine: \(X_0\) is quasi-projective over \(\operatorname{Spec}R\), hence separated over \(R\) (an open immersion is separated by Prop. 3.9(a), a projective morphism is separated by Cor. 3.10, and compositions are separated by Prop. 3.9(b)), hence separated (Prop. 3.4, 3.9(b)), so a finite intersection of affine opens of \(X_0\) is affine (Prop. 3.6(ii), by induction). Thus every point of \(X\) has a \(G\)-stable affine open neighbourhood, and \(q:X\to Y:=X/G\) exists by (iii). Since \(\pi\varphi_\sigma=\pi\), the universal property of \(q\) gives a morphism \(Y\to S\), so \(Y\) is an \(S\)-scheme.

Transitivity on fibres. Let \(y\in Y\), say \(y\in V_i\). Then \(q^{-1}(y)\subseteq q^{-1}(V_i)=U_i\) and \(q^{-1}(y)=p_i^{-1}(y)\) for \(p_i=q|_{U_i}\), which is a single \(G\)-orbit by (ii).

Finiteness. (We do not use that \(S\) is locally Noetherian: with Def. 3.35 the open immersion \(X\to Z\) is of finite type, which is what is needed.) The morphism \(X\to S\) is of finite type: it is the composite of an open immersion of finite type and a projective morphism, which is of finite type by Cor. 3.10; so Prop. 2.4(b) applies. Take \(U_i=\operatorname{Spec}A_i\) as in the existence part, so \(U_i\subseteq\pi^{-1}(S_0)\) with \(S_0=\operatorname{Spec}R\) affine. By definition of finite type, \(A_i\) is a finitely generated \(R\)-algebra, say generated by \(a_1,\dots,a_r\). Because \(\pi\varphi_\sigma=\pi\), the image of \(R\to A_i\) is contained in \(A_i^G\). Hence \(A_i=A_i^G[a_1,\dots,a_r]\), and every \(a_l\) is integral over \(A_i^G\) by (i), so \(A_i\) is a finite \(A_i^G\)-module. Thus the covering \(Y=\bigcup V_i\) satisfies: \(q^{-1}(V_i)=U_i\) is affine, with \(\mathcal{O}(U_i)=A_i\) finite over \(\mathcal{O}_Y(V_i)=A_i^G\). By Ex. 3.15, \(q\) is finite. ∎

Exercise 3.24

Problem

Let \(S\) be a locally Noetherian integral scheme with generic point \(\xi\). Let \(X\) be a scheme of finite type over \(S\). Show that if \(X_\xi\) is projective (resp. finite) over the function field \(K(S)=k(\xi)\), then there exists a non-empty open subset \(U\) of \(S\) such that \(X\times_SU\to U\) is projective (resp. finite). (Use Exercises 2.5 and 2.16.)

Solution

Reduction. Choose a non-empty affine open \(S_0=\operatorname{Spec}R\) of \(S\). Then \(R\) is a Noetherian domain, \(\xi\) is the generic point of \(S_0\), \(K=\operatorname{Frac}R=k(\xi)\), and \(X_\xi=\pi^{-1}(S_0)\times_{S_0}\operatorname{Spec}K\). An open subset of \(S_0\) is open in \(S\). So we may assume \(S=\operatorname{Spec}R\).

I will not use Ex. 2.16, but I prove below (Lemma Sp) the special case of Ex. 2.5 that I need (the isomorphism case at the generic point), since that exercise is cited in the hint.

Notation. Let \(R'\) be a Noetherian domain with fraction field \(K\), \(\pi:T\to\operatorname{Spec}R'\) an \(R'\)-scheme, \(0\neq h\in R'\). Put \(T_h=\pi^{-1}(D(h))=T\times_{R'}R'_h\) and \(T_K=T\times_{R'}K\). By Prop. 1.16 the projection \(\iota:T_K\to T\) is a homeomorphism onto \(\pi^{-1}(\xi)\), \(\xi\) the generic point of \(\operatorname{Spec}R'\). All schemes below are of finite type over \(R'\) (that is, finite unions of spectra of finitely generated \(R'\)-algebras; open subschemes of such schemes are again such, by Ex. 2.2). They are Noetherian (Hilbert's basis theorem, Ex. 2.1), so their topological spaces are Noetherian.

Lemma Z. Let \(T\) be of finite type over \(R'\) and \(F\subseteq T\) closed with \(F\cap\pi^{-1}(\xi)=\emptyset\). Then \(F\cap T_h=\emptyset\) for some \(h\neq0\).

Proof. Let \(F_1,\dots,F_r\) be the irreducible components of \(F\) with generic points \(z_1,\dots,z_r\). Then \(\pi(z_m)\neq\xi\), i.e. \(\pi(z_m)\) is a non-zero prime ideal \(P_m\) of \(R'\). Choose \(0\neq h_m\in P_m\) and put \(h=h_1\cdots h_r\neq0\). If \(z\in F\), then \(z\in F_m\) for some \(m\), so \(\pi(z)\) contains \(\pi(z_m)\) (since \(z\) is a specialization of \(z_m\)) and thus contains \(h_m\), hence \(h\). So \(\pi(z)\notin D(h)\), i.e. \(F\cap T_h=\emptyset\). \(\square\)

Lemma U (uniqueness). Let \(T\) and \(Y'\) be of finite type over \(R'\) and \(f,g:T\to Y'\) morphisms of \(R'\)-schemes with \(f\circ\iota=g\circ\iota\) (i.e. \(f_K=g_K\)). Then \(f|_{T_h}=g|_{T_h}\) for some \(h\neq0\).

Proof. Let \(\Sigma\) be the set of \(x\in T\) having an open neighbourhood \(N\) with \(f|_N=g|_N\); it is open, and \(f|_\Sigma=g|_\Sigma\). We show \(\pi^{-1}(\xi)\subseteq\Sigma\). Let \(x\in\pi^{-1}(\xi)\). Then \(y=f(x)=g(x)\). Choose an affine open \(V=\operatorname{Spec}C'\ni y\) of \(Y'\) with \(C'\) a finitely generated \(R'\)-algebra, and an affine open \(N=\operatorname{Spec}B'\ni x\) inside \(f^{-1}(V)\cap g^{-1}(V)\). The morphisms \(f|_N,g|_N:N\to V\) correspond to \(R'\)-algebra homomorphisms \(u,v:C'\to B'\). The assumption \(f_K=g_K\) says that \(u,v\) become equal after composition with \(B'\to B'\otimes_{R'}K\). Let \(c_1,\dots,c_p\) generate \(C'\) as \(R'\)-algebra. Then \(u(c_l)-v(c_l)\) is killed by some \(r_l\in R'\setminus0\). With \(r=\prod r_l\), \(u\) and \(v\) agree after inverting \(r\), i.e. \(f=g\) on \(N_r=N\cap T_r\), which is an open neighbourhood of \(x\) (as \(\pi(x)=\xi\in D(r)\)). So \(x\in\Sigma\). By Lemma Z applied to \(F=T\setminus\Sigma\) we get \(h\neq0\) with \(T_h\subseteq\Sigma\). \(\square\)

Lemma E (existence, affine case). Let \(T=\operatorname{Spec}B'\) and \(V=\operatorname{Spec}C'\) with \(B',C'\) finitely generated \(R'\)-algebras, and \(\psi:T_K\to V_K\) a morphism of \(K\)-schemes. Then there are \(h\neq0\) and a morphism of \(R'_h\)-schemes \(g:T_h\to V_h\) with \(g_K=\psi\).

Proof. Write \(C'=R'[y_1,\dots,y_p]/(g_1,\dots,g_q)\) (the ideal is finitely generated, \(R'[y]\) being Noetherian). The morphism \(\psi\) corresponds to a \(K\)-algebra homomorphism \(\psi^*:C'\otimes K\to B'\otimes K\). Write \(\psi^*(y_l)=b_l/r_l\) with \(b_l\in B'\), \(0\neq r_l\in R'\), and let \(r=\prod r_l\). Define an \(R'\)-algebra homomorphism \(\Phi:R'[y_1,\dots,y_p]\to B'_r\) by \(y_l\mapsto b_l/r_l\). Since \(\psi^*\) is well defined, \(\Phi(g_m)\) becomes \(0\) in \(B'\otimes K=(R'\setminus0)^{-1}B'_r\), so \(\Phi(g_m)\) is killed by some \(t_m\in R'\setminus0\). Let \(h=r\prod_mt_m\). Then \(\Phi\) induces an \(R'_h\)-algebra homomorphism \(C'_h\to B'_h\), i.e. a morphism \(g:T_h=\operatorname{Spec}B'_h\to V_h=\operatorname{Spec}C'_h\), and \(g_K=\psi\) since both correspond to homomorphisms agreeing on the generators \(y_l\). \(\square\)

Lemma Sp (spreading out an isomorphism). Let \(X,Y\) be \(R'\)-schemes of finite type and \(\varphi:X_K\to Y_K\) an isomorphism of \(K\)-schemes. Then there are \(h\neq0\) and an isomorphism of \(R'_h\)-schemes \(X_h\to Y_h\) whose generic fibre is \(\varphi\).

Proof. Cover \(Y\) by finitely many affine opens \(Y_a=\operatorname{Spec}C_a\) and \(X\) by finitely many affine opens \(X_i=\operatorname{Spec}B_i\) (finitely generated algebras). Fix \(i\). The open sets \(X_{iK}\cap\varphi^{-1}(Y_{aK})\) cover \(X_{iK}=\operatorname{Spec}(B_i\otimes K)\); each is a union of principal opens \(D(\beta)\), \(\beta\in B_i\otimes K\), and we may take \(\beta=b/1\) with \(b\in B_i\) (units of \(K\) are invertible). By quasi-compactness, finitely many \(D_{X_{iK}}(b_{il})\), \(l=1,\dots,L_i\), cover \(X_{iK}\), with \(\varphi(D(b_{il}))\subseteq Y_{a(i,l),K}\). Put \(X_{il}=D_{X_i}(b_{il})\), an affine open of \(X\) with \(X_{il,K}=D_{X_{iK}}(b_{il})\). The open set \(X^1=\bigcup_{i,l}X_{il}\) contains \(\pi^{-1}(\xi)\), so by Lemma Z there is \(h_1\neq0\) with \(X_{h_1}\subseteq X^1\). Replacing \(R'\) by \(R'_{h_1}\) and \(X\) by \(X_{h_1}\) (and \(Y\) by \(Y_{h_1}\)), we may assume \(X=\bigcup X_{il}\) with \(\varphi(X_{il,K})\subseteq Y_{a(i,l),K}\).

By Lemma E applied to \(\varphi|_{X_{il,K}}:X_{il,K}\to Y_{a(i,l),K}\) we get \(h_{il}\neq0\) and \(R'_{h_{il}}\)-morphisms \(g_{il}:(X_{il})_{h_{il}}\to Y_{a(i,l)}\subseteq Y\) with \((g_{il})_K=\varphi|\). For two indices, \(T=X_{il}\cap X_{i'l'}\) is of finite type over \(R'\) and \(g_{il}|_T,g_{i'l'}|_T\) have the same generic fibre \(\varphi|_{T_K}\); by Lemma U they agree on \(T_{h}\) for some \(h\neq0\). Let \(h'=\prod h_{il}\). Apply Lemma U to the restrictions of \(g_{il}\) and \(g_{i'l'}\) to \((X_{il}\cap X_{i'l'})_{h'}\), for each of the finitely many pairs, and let \(h_2\) be \(h'\) times the product of the elements \(h\) so obtained. Then all \(g_{il}\) are defined on \((X_{il})_{h_2}\) and agree on the overlaps. They glue to a morphism \(g:X_{h_2}\to Y\) of \(R'\)-schemes, which lands in \(Y_{h_2}\) and satisfies \(g_K=\varphi\).

By symmetry applied to \(\varphi^{-1}\), there are \(h_3\neq0\) and \(g':Y_{h_3}\to X_{h_3}\) with \(g'_K=\varphi^{-1}\). On \(X_{h_2h_3}\) the two morphisms \(g'\circ g\) and the inclusion \(X_{h_2h_3}\to X\) have generic fibre \(\varphi^{-1}\varphi=\operatorname{id}\), so by Lemma U they agree on \(X_{h_2h_3h_4}\) for some \(h_4\neq0\); similarly \(g\circ g'=\operatorname{id}\) on \(Y_{h_2h_3h_5}\). With \(h=h_2h_3h_4h_5\), \(g:X_h\to Y_h\) is an isomorphism with inverse \(g'|_{Y_h}\) and generic fibre \(\varphi\). \(\square\)

(This is the case \(s=\xi\), \(S\) affine, of Ex. 2.5, in the situation we need.)

The finite case. Suppose \(X_\xi\to\operatorname{Spec}K\) is finite. Taking \(V=\operatorname{Spec}K\) in the definition, \(X_\xi=\operatorname{Spec}C\) with \(C\) a finite \(K\)-algebra; let \(e_1,\dots,e_m\) be a \(K\)-basis of \(C\). There is \(0\neq d\in R\) such that \(1\) and all products \(e_ie_j\) lie in \(C_0:=\bigoplus_lR_de_l\) (clear denominators in the finitely many coefficients). Then \(C_0\) is an \(R_d\)-subalgebra of \(C\), free of rank \(m\) over \(R_d\), and \(C_0\otimes_{R_d}K=C\). Put \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}C_0\), a finite scheme over \(R'=R_d\) (a Noetherian domain with fraction field \(K\)), with \(Y_K=\operatorname{Spec}C=X_\xi\). Both \(X_d\) and \(Y\) are of finite type over \(R'\). By Lemma Sp there are \(h\neq0\) and an \(R_{dh}\)-isomorphism \(X_{dh}\simeq Y_{dh}=Y\times_{R_d}R_{dh}\), which is finite over \(R_{dh}\) by Ex. 3.17(b) (base change). Hence \(X\times_SU\to U\) is finite for \(U=D(dh)\), which is non-empty since \(dh\neq0\) in the domain \(R\).

The projective case. Suppose \(X_\xi\to\operatorname{Spec}K\) is projective, i.e. there is a closed immersion \(j:X_\xi\to\mathbb{P}^n_K\) over \(K\).

Lemma M. There is a closed subscheme \(Y\) of \(\mathbb{P}^n_R\) and a \(K\)-isomorphism \(Y_K\simeq X_\xi\).

Proof. Let \(\varepsilon:\mathbb{P}^n_K\to\mathbb{P}^n_R\) be the projection and \(e=\varepsilon\circ j\). Let \(Q_i=D_+(T_i)=\operatorname{Spec}A_i\), \(A_i=R[T_l/T_i]_l\). Then \(\varepsilon^{-1}(Q_i)=\operatorname{Spec}(A_i\otimes K)=:Q_{iK}\), and \(j^{-1}(Q_{iK})\simeq\operatorname{Spec}(A_i\otimes K/J_i)\) for the ideal \(J_i=\operatorname{Ker}(A_i\otimes K\to\mathcal{O}(j^{-1}Q_{iK}))\) (Prop. 2.3.20). The scheme \(X_\xi\) is Noetherian (closed in \(\mathbb{P}^n_K\)), so every morphism out of it, in particular \(e\), is quasi-compact. Also \(e^{-1}(Q_i)=j^{-1}(Q_{iK})\) is affine. Let \(Y=V(\mathcal{J})\subseteq\mathbb{P}^n_R\) be the scheme-theoretic closure of \(e\) (Ex. 2.3.17), a closed subscheme of \(\mathbb{P}^n_R\), with \(\mathcal{J}=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^n_R}\to e_*\mathcal{O}_{X_\xi})\). By Prop. 2.3.20, \(Y\cap Q_i=\operatorname{Spec}A_i/I_i\) with \(I_i=\mathcal{J}(Q_i)=\operatorname{Ker}(A_i\to\mathcal{O}(e^{-1}Q_i))=A_i\cap J_i\) (the preimage of \(J_i\) under \(A_i\to A_i\otimes K\)). We claim \(I_i(A_i\otimes K)=J_i\): the inclusion \(\subseteq\) is clear, and for \(x\in J_i\) choose \(0\neq r\in R\) with \(rx\in A_i\); then \(rx\in I_i\) and \(x=(rx)/r\). Hence the closed subscheme \((Y\cap Q_i)\times_RK=\operatorname{Spec}((A_i/I_i)\otimes K)=\operatorname{Spec}(A_i\otimes K/J_i)\) of \(Q_{iK}\) equals \(j(X_\xi)\cap Q_{iK}\). So the closed subschemes \(Y_K\) and \(j(X_\xi)\) of \(\mathbb{P}^n_K\) agree over each \(Q_{iK}\), i.e. they have the same ideal sheaf, and are therefore equal. This gives \(Y_K\simeq X_\xi\). \(\square\)

With \(Y\) as in Lemma M, \(Y\) is of finite type over \(R\) (closed in \(\mathbb{P}^n_R\), Cor. 3.10, Prop. 2.4). By Lemma Sp (with \(R'=R\)) there are \(h\neq0\) and an \(R_h\)-isomorphism \(X_h\simeq Y_h\). Now \(Y_h\) is a closed subscheme of \(\mathbb{P}^n_{R_h}\), so \(Y_h\to\operatorname{Spec}R_h\) is projective, and therefore so is \(X_h\to\operatorname{Spec}R_h\). Thus \(X\times_SU\to U\) is projective for \(U=D(h)\neq\emptyset\). ∎

Exercise 3.25

Problem

Let \(X,Y\) be schemes of finite type over a locally Noetherian scheme \(S\). Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a finite morphism of \(S\)-schemes. Let us fix a point \(s\in S\) such that \(X_s\neq\emptyset\). (a) Let us suppose that \(f_s:X_s\to Y_s\) is a closed immersion. Show that there exists an open neighborhood \(U\) of \(s\) such that \(f_U:X_U\to Y_U\) is a closed immersion. (b) Let us suppose that \(Y\) is reduced and that \(f\) is surjective. Show the analogue of assertion (a) for isomorphisms (compare to Exercise 2.5).

Solution

The statement is false as printed. Let \(S=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}\), \(s=(p)\), \(C=\mathbb{Z}[t]\), \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}C=\mathbb{A}^1_{\mathbb{Z}}\) and \(X=\operatorname{Spec}(C\times C/(1-pt))=Y\sqcup V(1-pt)\) with \(f\) the identity on \(Y\) and the closed immersion on \(V(1-pt)\simeq\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[1/p]\). Then \(X,Y\) are of finite type over \(S\), \(Y\) is reduced, \(f\) is finite and surjective, and \(X_s\neq\emptyset\). Since \(1-pt\equiv1\pmod p\), the fibre \(V(1-pt)_s\) is empty, so \(f_s\) is the identity of \(\mathbb{A}^1_{\mathbb{F}_p}\): it is an isomorphism. But for every open \(U\ni s\) of \(S\) there is a prime \(q\neq p\) in \(U\); choose \(c\in\mathbb{Z}\) with \(pc\equiv1\pmod q\). The closed point \(y=(q,t-c)\) of \(Y_U\) lies on \(V(1-pt)\) and has two preimages in \(X_U\), one in each component. So \(f_U\) is not injective, hence not a closed immersion (nor an isomorphism). The point is that the conclusion concerns a neighbourhood of \(s\) in \(S\), whereas the argument only gives a neighbourhood of the fibre \(Y_s\) in \(Y\); the passage from one to the other needs that \(Y\to S\) is a closed map (e.g. proper). The hypothesis \(X_s\neq\emptyset\) and the local Noetherianity of \(S\) are not needed below.

Corrected statement. Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a finite morphism of \(S\)-schemes, \(s\in S\). (a\('\)) If \(f_s\) is a closed immersion, there is an open subset \(W\subseteq Y\) containing the fibre \(Y_s\) such that \(f^{-1}(W)\to W\) is a closed immersion. If moreover \(Y\to S\) is a closed map (for example proper), then there is an open neighbourhood \(U\) of \(s\) such that \(f_U:X_U\to Y_U\) is a closed immersion. (b\('\)) If \(Y\) is reduced, \(f\) is surjective and \(f_s\) is an isomorphism, then in (a\('\)) the morphism \(f^{-1}(W)\to W\) is an isomorphism, and when \(Y\to S\) is closed, \(f_U\) is an isomorphism for a suitable open neighbourhood \(U\) of \(s\).

Proof of (a\('\)). Let \(S_0=\operatorname{Spec}R\ni s\) be an affine open, \(\mathfrak p\subset R\) the prime of \(s\), and let \(Y_j=\operatorname{Spec}C_j\) be affine opens covering \(\pi_Y^{-1}(S_0)\supseteq Y_s\). Since \(f\) is finite, \(f^{-1}(Y_j)=\operatorname{Spec}B_j\) with \(B_j\) a finite \(C_j\)-module. The fibre over \(s\) of \(f^{-1}(Y_j)\to Y_j\) is \(\operatorname{Spec}(B_j\otimes_Rk(s))\to\operatorname{Spec}(C_j\otimes_Rk(s))\). It is the restriction of the closed immersion \(f_s\) over an affine open set, so (Prop. 2.3.20) \(C_j\otimes_Rk(s)\to B_j\otimes_Rk(s)\) is surjective. Let \(Q_j=B_j/\operatorname{Im}(C_j)\), a finite \(C_j\)-module. By right exactness of \(\otimes\), \(Q_j\otimes_Rk(s)=0\).

Let \(y\in Y_j\) lie over \(s\), with prime \(\mathfrak q\subset C_j\). Then \(k(y)\) is a \(C_j\otimes_Rk(s)\)-algebra and \(Q_j\otimes_{C_j}k(y)=(Q_j\otimes_Rk(s))\otimes_{C_j\otimes k(s)}k(y)=0\). By Nakayama's lemma for the finite module \((Q_j)_{\mathfrak q}\) over the local ring \((C_j)_{\mathfrak q}\), \((Q_j)_{\mathfrak q}=0\). Since \(Q_j\) is finitely generated, there is \(c\in C_j\setminus\mathfrak q\) with \(cQ_j=0\). Let \(W_j\) be the union of all such open sets \(D(c)\subseteq Y_j\) (that is, \(W_j=Y_j\setminus\operatorname{Supp}Q_j\)); it contains all points of \(Y_j\) over \(s\). Over each such \(D(c)\) the map \((C_j)_c\to(B_j)_c\) is surjective (\((Q_j)_c=0\)), so \(f^{-1}(D(c))=\operatorname{Spec}(B_j)_c\to D(c)\) is a closed immersion (Lemma 2.3.17). Being a closed immersion is local on the target, so \(f^{-1}(W)\to W\) is a closed immersion for \(W=\bigcup_jW_j\), which contains \(Y_s\).

If \(\pi_Y:Y\to S\) is closed, then \(F=\pi_Y(Y\setminus W)\) is closed and does not contain \(s\). Let \(U=S\setminus F\). Then \(Y_U=\pi_Y^{-1}(U)\subseteq W\), and \(f_U\) is the restriction of the closed immersion \(f^{-1}(W)\to W\) over \(Y_U\), hence a closed immersion.

Proof of (b\('\)). If \(f_s\) is an isomorphism it is a closed immersion, so (a\('\)) applies. The closed immersion \(f^{-1}(W)\to W\) is surjective because \(f\) is. Over an affine open \(\operatorname{Spec}C\subseteq W\) it is \(\operatorname{Spec}C/J\to\operatorname{Spec}C\) with \(V(J)=\operatorname{Spec}C\), so \(J\) is contained in the nilradical, which is \(0\) as \(W\subseteq Y\) is reduced. Thus \(J=0\) and the closed immersion is an isomorphism. The second assertion follows as in (a\('\)). ∎

Exercise 3.26

Problem

We will study the example (kindly communicated by Florian Pop) of a valuation ring having `very many' prime ideals. Let \(k\) be a field. Let \(I\) be a totally ordered set. Let \(K=k(T_i)_{i\in I}\) be the field of rational fractions whose variables are indexed by \(I\). (a) Let \(\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}^{(I)}\) be the direct sum of copies of \(\mathbb{Z}\) indexed by \(I\). This is a group which is totally ordered by the lexicographical order. Let us denote the canonical basis of \(\mathbb{Z}^{(I)}\) by \(\{e_i\}_i\). Show that there exists a unique valuation (up to isomorphism) \(\nu:K^*\to\Gamma\) such that \(\nu(T_i)=e_i\) and \(\nu(k^*)=0\). (b) Let \(i\in I\). Show that the radical of the set \(\mathfrak{p}(i):=\{a\in K\mid\nu(a)\geq e_i\}\) is a prime ideal of \(\mathcal{O}_\nu\). Show that the map \(I\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_\nu\) defined in this way is injective and increasing, if we endow \(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_\nu\) with the order relation determined by the inclusion. (c) Let \(\theta:\mathbb{Z}^{(I)}\setminus\{0\}\to I\) be the map defined by \(\theta((n_i)_i)=\) the smallest \(i\) such that \(n_i\neq0\). Let \(\mathfrak{p}\in\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_\nu\). Show that if \(\theta(\nu(\mathfrak{p}))\) is bounded from above by an index \(i\), then for every \(j>i\), we have \(\mathfrak{p}\subseteq\mathfrak{p}(j)\). Show that if \(\theta(\nu(\mathfrak{p}))\) is not bounded, then \(\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}_\nu\).

Solution

The order on \(\Gamma\). For \(\gamma=(n_i)\neq0\) in \(\Gamma\) (finite support), \(\theta(\gamma)\) is the smallest index in the support. The lexicographic order is: \(\gamma>0\) iff \(n_{\theta(\gamma)}>0\); and \(\gamma>\delta\) iff \(\gamma-\delta>0\). Let \(\Gamma_+=\{\gamma\neq0:n_{\theta(\gamma)}>0\}\). Then \(\Gamma=\Gamma_+\sqcup\{0\}\sqcup(-\Gamma_+)\), and \(\Gamma_+\) is closed under addition: for \(\gamma,\gamma'\in\Gamma_+\) with \(\theta(\gamma)<\theta(\gamma')\) the sum has smallest index \(\theta(\gamma)\) with positive coefficient; if \(\theta(\gamma)=\theta(\gamma')\) the coefficient there is the sum of two positive integers. So \(\le\) is a total order compatible with addition. We use three comparison facts, with \(i\in I\): (F1) if \(\gamma>0\) and \(\theta(\gamma)<i\), then \(\gamma>e_i\) (the difference \(\gamma-e_i\) has smallest index \(\theta(\gamma)\) with positive coefficient); (F2) if \(\gamma>0\) and \(\theta(\gamma)=i\), then \(2\gamma>e_i\) (the coefficient of \(2\gamma-e_i\) at \(i\) is \(2n_i-1\ge1\) and lower indices vanish); (F3) if \(\gamma>0\) and \(\theta(\gamma)>i\), then \(m\gamma<e_i\) for all \(m\ge1\) (the coefficient of \(e_i-m\gamma\) at \(i\) is \(1\) and lower indices vanish).

(a) Existence. Every non-zero polynomial \(P=\sum_\alpha c_\alpha T^\alpha\in k[T_i]_{i\in I}\) is a finite sum over exponents \(\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{(I)}\subseteq\Gamma\). Set \(\nu(P)=\min\{\alpha:c_\alpha\neq0\}\in\Gamma\) (a minimum in the totally ordered group \(\Gamma\), over a finite set). If \(P,Q\neq0\), let \(\alpha_0,\beta_0\) be the minimal exponents of \(P,Q\). In \(PQ\) the monomial \(T^{\alpha_0+\beta_0}\) has coefficient \(c_{\alpha_0}d_{\beta_0}\neq0\) (all other products of monomials \(T^\alpha T^\beta\) with \(\alpha\ge\alpha_0\), \(\beta\ge\beta_0\), \((\alpha,\beta)\neq(\alpha_0,\beta_0)\) have exponent \(\alpha+\beta>\alpha_0+\beta_0\), so they do not contribute to it), and every monomial of \(PQ\) has exponent \(\ge\alpha_0+\beta_0\). Hence \(\nu(PQ)=\nu(P)+\nu(Q)\). Also all exponents occurring in \(P+Q\) occur in \(P\) or in \(Q\), so \(\nu(P+Q)\ge\min(\nu(P),\nu(Q))\). Now define \(\nu(P/Q)=\nu(P)-\nu(Q)\) on \(K^*\); this does not depend on the representation (\(P/Q=P'/Q'\) gives \(\nu(P)+\nu(Q')=\nu(P')+\nu(Q)\)), \(\nu\) is a homomorphism, and \(\nu(a+b)\ge\min(\nu(a),\nu(b))\) follows from the case of polynomials by writing \(a+b=(PQ'+P'Q)/(QQ')\). We have \(\nu(T_i)=e_i\) and \(\nu(c)=0\) for \(c\in k^*\) (exponent \(0\)). So \(\nu\) is a valuation as in Def. 3.22.

Uniqueness. Let \(\nu'\) be a valuation with \(\nu'(T_i)=e_i\) and \(\nu'(k^*)=0\). For a monomial, \(\nu'(cT^\alpha)=\alpha\). The monomials of a polynomial \(P\) have pairwise distinct exponents, hence pairwise distinct values of \(\nu'\). For a sum of elements with pairwise distinct valuations, \(\nu'\) equals the minimum (if \(\nu'(x)<\nu'(y)\) then \(\nu'(x+y)\ge\nu'(x)\) and \(\nu'(x)=\nu'(x+y-y)\ge\min(\nu'(x+y),\nu'(y))\) forces \(\nu'(x+y)=\nu'(x)\)). So \(\nu'(P)=\nu(P)\) for polynomials, hence \(\nu'=\nu\) on \(K^*\).

(b) Let \(\mathcal{O}_\nu=\{\nu\ge0\}\) and \(\mathfrak{m}_\nu=\{\nu>0\}\). Since \(e_i>0\), the set \(\mathfrak p(i)\) is contained in \(\mathfrak m_\nu\), and it is an ideal of \(\mathcal{O}_\nu\) (closed under sums since \(\nu(a+b)\ge\min\), and under multiplication by elements of \(\mathcal{O}_\nu\)). Let \(\mathfrak q(i)=\sqrt{\mathfrak p(i)}=\{a\in\mathcal{O}_\nu:a^m\in\mathfrak p(i)\text{ for some }m\ge1\}=\{a:m\,\nu(a)\ge e_i\text{ for some }m\ge1\}\). By (F1)–(F3), for \(a\neq0\) with \(\nu(a)=\gamma>0\):

\[ a\in\mathfrak q(i)\iff\theta(\gamma)\le i. \]

Indeed if \(\theta(\gamma)<i\) then \(\gamma>e_i\) by (F1); if \(\theta(\gamma)=i\), then \(2\gamma>e_i\) by (F2); if \(\theta(\gamma)>i\) then \(m\gamma<e_i\) for all \(m\) by (F3). (If \(\nu(a)=0\) then \(m\nu(a)=0<e_i\), so \(a\notin\mathfrak q(i)\).) Thus \(\mathfrak q(i)=\{0\}\cup\{a:\nu(a)>0,\ \theta(\nu(a))\le i\}\). It is a proper ideal (the radical of an ideal), and it is prime: let \(a,b\in\mathcal{O}_\nu\setminus\mathfrak q(i)\). Then \(\nu(a),\nu(b)\) are each \(0\) or have \(\theta>i\) (all indices in the support \(>i\)). So \(\nu(ab)=\nu(a)+\nu(b)\) is \(0\) or has its support within indices \(>i\), i.e. \(\theta(\nu(ab))>i\). So \(ab\notin\mathfrak q(i)\).

Injective and increasing. If \(i<j\), then \(\{\theta\le i\}\subseteq\{\theta\le j\}\) gives \(\mathfrak q(i)\subseteq\mathfrak q(j)\). Moreover \(T_j\in\mathfrak q(j)\setminus\mathfrak q(i)\), since \(\nu(T_j)=e_j\) has \(\theta=j>i\). So \(i<j\Rightarrow\mathfrak q(i)\subsetneq\mathfrak q(j)\), and the map \(i\mapsto\mathfrak q(i)\) is injective and strictly increasing.

(c) Let \(\mathfrak p\in\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_\nu\). As \(\mathcal{O}_\nu\) is local with maximal ideal \(\mathfrak m_\nu\), \(\mathfrak p\subseteq\mathfrak m_\nu\), so \(\nu(a)>0\) for \(0\neq a\in\mathfrak p\) and \(\theta(\nu(a))\) is defined. (In (c) the set \(\mathfrak p(j)\) may be taken to be the set itself or its radical \(\mathfrak q(j)\supseteq\mathfrak p(j)\); we prove the stronger inclusion in the set \(\mathfrak p(j)\).)

Bounded case. Suppose \(\theta(\nu(a))\le i\) for all \(0\neq a\in\mathfrak p\). Let \(j>i\) and \(0\neq a\in\mathfrak p\). Then \(\theta(\nu(a))\le i<j\), so \(\nu(a)>e_j\) by (F1), i.e. \(a\in\mathfrak p(j)\). Hence \(\mathfrak p\subseteq\mathfrak p(j)\subseteq\mathfrak q(j)\).

Unbounded case. Suppose \(\theta(\nu(\mathfrak p\setminus0))\) has no upper bound in \(I\). Let \(0\neq b\in\mathfrak m_\nu\), \(\beta=\nu(b)>0\), \(i_0=\theta(\beta)\). As \(\theta(\nu(\mathfrak p\setminus0))\) is not bounded above by \(i_0\) and \(I\) is totally ordered, there is \(a\in\mathfrak p\), \(a\ne 0\), with \(l:=\theta(\nu(a))>i_0\). Then \(\beta-\nu(a)\) has smallest index \(i_0\) (here \(\nu(a)\) vanishes at indices \(\le i_0\)) with coefficient \(n_{i_0}(\beta)>0\), so \(\nu(b)>\nu(a)\), i.e. \(b/a\in\mathcal{O}_\nu\). Then \(b=a\cdot(b/a)\in\mathfrak p\). Hence \(\mathfrak m_\nu\subseteq\mathfrak p\), and \(\mathfrak p=\mathfrak m_\nu\). ∎

Exercise 3.27

Problem

(A scheme without closed point) Let \(I\) be a totally ordered set without maximal element. Let \(\mathcal{O}_\nu\) be the valuation ring defined in the preceding exercise, with maximal ideal \(\mathfrak{m}_\nu\). Show that the scheme \(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_\nu\setminus\{\mathfrak{m}_\nu\}\) has no closed point.

Solution

Let \(U=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_\nu\setminus\{\mathfrak m_\nu\}\); it is an open subscheme since \(\{\mathfrak m_\nu\}\) is closed. If \(I=\emptyset\) then \(\mathcal{O}_\nu=k\) and \(U=\emptyset\), and there is nothing to prove; so let \(I\neq\emptyset\). Let \(\mathfrak p\in U\), so \(\mathfrak p\neq\mathfrak m_\nu\). We show that \(\{\mathfrak p\}\) is not closed in \(U\), by exhibiting a prime \(\mathfrak q\) with \(\mathfrak p\subsetneq\mathfrak q\subsetneq\mathfrak m_\nu\). Then \(\mathfrak q\in U\) and \(\mathfrak q\in V(\mathfrak p)\cap U\), which is the closure of \(\{\mathfrak p\}\) in \(U\), with \(\mathfrak q\neq\mathfrak p\).

By Ex. 3.26(c), since \(\mathfrak p\neq\mathfrak m_\nu\), the set \(\theta(\nu(\mathfrak p\setminus0))\) is bounded above by some \(i\in I\) (if \(\mathfrak p=0\) this holds trivially). As \(I\) has no maximal element, there are \(j,j'\in I\) with \(i<j<j'\). By Ex. 3.26(c), \(\mathfrak p\subseteq\mathfrak p(j)\subseteq\mathfrak q(j)\), where \(\mathfrak q(j)=\sqrt{\mathfrak p(j)}\) is the prime of Ex. 3.26(b). By Ex. 3.26(b), \(\mathfrak q(j)\subsetneq\mathfrak q(j')\). Finally, \(\mathfrak q(j')\neq\mathfrak m_\nu\): pick \(l>j'\) in \(I\); then \(T_l\in\mathfrak m_\nu\) (\(\nu(T_l)=e_l>0\)) and \(T_l\notin\mathfrak q(j')\) because \(\theta(e_l)=l>j'\). So

\[ \mathfrak p\subseteq\mathfrak q(j)\subsetneq\mathfrak q(j')\subsetneq\mathfrak m_\nu, \]

and \(\mathfrak q=\mathfrak q(j')\) satisfies \(\mathfrak p\subsetneq\mathfrak q\subsetneq\mathfrak m_\nu\). Hence no point of \(U\) is closed in \(U\). ∎
